# Introduction.

When the Jews were led into captivity, the ten tribes came to the higher regions of Asia—to Assyria, Media, and Mesopotamia on the river Kur <note>The Kura River</note> near the Caspian Sea; the two other tribes descended further down into a realm that was soon swallowed up by the northern peoples, until the new Persian monarchy rose from the very roots of these northern empires. Thus, in those regions at that time, there was a seed and a center of political influence that spread far and wide and lasted for ages. For the human spirit, it was likewise a most remarkable turning point. In Urmi, not long before, Zoroaster had appeared—the purported great purifier of Sabianism <note>An ancient religion often associated with star-worship</note> into the new religion of light of the Persians and Medes. He had first learned his wisdom from the Chaldean Magi; then, on Alborz in Georgia, he received the revelation of Ormuzd <note>Ahura Mazda, the creator deity in Zoroastrianism</note> and his living word, the *Zend-Avesta*. He brought this to Balkh, and the lands around the Araxes <note>The Aras River</note> and Kur adopted his religion. From Media, this spread through the hands of the conquerors to Babylon, and with the great Persian Empire it spread far and wide; their wisdom became what its mother—Chaldean wisdom—had been before: the ruler of the regions of Asia from the Araxes down to the Nile.

It was precisely during these times of first blossoming and initial striving that God decreed the exile of the Jewish people. They came to Babylon, into the remnants of that Chaldean wisdom from which the former had drawn, and through Daniel, they gained access to its innermost depths. Daniel’s prestige lasted until the reign of Darius of Media, and even under Cyrus: he was himself the *Archimagus* <note>original: "Archimagus"; meaning the Chief Magus or head of the wise men</note>, and his book is partly Chaldean in language, but entirely Chaldean in its imagery. The visions that Ezekiel saw at the River Chebar are consistent with those regions. The visions of Zechariah are Chaldean. It is well known that the Jews returned completely immersed in this dialect and this way of thinking. Their Hebrew

# Introduction.

When the Jews were led into captivity, the ten tribes came into the upper regions of Asia—to Assyria, Media, and Mesopotamia along the river Kur <note>original: "Kur", likely the Kura River</note> near the Caspian Sea. The two other tribes went further down into a kingdom that was soon swallowed up by the northern nations, until the new Persian monarchy rose from the roots of these very same northern empires. At that time, these regions were a germ and center of political influence that spread far and wide and lasted for a long time. For the human spirit, it was a similarly remarkable turning point. In Urmi, not long before, Zoroaster had arisen, the alleged great purifier of Sabianism <note>Sabianism: An ancient religious tradition involving the veneration of stars and celestial bodies.</note> into the new religion of light practiced by the Persians and Medes. From the Chaldean Magi he had first learned their wisdom; then, on Mount Albordj in Georgia, he received the revelation of Ormuzd <note>Ormuzd: Also known as Ahura Mazda, the supreme god of light in Zoroastrianism.</note> and his living word, the Zend-Avesta <note>Zend-Avesta: The primary collection of sacred texts of the Zoroastrian faith.</note>. He brought this to Balkh, and the lands around the Araxes and Kur (Cyrus, Oxus) adopted his religion. From Media, this faith spread through the hands of the conquerors to Babylon, and then far and wide with the great Persian Empire. Its wisdom became what its mother—Chaldean wisdom—had been before: the ruler of the Asian regions from the Araxes down to the Nile.

It was precisely during this time of the first flowering, of the first striving forward, that God decreed the abduction of the Jewish people. They came to Babylon into the remnants of that Chaldean wisdom from which the other had drawn its source, and through Daniel, they gained access to its innermost core. Daniel's prestige lasted until the reign of Darius the Mede, and even under Cyrus. He was himself an Archimagus <note>Archimagus: A chief priest or head of the Magi, the scholarly and priestly class of ancient Persia.</note>, and his book is partly in the language, but entirely in the imagery, of the Chaldeans. The images Ezekiel saw at the river Chebar are consistent with those regions. The visions of Zechariah are Chaldean. It is well known that the Jews returned [to their homeland] completely immersed in this dialect and this way of thinking. Their Hebrew

<summary>This introduction frames the Jewish exile within the broader historical and spiritual rise of the Persian Empire and Zoroastrianism, suggesting that Jewish thought was deeply reshaped by Chaldean and Persian wisdom.</summary>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion of the Jewish exile in Babylon. The catchword "sches" from the previous page likely completes the word "Hebräisches" (Hebrew), referring to the Hebrew spirit mentioned in the opening line.</meta>

[Hebrew] spirit and their spirit of Moses was gone; the eyes with which they now viewed his books and the hands with which they now touched his temple vessels were Chaldean eyes, Chaldean hands. Their hopes for the future, their new spirit of interpretation and exegesis—Pharisaism, which they derived with so much pomp from Sinai—had its more immediate Sinai in Chaldea.

If we therefore had writings from Chaldea, such as the books of the Sabians <note>Sabians: An ancient religious group often associated with star-worship; Enlightenment scholars frequently sought their texts to understand the roots of Middle Eastern religion.</note>—even if only in fragments, authentic and understood in Europe—we would have through them internal insight into Chaldean wisdom (whatever it may have been!), and thus the source of the newer Jewish way of thinking. Or, since we do not have these, and perhaps will not for a long time: if we had their offspring and innovator, Zoroaster—the religion and wisdom of the Medes and Persians, which flourished so greatly back then—we would have much [clarity] regarding the writings of the final prophets, the opinions of the Pharisees and Essenes <note>Essenes: A Jewish sect known for communal living and asceticism, often thought by 18th-century scholars to have been influenced by Eastern mysticism.</note>, and perhaps much else! —

->*&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*<-

The Persian Empire expanded as far as Egypt and the Greek colonies. Ancient tradition says that Pythagoras and his teacher had already drawn from Asian sources. Of the former [Pythagoras], it is claimed that he tasted of Egypt and Chaldea, and even—if chronology did not leave everything in the dark—that he heard Zoroaster or his students himself. Enough; the Oriental <note>original: "Morgenländischen"; a term used by the author to describe the "East" or "Land of the Morning."</note> seeds in the oldest philosophy of the Greeks are obscure, like everything ancient and like this entire philosophy itself; but a time was to come when the blending of ideas from both sides would become a world-altering phenomenon. Alexander [the Great] pushed into the heart of Persia, and as must always be the case, he succumbed to the "shackles" of those he had conquered <note>The author suggests that while Alexander conquered Persia militarily, the Persian culture and philosophy "conquered" the Greeks intellectually.</note>. Greek kingdoms were established in Asia. But since the Asians, who cling to everything that is antiquity, could not or would not suddenly change their way of thinking for the sake of these Greek "children" and newcomers, there was (the only path that united both) a mixture. The Greek language became the vessel or garment for Asian ideas: Asian angels became Greek gods, demigods, heroes, and finally Aeons <note>Aeons: In Neoplatonism and Gnosticism, these are divine emanations or powers that bridge the gap between the absolute God and the physical world.</note>; the wisdom of the Chaldeans and Persians entered into the forms of Hellenism, Neoplatonic philosophy, and many other sects.

<summary>This page argues that Jewish thought (specifically Pharisaism) and later Greek philosophy were profoundly reshaped by Chaldean and Persian influences, specifically the teachings of Zoroaster.</summary>
<keywords>Chaldea, Pharisaism, Zoroaster, Essenes, Pythagoras, Alexander the Great, Hellenism, Neoplatonism, Aeons, cultural syncretism</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion on the origins of ancient wisdom, moving from Pythagoras and Egypt to the influence of Gnostic thought and the multicultural world of early Christianity.</meta>

...and finally of Gnosis <term>Gnosis: A mystical or esoteric knowledge often associated with early Christian sects</term>. Egypt became, for ten internal and external reasons, the land most defined by mixture.

If only we had access to those wells or puddles in Chaldea and among the Median mountains! The Alexandrians and Essenes, the Seventy Interpreters <note>original: "siebzig Dollmetscher"; a reference to the Septuagint, the first Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible</note>, and the Apocrypha, the Gnostics at last, and a field of names, sects, and fantasies—perhaps the entire "hidden quality" <note>original: "qualitas occulta"</note> of that overused word "Hellenism" <term>Hellenism: The spread of Greek culture and language across the Middle East following Alexander the Great's conquests</term> would thereby find its invisible vital spark <note>original: "Archäus"; a term from Paracelsian medicine for a life-force</note>, its guiding spirit <note>original: "Spiritus rector"</note> that binds them all!

---
->* * *<-

Christ appeared. At a time when the Romans had completed what the Greeks could not do: the wall between national ways of thinking lay broken down. It was a shifting sea of mixed ideas and languages. Throughout these regions, Syro-Chaldean culture and the Greek language existed simultaneously. Judea was linked with the "God-fearing" <note>original: "σεβομενοις" (sebomenois); a term for non-Jewish people who were attracted to Jewish monotheism</note>, and with the scattered Greeks from Egypt to the Parthians, Medes, Elamites, and Mesopotamians. When Christ spoke from the people to the people, how could he speak in any other way than in the common language, a mixture of Pharisaic and Greek ideas? If he spoke from and in Galilee—heathen, populous Galilee—how could he speak anything but "Galileism" <term>Galileism: The specific dialect and cultural outlook of the Galilee region, which was often viewed by Judeans as linguistically distinct or less pure</term>? Matthew, who seems to have recorded Jesus's words with true common simplicity, must therefore use Chaldean styles of expression; Luke, when presenting them for Hellenists <note>Hellenists: Greek-speaking Jews or people influenced by Greek culture</note>, must use Greek styles. Even during Jesus's lifetime, John provides hints that the Gospel was intended for the scattered Greeks. When they come to see Jesus shortly before the end, he feels himself explained in them (an otherwise much-tortured passage! \*) and how abundantly was this fulfilled right on the feast of Pentecost. Parthians, Medes, Elamites, Mesopotamians, and so forth, were the first to feel the voice of the Spirit. (It was presumably the audience of high Jerusalem that felt the effects of the "sweet wine" <note>A reference to Acts 2:13, where onlookers accused the Apostles of being drunk when they spoke in tongues</note>!) Those who debated with Stephen, and could not [withstand] his spirit—

---

\*) John 12:20–21. See the remarkable preface by Semmler <note>Johann Salomo Semmler: A prominent 18th-century German theologian and pioneer of the historical-critical study of the Bible</note> to Baumgarten’s work on the Hebrews.

<summary>This page describes the Mediterranean world at the time of Christ as a "shifting sea" of blended cultures and languages, arguing that the New Testament must be understood through this multicultural lens. It highlights how the different backgrounds of the Gospel writers—such as Matthew's "Chaldean" style and Luke's "Greek" style—reflect the diverse audience of the early Church.</summary>

<keywords>Hellenism, Gnosis, cultural syncretism, Galilee, Pentecost, Matthew, Luke, John, Semmler, historical-critical method</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the New Testament must be understood through the lens of its specific cultural and linguistic context—Hellenism—rather than through classical Greek or ancient Hebrew alone. It concludes by introducing the figure of Anquetil-Duperron as an example of a scholar seeking original linguistic sources.</meta>

...were able to resist, were Hellenists <term>Hellenists: Greek-speaking Jews living outside of Judea who adopted Greek culture</term> of scattered lands. Since Paul and his brothers were cast out by the Jews, they turned to the scattered Jewish associates and the Gentiles.

The New Testament <note>original: "N. T."</note> could therefore no more have been written in Athenian Greek than Jesus and the Apostles lived in or wrote for Athens. It consists of monuments of their time, where each clearly demonstrates its origin; even when one Paul writes to Christians in Asia or Europe, his tone is not the same. He "Asiaticizes" (or as some have even said, "Gnosticizes" <note>To adopt the mystical or esoteric language of Gnosis</note>) as obviously in his letters to Ephesus and Colossae as he does not do, for example <note>original: "z. E." (zum Exempel)</note>, with Rome and Corinth. The Letter to the scattered Hebrews is a living monument to their way of thinking. So too the letters of Peter and James. Finally, the writings of John—who could be so dull-witted as not to have noticed in them the refined, simple, very select tone and linguistic character, maintained with extraordinary consistency down to the placement of every word and image? — If only we had the general, more specific characteristic source for all of this! Then, with the aforementioned outlets—Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher</note> and the entirety of Hellenism—as guarantors and witnesses, what a treasure it would be! We would not have to run to a thousand-year-old language of the Hebrew Old Testament <note>original: "A. T." (Altes Testament)</note> for every word that is not Greek. That is as much as if we, in the foreign analogies of our own century, were to take refuge in Ulfilas <term>Ulfilas: A 4th-century Goth who translated the Bible into the Gothic language</term> and Otfrid <term>Otfrid: A 9th-century monk known for the first extensive work in Old High German</term>, and thought ourselves wonderfully great for having learnedly found "Alemannisms" and "Gothisms" here, and "Hebraisms" there in the New Testament, which no one else finds.

---
->*      *      *<-

There was a man in our century who was awakened, like migratory birds, to seek a new language. In the library of the Great King <note>Referring to the King of France and the Royal Library (Bibliothèque du Roi)</note>, he saw unknown characters and books of which it was said that no one in Europe understood them. The youth made the resolution to learn them himself at the place of their origin. He left his people and went to sea as a soldier, until the kindness of his king eased his path. A quarto volume <note>original: "Quartant"</note> is the "novel of misery" of his journey: he did not find what he sought—the language of the Brahmins <term>Brahmins: The priestly caste of India</term>—and finally found what he was not looking for: the philosophy, religion, and language of the ancient Parsis <term>Parsis: Followers of Zoroastrianism who migrated to India</term> in their quiet dwellings at Gujarat <note>original: "Gusurate"</note>.

<summary>This page argues that the New Testament reflects the diverse regional "Hellenistic" dialects of its authors rather than classical Athenian Greek or ancient Hebrew. It introduces the explorer Anquetil-Duperron as a parallel for modern scholars seeking to recover lost linguistic and religious origins.</summary>
<keywords>New Testament, Hellenism, Paul the Apostle, Gospel of John, Philo, Hebraisms, Ulfilas, Otfrid, Anquetil-Duperron, Parsis, Zoroastrianism, Gujarat</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of finding original source materials for ancient religious texts. Having established the need for a specific cultural context for the New Testament, the author now introduces the parallel example of Abraham-Hyacinthe Anquetil-Duperron’s discovery of the Zoroastrian scriptures, the Zend-Avesta.</meta>

->* ( ) *<-

Over a span of many years, during which he was forgotten in Europe, he reached his goal through the avarice of the Darabs <note>The Dasturs, or Zoroastrian priests, specifically Darab Kumana, who taught Anquetil du Perron</note>, through patience and labor. He arrived in England after an equally adventurous return journey and found that neither Hyde <note>Thomas Hyde (1636–1703), an English scholar who wrote a history of the Persian religion but could not read the original Zend language</note> nor his successors—the keepers of the Zend manuscripts—understood a single word of them. Finally, with a sense of triumph that few will be able to truly share, he delivered eighteen books of the Parsi religion into his King's library and to the world:

->**Zend-Avesta, Work of Zoroaster,**<-

Containing the Theological, Physical, and Moral Ideas of this Legislator; the Ceremonies of Religious Worship which he established, etc. Translated into French from the Zend Original with Remarks, etc. <note>original: "Zend-Avesta, Ouvrage de Zoroastre, Contenant les Idees Theologiques, Physiques et Morales de ce Legislateur, les Ceremonies du Culte Religieux, qu' il a etabli &c. Traduit en François sur l'Original Zend avec des Remarques &c."</note>

By Mr. Anquetil du Perron, Paris 1774. Three thick quarto volumes. This is the discovery. What is it? What can be made of it?

Whether these are the actual writings of Zoroaster can remain a matter of indifference to us for now, as much as whether he wrote them with his own hand. It is enough that it is the prevailing liturgy of the Parsis <note>Followers of the Zoroastrian faith in India</note> in their temples: the books contain nothing but ceremonies and prayers. They are therefore, whatever else they may be, the Zend-Avesta—that is, the living word <term>Zend-Avesta: Literally meaning "Commentary and Law," interpreted here as the "living word" or liturgy</term> of their divine service; and how much does that alone signify!

1. They are written in the Zend language (the most prominent and first among them, that is), or rather, the ancient Georgian and Median Zend language is preserved only in this living holy word, the Zend-Avesta. In no other language can the prayers be spoken or the divine service administered: the sanctity, the hearing of prayers, and their efficacy are bound to the language itself. It is noted with Masoretic precision <note>Referring to the Masoretes, the Jewish scholars who meticulously preserved the Hebrew biblical text with vowel points and accents</note>: which prayer exists in Zend, or in Pahlavi <note>original: "Pehlvi," the Middle Persian language</note>, the younger dialect of this language? How the following generation of the Parsi language explains this or that obscure word? Where, when, and how—whether loudly or softly, standing or sitting—each must be spoken by the Djouti <note>The lead priest (Zoti)</note> or Raspi <note>The assistant priest (Rathwi)</note>, and with which gestures—for example, the handling of the sacred belt (Kosti) <note>The Kushti, a sacred girdle worn by Zoroastrians as a symbol of their faith</note>, their greatest holy object? The Indian and Sanskrit translations and the like. Even if I do not immediately derive from this the least...

<summary>This page describes Anquetil-Duperron's arduous journey to recover and translate the Zend-Avesta, the sacred liturgy of the Zoroastrians. The author emphasizes the importance of these texts as the "living word" of a religion, preserved with extreme linguistic and ritual precision.</summary>

<keywords>Anquetil du Perron, Zoroaster, Zend-Avesta, Parsis, liturgy, Zendsprache, Pahlavi, ritual gestures, religious translation, philology</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of the authenticity of the Zoroastrian scriptures brought to Europe by Anquetil-Duperron, shifting from the person of the translator to the historical and linguistic weight of the texts themselves. The catchword "brought" (gebracht) from the previous page's end links to the discussion of the Zend language's transmission.</meta>

...brought, cannot and will not draw conclusions regarding the faithful translation, internal clarity, or even the critical accuracy of the ancient <term>Zend language</term> <note>The language of the Avesta, the primary collection of religious texts of Zoroastrianism</note>. Nevertheless, unless one wishes to argue like *Hardouin* <note>Jean Hardouin (1646–1729) was a Jesuit scholar notorious for claiming that most ancient classical texts were forgeries by medieval monks</note> and shout "lies!" to the world's face, the structure of these liturgies, opinions, and prayers is ancient and certain. Already in the time of *Artaxerxes Longimanus* <note>Artaxerxes I (reigned 465–424 BC), a King of the Persian Achaemenid Empire</note>, this language was ancient and used by the court; its first descendants, the <term>Pahlavi</term> and <term>Parsi</term> languages, have long since passed away, succumbing to the mixing of peoples—Arabs, Turks, Indians, and Mongols. Either all the memoirs that *Anquetil* provided, along with his samples, are fabricated—and such a consistent deception is impossible, or at least has never occurred since the world began (for what reason would one have to burden an honest man with such a thing without any evidence?)—or the structure of this liturgy, as the Zend-Avesta, as the living word, is ancient, dating from the times of the ancient Median and Parsi kings before the Persian Monarchy.

2. From all of history, the zeal, legal strictness, and rigor of the Parsi priests to preserve their Word and Light is well known. Since *Alexander* <note>Alexander the Great, whose conquest of the Persian Empire in 330 BC led to the destruction of many Zoroastrian religious centers</note> destroyed the flourishing state of their empire, one fate after another has befallen them, until finally <term>Muhammadanism</term> <note>Islam; the text refers to the early Islamic conquests of Persia in the 7th century</note> in its initial fiery zeal almost completely rooted them out, forcing them toward India and leaving them barely a corner in Kirman by the Caspian Sea where they might celebrate their holy birthplace. Both branches continue to endure as the remaining shoots of a once widespread holy trunk: those in Gujarat in India, where their temple is located, still regard the Median mountains as their fatherland, and the holy Albordj <note>Mount Alborz; in the 18th century, European scholars often identified this mythical/spiritual mountain with the Caucasus range near Georgia</note> in Georgia as the place where their tree of immortality grows, the symbol of which they still fetch from those regions for their divine service. The entire history and character of this sect, which is built upon the living word, is therefore a confirmation. And how much more would it be confirmed if one of the Russian learned embassies, who come so close to Kirman or travel through it, would take the trouble to seek internal news of the mother church of this religion. If an *Anquetil* were there, how many gaps would be explained! How much more originally everything would be proven at its place of origin! — And what would be easier, since the possessor of these languages, who brought them to Europe,

<meta>This page continues the argument for the authenticity of the Zend-Avesta. brought, is still living, and is not secretive with his gift. How easily someone could learn from him, and how close **Kerman** <note>original: "Kirman"; a city in Iran that has historically been a major center for the Zoroastrian (Parsi) community</note> lies to the Russian Academy!

3. Everything that we previously knew regarding the Persian religion—though it was extremely fragile and imperfect—shows that here, we are at the very origin. If one looks up what **Hyde** <note>Thomas Hyde (1636–1703), an English orientalist who wrote early accounts of Persian religion</note>, **Hottinger** <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss philologist and theologian</note>, **Beausobre** <note>Isaac de Beausobre (1659–1738), a French Protestant scholar of Manichaeism</note>, and **Brucker** <note>Johann Jakob Brucker (1696–1770), author of the influential "Critical History of Philosophy"</note> collected from Greek and Arabic sources (or simply from one another), and then adds the reports of travel writers and reads **Anquetil** <note>Abraham-Hyacinthe Anquetil-Duperron, the first European to translate the Zend-Avesta</note>, it is as if mere fables were suddenly becoming the truth. And often, it is a truth of a completely different kind than what one imagined from the fables. Those earlier accounts are like castles in the air shaped of fire: as one draws closer, they turn into a humble mud hut of clay and brushwood, yet one that was perfectly suited to its creator and its era. 

In the **Zend-Avesta**, you see nothing but **Sabianism** <note>An ancient religious system often associated with the veneration of the stars and celestial bodies</note> purified into light. One person might call it an attempt of budding reason to conceive of the universe theologically, physically, and morally; another might call it an attempt to establish legislation with the help of philosophy and religion, grounding a system of **life** on earth upon ideas from heaven. Both are true. And however simple and decayed this "clay hut" may appear here, the spirit of antiquity hovers over it, and the moss of holy years rests upon it. One sees the genius of that ancient, mild, human age, when man was guided by "child- and angel-ideas" instead of the prison, the gallows, and the breaking wheel. In those times, legislators utilized these ideas—even for the smallest purposes—to encourage a common good deed, to maintain purity, vigilance, peace, love, and order, and to recognize the good in all elements; they did not disdain the most spiritual of inspirations. 

Today, people laugh at this as if it were child's play; yet the child's play itself cries out: Truth! Truth! This, and only this, is what the Magian spirit <note>original: "Magisinus"; referring to the wisdom or system of the Magi, the Zoroastrian priests</note> could have been. Had a political philosopher of our own age been tasked to speak for Zoroaster, what oracles of "enlightenment" they would have become! Yet here, one finds nothing but prayers, consecrations, liturgies, and order—and within them lies everything that Zoroaster intended. He taught through the living word, not through systems.

# * ( 0 ) *

must, as he constantly cries: *He was not the light!* <note>original Latin/German allusion to John 1:8: "He was not that Light, but was sent to bear witness of that Light."</note> Jesus was the Son of God, and not John [the Baptist]. And since it is known that these people, from the beginning until this very day, have preferred to attach themselves to the **Sabian** and **Chaldean** sects, how could the Evangelist have written differently or more aptly than in the language of these sects? Behold! There exactly lay the fine, glorious design of his Gospel! Toward that end, as we shall see, his great prologue is directed! For this reason, he begins with the Baptism, where John is merely a servant, and that very testimony of Jesus rings out! For this reason, he divides everything, in the language of this sanctuary, into light and darkness, heaven and earth; he makes use of all the symbols of their mysteries—of the **living bread**, the **heavenly flesh**, the **water of immortality**—in order to show, through their grand imagery, that Jesus alone is the salvation of the world. Down to every miracle and every speech, in the selection, progression, and completion of all events, everything is brought into the most sublime light. His first letter bore the Latin inscription: *To the Parthians* <note>original: "ad Parthos"; a traditional but debated title for the First Epistle of John, suggesting it was sent to the Parthian Empire in the East</note>, which no one until now has known what to make of, until **Whiston** <note>William Whiston (1667–1752), an English theologian best known for his translation of Josephus</note> turned it into *virgins* <note>original: "$\pi\alpha\rho\vartheta\acute{\epsilon}\nu\omicron\varsigma$"; Whiston conjectured the title "ad Parthos" was a corruption of the Greek word for "to the virgins"</note>. The hint provided by this inscription now becomes clear: the letter is nothing less than what the hypothesis makes of it—**Anti-Gnostic**: it is truly written in the language of the Parthian sanctuary, just like the Book of Revelation throughout. If only I could one day show the writings of John in their pure, radiant image!

Furthermore, it becomes clear as day that our newest system of interpretation—in which we shroud the worst **Naturalism**, **Socinianism** <note>A rationalist Christian movement that denied the Trinity and the full divinity of Christ</note>, and **Epicureanism** <note>A philosophy often accused in this era of being overly materialistic or dismissing divine providence</note> in the washed-out phrases of the New Testament—is by no means grounded in the linguistic usage of the Orient, though it always boasts of being so. In pagan **Chaldea**, and in the teachings of the magical **Zoroaster**, all those phrases meant more and something different than they mean to you. In the mouth of all **Hellenism**, in the metaphysics of **Philo** and the **Alexandrians**, they mean more and have a more spiritual connection than you intend them to have. All the Orient, then—which used the words *Angel*, *Word*, *Kingdom of God*, *Satan*, *Redeemer*, *Salvation*, *Truth*, *Light*, and *Heaven*, even if in the poorest application—connected spiritual ideas with them; yet you, the fashionable philosophers, transform them into water and a continuous, inflated nonsense.

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>The catchword "writes" (schreibt) links to the final sentence, where the author transitions to explaining how he approached the Gospel of John. This page continues the critique of biblical philology that ignores the specific Near Eastern context of the New Testament.</meta>

# 13
## B 3

From Xenophon and the Arabs, Polybius and the Mongols, people have sought to explain the New Testament: but what use is all this explaining if one merely collects phrases and shows that this or that author used the same expression, yet with a meaning miles apart? Since his crucifixion, Jesus has never received more false scepters and purple robes on one hand, and more drinks of gall and crowns of thorns on the other, than from all the learned interpreters and explainers of the Bible. What filth have they scraped together! And conversely, into what watery thinness have they dissolved everything! One sect finds everything in every place; the other finds nothing in the entire New Testament. The first has, thank God, been pushed aside; the latter now reigns.

My purpose, therefore, was to provide explanations not merely of words, but of the sense: what the expression not only *could* mean in such a context, but what it *actually* meant. If it seems to you that I have here and there placed too much emphasis on a word: go over it and soften it! To provide preacher-labors <term>Preacher-ergasien: from the Greek 'ergasia', meaning work or business; here referring to tedious homiletic exercises</term> was not my purpose, nor is it to my taste: the "a little more" and "a little less" <note>original Italian: "poco piu und poco meno"</note> can only be determined by the context, which did not belong in my book. But the sense as a whole, within the context of the New Testament—that was my intention. In that regard, I could certainly have traced the similar way of speaking down the line from Chaldea <note>An ancient region in Mesopotamia, often associated by 18th-century scholars with the origins of mystical and esoteric language</note> through later Judaism, Philo, the Seventy <note>The Septuagint, the Greek translation of the Old Testament</note>, down to the Church Fathers: then I could have let the interpreters make a grand show, and so on. But why the infinite procession?

Lightfoot, Danz, Rhenferd, Schöttgen, Meuschen; Heinsius, Kapellus, Grotius; C. Kircher, Alberti, Vorstius, Tromm; Krebs, Carpzov, Kypken <note>The author lists a "who's who" of 17th and 18th-century biblical scholars, Hebraists, and philologists</note> are all available: why compile them? Lovers of the subject and experts will, where they doubt, look them up for themselves and—find them. I do not write for aesthetic chatterers and diluters; they have already taken sides. However, I heartily wish that from all these sources we might soon see a complete dictionary of the New Testament.

Therefore, I also did not want to explain books from top to bottom; the healthiest mind must perish in doing so, especially when drawing from a one-sided source. I went, for example, to the Evangelist **John**, who after all [writes] entirely in this language...

<summary>The author critiques superficial biblical scholarship that relies on unrelated Greek or secular texts, arguing instead for a contextual understanding of the New Testament's "sense" rooted in Near Eastern traditions. He dismisses the need to re-compile existing scholarly commentaries, calling instead for a new, comprehensive dictionary of the New Testament's unique vocabulary.</summary>
<keywords>biblical interpretation, philology, New Testament, Gospel of John, Chaldea, Septuagint, scholarship, linguistics</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's reflection on his methodology, specifically the tension between dissecting the historical origins of biblical language and preserving its spiritual vitality. He transitions from a critique of scholars to a defense of his own "imperfect" work.</meta>

* ( 0 ) *

writes — my heart broke with every stroke of the pen. If I now show ten times over that the expression, the imagery of John <note>Referring to the Gospel of John, which the author views as deeply spiritual</note> was already so old, and was common here and there—yet in a different usage? for a different purpose than is common in this passage?—then I lose the very thing that is most precious: the meaning of John in this specific place. The sap that wells up in the earth before it is drawn in by the tree and purified into its life is indeed a different thing than that which lives here in the beautiful branches and fruits. If you, instead of enjoying these, instead of refreshing yourself in the growth and shade of the tree, peel its bark and lay bare its roots in the earth to see where each one runs and draws its sap, then woe to your murderous hand, or at least to your slave labor! — I hurried, therefore, as much as I could, toward the whole: I only exposed the tree out of necessity, for the sake of the deniers and diluters <note>original: "Läugner und Verschwemmer"; a reference to rationalist theologians who "washed away" the unique spiritual meaning of the text through historical criticism</note>; but no one feels it as much as I do, that all my explanations and citations cling like dust in the sun, or like a weight of earth upon a body that ought to be light and simplicity. Forgive me there, my reader, and pity the fact that I had to do it. But do not dwell upon the bark and the husks; rather, hurry to the sap, to the sense, to the truth. A noble youth, a woman who feels with reason, who here senses simplicity and dignity, a great coherence, a high-calling, and a revelation of God—even if they only feel it from afar, suspect it, or peer at it through the dross—they will throw my book full of dross away and go toward the sun, reading the New Testament with a new sense, a new feeling for the greatness of its content. How greatly would I then be rewarded!

On the other hand, let no one forget that I provide explanations from only one, and indeed only one, source. I must not, therefore, wish to explain more than I am able from this side. No article is completed, and the most secret sap of the New Testament wells up from the revelations of the Old Testament, which I was not permitted to touch upon here. I show the building in an elevation <term>Aufriß: a technical term for a scale drawing or architectural blueprint of a building's facade</term> from only one side: would to God I could show it from all sides, especially in one article, which I was forced to touch upon here in only one way of representation because of my opponents. My purpose was to show that the words Redemption, Savior, Christian, Christ, Name, Priest, Faith, Son of God, King over the Angels, Word, Light-

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense of the historical and linguistic context of the New Testament. Having previously lamented the "murderous hand" of cold criticism, he now explores how diverse ancient cultures—even those considered pagan or "superstitious"—unwittingly prepared the language and concepts that Christ would eventually imbue with divine spirit.</meta>

->* ( ) *<-

The Kingdom of Light, Heaven—even in pagan superstition and in the common usage from which this language originates—meant more and something quite different than what is currently fashionable to let them mean. Thus, if Christ spoke this language and yet was nothing more than what our century claims him to be, he would have been the worst deceiver, or—

Critically speaking, I would have wished for much more regarding my source: to have been able to explain from a Latin translation rather than a French paraphrase; to have drawn from Kerman <note>A city in Iran, likely referring to Persian/Zoroastrian source material</note> rather than from Gujarat <note>A state in India; the author is likely referencing the *Zend-Avesta* or other Eastern texts available in Europe at the time via French translations</note>; to know more about the Sabians and the Disciples of John <note>The "Johannesjünger" likely refers to the Mandaeans, a group that reveres John the Baptist and whose texts were being studied for their parallels to the Gospel of John</note>, and so forth. But what use is wishing? A thing must be all the more recognizable if it remains so faithful even in a French transcription and coming all the way from India, without further news or conjectures from the one who delivered it.

How wonderful it is when one reflects on the developing path of Providence! Did Chaldea <note>Ancient Babylonia, often associated with the origins of astronomical and mystical language</note> imagine that with its clay-like vessels of language, it was forming containers for a future messenger of God, the Son of Heaven? Vessels that he would pour through with Spirit, and—as once happened at the Creation—form a bright world of God out of chaos and refuse? Did Judea imagine that when God drew off the clouded drink from its dregs and forced it into another vessel, where it gathered new and even cloudier dregs, that this fermentation was meant to produce the juice of life? They failed to recognize the ultimate purpose, not only during the dark preparation; even in the clear execution, they failed to recognize it. —

In the broken Greek of the Apostles, ideas and chains of thought flowed together from all the ends of the earth: Judea, Chaldea, Persia, Egypt, Greece, and Rome had all shaped them. Their essence—their fragrance—was destined for the "foolish" preaching of the Gospel of Jesus. What strength and simplicity lies within it! A strength that all our modern paraphrasers still cannot wash away, and which certainly could not have been achieved in the language of Cicero or Xenophon <note>Classical Roman and Greek authors known for their refined, elite prose</note>, just as those languages are unable to replace or express it. Here too, what God chose remained the best: the language of the simple, of non-Greeks, and of the unlearned <note>original: "Unmündigen, Ungriechen und Ungelehrten"</note>; a stumbling block to the "wise" and foolishness to the eloquent Greeks <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 1:23; the author contrasts the rough Greek of the New Testament with the "beautiful babbling" (schönlallend) of classical stylists</note>. Yet within its heart lies Divine Power and Divine Wisdom.

Whoever, therefore, reads this book from the simplest point of view, with the most unifying feeling, reads it best, and will then find a thousand times more in the original book than I was able or permitted to show. ...of the soul, begotten as if it were not begotten, intimate, present, and personal to us. It walks with us, the inner son of our being: making the soul visible to herself: as she was, He was: He is what she herself is.

This inner power of formation and imagination is divine—the seed of God within us. Without it, everything from the outside is death and desolation; within it lies the universe, the invisible, eternal power of the Creator, for which the senses provide only parables, tests, and examples. Power from His power! Light from His light! The reflected countenance of the Highest in a dark, impure drop <note>The author uses the "impure drop" as a metaphor for the human soul reflecting the divine light, albeit through the filter of human limitation.</note>.

Finally, nothing is more active or more beatific than this Word. It is will, the pre-formation of what is to become, power, and deed: a drop from the sea of God's omnipotence, of His omnipresence and bliss in His works: the root of our innermost existence, noblest enjoyment, activity, and life. The Word in our soul is what holds, carries, and moves us. —

Now remove, if you can, all that is imperfect, non-essential, fragmented, and deceptive about these powers: imagine yourself out of your cave of shadows <note>This is a likely reference to Plato’s "Allegory of the Cave," suggesting a move from perceived shadows to true reality.</note> into the light where everything is truth and essence, and feel the voice of revelation: *In the beginning, before all creation, was the Word: it was with God: God was the Word.* He is the radiance of His glory, the <term>character</term> <note>original: "Charakter"; here used in the classical sense of a "stamp" or "engraved mark," as used in Hebrews 1:3.</note>—that is, the image, the imprint, the visible stamp—of His essence, which is entwined in infinity, light, and fullness: the only-begotten, the beloved, eternally from and within His bosom: the very image and delight of the invis-

( 21 )

C 3

...of the invisible God: whoever sees him, sees the Father. He and the Father are One. The entire design of the New Testament rests upon this revelation: even the simple soul <note>original: "Unmündige"; literally 'the minor' or 'the immature,' but used here in the biblical sense of the 'babes' or 'simple-hearted' to whom divine truths are revealed.</note> must find it: without it, without the foundational concept of the eternal divinity of Jesus, everything is mere shadow and ruin.

---

### Explanation.

But from where did John get this expression? Are we also certain that it means this and nothing else? As certain as one can be about any expression in any language in the world.

Since the Jews were in Chaldea, they possessed the **Word** (it is not found in this sense earlier in the Old Testament); but there they held it very strongly and frequently. The Chaldean translator <note>This refers to the authors of the *Targums*, Aramaic translations and paraphrases of the Hebrew Bible, which often used the term "Memra" (Word) as a way to speak of God's action without implying a physical form.</note> applies it wherever possible when speaking of God: how much it meant to the Alexandrians can be seen in Philo; finally, how much it meant in Gnosticism <note>A diverse group of early mystical movements that emphasized "gnosis" or secret knowledge.</note> can be seen in Clement of Alexandria and all the way down the line of heretics <note>original: "Keherreihe" (Ketzerreihe).</note>. Perhaps even Pythagoras (though we know little of him and only through the mouths of the Alexandrians), more clearly Plato, and even more clearly the later Platonists—who coincide with the Alexandrians—shared in this way of thinking as a holy expression of God, or of the Divine in the human soul, and so forth. The expression was thus well-known, and precisely in the meaning that I have developed.

But how did it reach such diverse and scattered sects? Platonists, Hellenists, perhaps Pythagoreans, Jews, Gnostics? How did it arise so early? From what source? Likely from the one I am pointing out; at least the concept of the **Word** appears there in a simplicity that none of the subsequent offshoots possess. In Zoroaster <note>The ancient Persian prophet; the author suggests that the concept of the "Word" (Honover) in Zoroastrianism is the purest or most ancient source of this theological idea.</note>, the expression “Word!” is already present and is sacred in exactly this way of thinking.

<vocab>John, New Testament, Old Testament, Chaldea, Philo, Gnosticism, Clement of Alexandria, Pythagoras, Plato, Platonists, Hellenists, Zoroaster, Word, Logos</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of the term "Word" (Logos). "He calls it the **Beginning** b), the primal start, the origin (John’s *arche* <note>original Greek: "$\alpha\rho\chi\eta$"</note>, the Chaldean *Kedem* <note>A Hebrew/Aramaic term meaning "east" or "that which is ancient/from the start."</note>) in which God existed before any creatures were made. Through this, he personifies the Word as God’s own imprint, eternally and immediately indwelling within Him. He grants it a kind of personality (like a *Ferouer* <note>In Zoroastrianism, a *Ferouer* or *Fravashi* is a pre-existent soul or spiritual essence that acts as a guardian and prototype.</note>), so that it may be invoked as a self-living and self-acting being—eternal, holy, pure, and great. It is inseparable from God, as if He were eternally speaking it: the image of the unobservable Being, enveloped in glory, eternal in its effects, for whom all of nature is merely the veil of His power—and so forth c)." If this is the case, then:

1) All those are right who find similarities between John’s language and other expressions used by the Chaldeans, Greeks, Jews, and Hellenists d). This language was widespread; one can see from which source it flowed. A whole library of disputes and fragmented theories falls away, leaving nothing but to calmly trace the path: "Whence did this way of thinking sprout? How did it spread? How was it transformed by the passage of time, different schools of thought, and individual minds, each adding their own part?" Further material for much of this will be found in the oldest records of the human race — — —

2) No one is right in claiming that John or Paul borrowed their expressions specifically from Plato, Philo, Cerinthus, the Kabbalists, or—why not?—even from Zoroaster e), and his dreaming—

<summary>This page argues that the concept of the "Word" (Logos) used by the Apostles was not stolen from a single philosopher like Plato or Philo, but was part of a widespread ancient tradition connecting God to the world. It suggests that by understanding these cultural roots (Chaldean, Persian, and Jewish), we can resolve long-standing theological disputes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that the term "Logos" or "Word" was a common linguistic vessel of the time, used by many thinkers, rather than a specific borrowing from one sect or philosopher like Plato or Philo. The author suggests that the Gospel writers used the language of their era to convey an entirely new message.</meta>

...held a dreamy application of it. Even based on history, this is not true. Every philosopher, fool, and sectarian used that term to say whatever he wished; John and Paul, however, say what they specifically have to say. For human beings, a human vessel of language <term>Sprachgefäß: literally "language-vessel," a metaphor for how words hold and carry meaning.</term> is required: that was the expression used here. But what did they pour into it? Where did they obtain it, and what was it intended to hold for them? These are entirely different questions. Is there no difference between a tool and what is accomplished with it? And is it wise, out of a desire for great scholarship, to always fixate on the finger without perceiving what the finger is doing? <note>This refers to the Zen-like proverb about the finger pointing at the moon; the scholar focuses on the word (the finger) rather than the truth it points to.</note> Christ himself, for example, never used the expression "Word" in that sense; for the Galilean Christ spoke in no artificial language <note>original: *Kunstsprache*</note>. In his simple, comprehensible folk-language, however, he expressed all the high ideas that this expression only dimly grasped. John and Paul could—and perhaps had to—use expressions that Plato, Philo, or Zoroaster <note>The founder of ancient Persian Zoroastrianism; the author implies a cross-cultural exchange of ideas.</note> had used elsewhere: they spoke the common, understandable language and proclaimed within it their high, authentically new Gospel, without worrying about who else had used the words they employed.

3) Most unworthy, it seems to me, is the notorious fairy tale "that John's Gospel was written against Cerinthus," <note>Cerinthus was an early Gnostic teacher whom tradition, via Irenaeus, claimed was the antagonist John wrote his Gospel to refute.</note> a claim defended most strongly by pointing to the language of this opening. Even if accepted as true, it is not worth the defense. If this language is neither unique to Cerinthus, nor invented by him, nor even in prominent use by him—and if in the whole Gospel there is no trace of such a narrow, miserable polemical purpose, but rather from beginning to end everything moves toward a higher goal: *that you may believe Jesus is Christ the Son of God, and that through faith you may have life in his name:* then what is the use or reward in forcing it into the narrow box of Cerinthus, when it breathes such wider, freer, and purer ether? Who will not believe John—who openly states his own purpose and demonstrates it through the whole work—more than the collector of fairy tales, Irenaeus? <note>Irenaeus of Lyons (2nd century), an early Church Father who documented various "heretical" sects.</note> Moreover, this unworthy fairy tale does not even hold up. If someone claimed that Homer cobbled together his mythology from Huet and Banier <note>Pierre Daniel Huet and Abbé Banier were 17th and 18th-century scholars who attempted to explain myths as historical or allegorical fabrications.</note>, it would still be nothing compared to the claim that John [composed] his luminous Gospel out of the mud-puddle of the personifications of this fool

<meta>The previous page established that the Gospel writers used the "Logos" or "Word" as a common linguistic tool of their era to express something entirely new, rather than simply borrowing from a single philosopher like Philo or a specific sect.</meta>

...believed by fools. Is a body in decay, where everything already swarms with living personifications, supposed to be the archetype of this healthy human being? Have not so many others, earlier and better, possessed the same language much more closely and uniformly—those whom the blissfully ignorant Cerinthus-hypothesis <note>The "Cerinthus-hypothesis" refers to the theory that the Apostle John wrote his Gospel to refute the heretic Cerinthus, or that he borrowed terminology from him. The author finds this theory insufficient to explain the depth of John's language.</note> must either deny or does not know at all? In short, the alleged sectarian language of a fool becomes, if one lifts one's eyes, the ancient, widely distributed, and cultivated language of the philosophical world of that time. For John and Paul, it was the most intelligible and worthy expression.

And now the Apostles return again to Jesus, whom the Cerinthus-hypothesis unworthily tore away. They say exactly the same thing Jesus said, not in the hypocritical expression of a heretic, but in a self-chosen language of truth.

And now Socinianism <note>A 16th and 17th-century theological movement that rejected the Trinity and the pre-existence of Christ, often interpreting New Testament terms in a more literal or "rational" way.</note>, which washes up here, likes to take "Beginning" as the start of the New Testament era, "Word" as the Gospel, "Power of God" and the like—however many patrons it may find, it is distorting and worthless. The obvious connection and stream of linguistic usage of so many nations, dialects, minds, and heretics—who truly did not want to be orthodox regarding the divinity of Christ—stands against it. The beginning of the Gospel of John, in the splendor of its time, is a foundation of truth.

The saying in the Epistle to the Hebrews—that Jesus is the radiance, the light-image of God’s glory, and the character, the imprint as it were, of the Father’s essence—is just the same. It is ignorance of the language to take "radiance" <term>apaugasma: original Greek $\alpha\pi\alpha\upsilon\gamma\alpha\sigma\mu\alpha$, often translated as "effulgence" or "reflection."</term> for a mere ray, such and such a reflection, an atmospheric phenomenon, the Shechinah <note>The Hebrew term for the "dwelling" or divine presence of God, often depicted as a cloud of light.</note>, and what else? It means splendor <term>phengos: original Greek $\Phi\epsilon\gamma\gamma o\varsigma$, meaning a brilliant light or luster.</term>, a radiant image—for example, the sun itself—just as "character" <term>charakter: original Greek $\chi\alpha\rho\alpha\kappa\tau\eta\rho$, meaning the mark or impression made by a seal.</term> is the imprint or stamp of the thing <term>hypostasis: original Greek $\upsilon\pi o\sigma\tau\alpha\sigma\iota\varsigma$, meaning the underlying substance, essence, or reality.</term> that is being depicted f). If Paul is supposed to speak here of Jesus after the resurrection—"dear friend! go and ask...

<meta>The previous page concluded by arguing that the language used by John and Paul regarding the "Word" was not a local sectarian dialect, but a widely understood philosophical language of the time. It ended with the "catchword" indicating that the argument would "go" forward to examine how this language applies to Jesus's pre-existence.</meta>

"Ask the Apostle himself about it!" says a modernizer <note>The author likely refers to contemporary Enlightenment theologians who sought to reinterpret traditional doctrines through a purely rational or historical lens.</note>, and if that is the case, I must confess that I do not understand a single word of the clearest passages in the New Testament. The very same expressions used here specifically for Jesus are used by others to describe **Wisdom**, which was with God before the foundation of the world; the blueprint of the world in the mind of God; the face of power and goodness through which He poured His image into His creatures. This **archetype** they sometimes call His **Son**, the one begotten within Him; or Wisdom, who was with Him or at His right hand; or His **daughter**, in whom He rejoiced and took delight g). All these expressions were therefore already well-known. In the *Orphic Fragments* <note>Ancient Greek poems attributed to the mythical Orpheus, which discussed the divine "Word" or "Logos" in a cosmological sense.</note>, the "face," the "radiance," the "mirror of God" is already called His **Word**, placed before the creation of the world, and so on. If someone were to come along now and find in this personification something like the return of Eurydice from the underworld, with what "Sardonic" and "Phrygian" mocking laughter <note>Classical idioms for extremely harsh or derisive scorn.</note> would the commentators receive him! Yet, when it comes to the clear, unpoetic, historical sense of the New Testament, everything is permitted. Go ahead and invent "office names" or "prophetic titles" applied after the resurrection to what is said here *before the creation of the world*; invent whatever you please.

If only I could persuade a single honest doubter to examine the expressions in which the New Testament speaks of the existence and divinity of Jesus before all creation—at and with God—within the writings of pagan and Jewish authors. The test is so impartial and the meaning so obvious! Now, the entire New Testament doctrine of the divinity of Jesus proceeds from those given concepts, clearly defined in pagan writers: <term>Word</term> <note>original: "λογος" (logos)</note>, <term>Image</term> <note>original: "εικων" (eikon)</note>, <term>Radiance</term> <note>original: "απαυγασμα" (apaugasma)</note>, the "Image of the Invisible, Unbeholdable." To these it adds the derived terms <term>Son</term> <note>original: "υιος" (huios)</note>, <term>Firstborn</term> <note>original: "πρωτογονος" (protogonos)</note>, <term>Only-begotten</term> <note>original: "μονογενης" (monogenēs)</note>, "Son," "Lord," "Firstborn"; (here too, linguistic usage dictates the definition of these derivatives). The first Church Fathers, to whom **Socinianism** <note>A theological movement that rejected the Trinity and Christ's pre-existence, arguing he was a man who became divine.</note> was certainly a foreign concept, followed this; as did the best teachers of our Church, among whom I name **Melanchthon** first h).

g) See Grotius <note>Hugo Grotius (1583–1645), a famous Dutch scholar and theologian.</note> on John.

And the words <term>Only-begotten</term> <note>original: "μονογενης"</note>, <term>Son of God</term> <note>original: "υιος θεου"</note>, <term>Holy</term> <note>original: "οσιος"</note> of the <term>Lord</term> <note>original: "Κυριος"</note> have been developed from the linguistic usage of Hellenism and not from Socinus.

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page, where the author began refuting the idea that the Apostles borrowed their theological language from contemporary heretics. ...supposedly believed by a fool. A body in a state of decay, where everything already swarms with living personifications <note>A reference to the complex Gnostic systems of "Aeons" or divine emanations, which the author views as a corruption of simpler truths.</note>, is supposed to be the archetype of this healthy person! A language which the blessedly ignorant **Cerinthus hypothesis** <note>The theory that the Apostle John wrote his Gospel specifically to combat or adapt the terminology of the heretic Cerinthus.</note> must either deny or does not know at all. In short: the alleged sectarian language of a fool becomes, if one only opens one's eyes, the ancient, widespread, and refined language of the philosophical world of that time. For John and Paul, it was the most intelligible and worthy expression.

And now the Apostles return once more to Jesus, from whom the Cerinthus hypothesis unworthily tore them away. They say exactly the same things that Jesus said, not in the hypocritical expressions of a heretic, but in a self-chosen language of truth.

And now we come to **Socinianism** <note>A rationalist Christian movement that denied the Trinity and the pre-existence of Christ, often interpreting "the Word" as merely the preaching of the Gospel.</note>, which washes up here and likes to take "the beginning" as the "beginning of the New Testament times," "the Word" as "the Gospel," the "power of God," and the like. However many patrons this view may find, it is distorting and worthless. The manifest connection and the flow of linguistic usage across so many nations, dialects, thinkers, and even heretics—who truly did not wish to be orthodox regarding the divinity of Christ—stands against it. The beginning of the Gospel of John, seen in the brilliance of its time, is a foundation of truth.

The passage in Hebrews stating that Jesus is the **radiance** <note>original: "$\alpha\pi\alpha\upsilon\gamma\alpha\sigma\mu\alpha$" (apaugasma)</note>, the light-image of God's glory, and the **character** <note>original: "$\chi\alpha\rho\alpha\kappa\tau\eta\rho$" (charaktēr)</note>—the imprint, as it were, of the Father’s **essence** <note>original: "$\upsilon\pi o\sigma\alpha\tau\iota\varsigma$" (hypostasis) – the author uses the term for the fundamental reality or substance of a thing.</note>—is exactly the same. It is an ignorance of the language to take "radiance" for a mere ray, or this or that reflection, or an atmospheric phenomenon, or the **Shekinah** <note>A Hebrew term for the "settling" or visible presence of God's glory.</note>, or what have you. It means **splendor** <note>original: "$\phi\epsilon\gamma\gamma o\varsigma$" (phengos)</note>, a radiant image—for example, the sun itself—just as the "character" is the impression or imprint of the actual thing being depicted. If Paul is supposed to be speaking here of Jesus only after the resurrection—"my dear friend! go and

<meta>This page continues the critique of Socinian and modern rationalist interpretations of the New Testament. It follows from the previous page's discussion on how the Apostles used established philosophical and theological language to describe the nature of Christ, rather than inventing a new sectarian jargon. Catchword: go [gehen].</meta>

->* ( ) *<-

“Ask the Apostle himself about it!” says a modernist <note>Referring to Enlightenment-era rationalists or Socinians who sought to reinterpret the divinity of Christ through a purely human or temporal lens.</note>, and if that holds true, I confess that I do not understand a single word of even the clearest passages in the New Testament. The very same expressions that are specifically used here for Jesus are used by others for the Wisdom that was with God before the foundation of the world, for the design of the world in the mind of God, and for the countenance of power and goodness through which He poured His image into all creatures. This original image <note>Urbild</note> they sometimes call His Son, the one begotten in Him; the Wisdom that was with Him or at His right hand; or His daughter, in whom He rejoiced and took delight g). All these expressions were therefore already well-known. In the **Orphic Fragments** <note>Ancient mystical poems attributed to the legendary Orpheus, which early Christian apologists often cited to show parallels between Greek philosophy and the concept of the Divine Word.</note>, the face, the radiance, and the mirror of God are already called His Word, placed there before the creation of the world, and so on. If someone were now to come along and find in this personification the return of Eurydice from hell or some such thing: with what Sardonic and Phrygian mocking laughter <note>Classical references to types of scorn; "Sardonic" refers to bitter mockery, while "Phrygian" suggests a more wild or frenzied derision.</note> would the commentators receive him! Yet, regarding the clear, unpoetic, historical sense of the New Testament, anything is permitted. Fabricate an official title, a prophetic rank after the resurrection, out of what is said here to have existed before the creation of the world; fabricate whatever you have a desire for.

If I could only succeed in persuading a single honest doubter to examine the expressions in which the New Testament speaks of the existence and the divinity of Jesus before all creation—as being by and with God—within the works of pagan and Jewish writers. The test is so impartial and the meaning so obvious! Now, the entire New Testament doctrine of the divinity of Jesus proceeds from these given concepts, which are clearly defined in pagan writers: <term>logos</term> <note>original: "λογος"; The Word or Reason.</note>, <term>eikon</term> <note>original: "εικων"; Image.</note>, <term>apaugasma</term> <note>original: "απαυγασμα"; Radiance or reflection.</note>, the Image of the Invisible, the Unperceivable. To these it adds the derivative terms <term>huios</term> <note>original: "υιος"; Son.</note>, <term>protogonos</term> <note>original: "προλογονος"; First-born.</note>, <term>monogenes</term> <note>original: "μονογενης"; Only-begotten</note>—Son, Lord, First-born (here too, linguistic usage continues to define these derivatives). The first Church Fathers, to whom **Socinianism** <note>A non-trinitarian doctrine that denied the pre-existence of Christ, viewing him as a man who was granted divinity after his resurrection.</note> was certainly a foreign concept, followed this; as did the best teachers of our Church, among whom I name **Melanchthon** <note>Philipp Melanchthon (1497–1560), a key leader of the Protestant Reformation alongside Luther.</note> first h).

<meta>This page concludes the argument from the previous page, contrasting the "clear path" of the Apostles with the convoluted interpretations of modern rationalists. It continues the critique of those who treat the divine nature as a mere philosophical puzzle.</meta>

...go exactly that way. Only a tiresome philosophy and **Federal Theology** <term>Bundestheologie; a theological framework that interprets the Bible through "covenants." The author suggests this system became too caught up in legalistic or physical definitions of divine relationships.</term> could have turned the act of begetting and sonship into fundamental concepts derived from such physical terms. At this, the **Socinians** <term>Followers of a 16th-century rationalist movement that denied the Trinity and the full divinity of Christ.</term> took offense, and responded to one misuse with an even greater misuse. Thus the doctrine lies neglected in our "dear philosophical age," where God is moreover demonstrated to be a **Monad** <term>A term from Leibnizian philosophy meaning a singular, indivisible unit; here used to mock a God who is so solitary He cannot have a "Son."</term> and therefore cannot beget a Son or an eternal Word, who is His image. **Which was to be proven.** <note>original: "W. Z. E." (Was zu erweisen), the German equivalent of the Latin Q.E.D., or "Quod erat demonstrandum."</note>

Let us return to the simplicity of the Scriptures! And you, philosopher—if, instead of investigating God with the eye of an art critic, you would only pay attention to the incomprehensible "One and Many" within your own Monad—see, that is merely an image in the shadows, a dark parable! In creation, we see God as if through a mist; in Jesus, we see Him in a clear image. He was the personal, eternal Word of God.

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding the "eternal Word" as the personal image of God. While the previous page critiqued philosophical abstractions like "Monads," this page turns to the Johannine imagery of light and life to describe the act of creation.</meta>

## II.
## The Creator of the World.

---

All things were made through the Word. Nothing that has been made was made without the Word. In the Word was life, and that life was the light of mankind. The light shines in the darkness, and the darkness did not comprehend it—that is, did not take it in, or was not able to withstand it. <note>The author is paraphrasing the Gospel of John 1:3-5. He provides two interpretations for the Greek *katalambanō* (comprehend/overcome): receiving the light or resisting it.</note> Behold there, the portrait of the emerging and finished creation up to the noblest forms of existence, all arising from a single Ground.

Light radiates only light. The eternal Word—which, like God, was light and life—when it shared itself in creation, became the primal source of light and life for the purpose of a higher light and life. Even the noblest light that we know, human reason <term>Menschenvernunft; the human capacity for rational thought, logic, and moral discernment.</term> and life, flowed from Him. Into the darkness, upon a great ocean of undeveloped—and thus to-be-developed—forces, they were scattered, these higher sparks of life, like stars upon the dark tapestry of the night. The night is not of their kind: it does not grasp them, but it neither can nor should swallow them up. They are meant to keep radiating, to become flames, to unite themselves, and to transform the darkness into light: behold the blueprint of the world! All paths of creation are gradations <note>original: "Stufengänge," referring to a "Great Chain of Being" or a stepped progression from lower to higher forms of life.</note> toward light and life.

Happy are we, to whom even a ray of light from His essence became human life. We are here to recognize, to will, to love, and to act, just as He recognizes and acts. We are of His kind. <note>original: "seines Geschlechts." This likely references Acts 17:28, "For we are also his offspring."</note>

<vocab>Creator, Word, Light, Life, Darkness, Human Reason, Creation</vocab>

It presents creation not as a cold mechanical act, but as a purposeful progression of divine sparks returning to their source.</summary>

# Notes.

If it is not a matter of mere wordplay here—that life sometimes means light and light means life—but rather *why* one signifies the other; how John can simply say: *In the Word was life, and the life was the light, and indeed the light of men: “He is the true light that enlightens all people who come into the world,”* then we are here at the source. “According to the Oriental <note>In the 18th century, "Oriental" was a broad term used by scholars to describe the cultures of the Near and Middle East, particularly Persia and the Levant.</note> way of thinking, the innermost ground of creation is life, and this life is light: to them, the entire creation is a purification of light into life. The more differentiation of light, the more the breaking-forth of life; the finer and more serene the light, the purer and more inward the life. To Zoroaster <note>Zoroaster is the Greek name for Zarathustra, the ancient Persian prophet. The author is using the "Zend-Avesta"—the primary collection of Zoroastrian religious texts—to find parallels with the Gospel of John.</note>, the life of the earth, the sun, the trees, the animals, and humans are so many various stages and purifications of light into life, up to the purest life, most similar to God: the image of God in holy, charitable, and kind humans a). — Creation is, to him, the action of light and life. God, hidden in His fullness, dwelling in uncreated light—He stepped forth through the Word, which, like Him, is light and life: there, light and life welled up: there arose the magnificent, beautiful blueprint of existence, germinating with light and life. God spoke His Word and all beings came to be. He speaks it eternally: they exist and endure. God speaks: ‘I am,’ and all beings come to be!” b) Here, too, all the chatter about Aeons <term>Aeons; in Gnostic systems, these were a series of spiritual emanations or lesser deities that acted as intermediaries between the high God and the physical world. The author argues that John's "Word" makes these unnecessary.</term> and poetic fictions falls away. There is neither a separate source of light nor a separate creator of the world; the Son, the eternal Word, was the source of all light and life—that is, the deepest primal grounds of creation in both its being and its progression toward higher stages.

This is exactly what Paul also says. Heaven and earth, the visible and the invisible, thrones, archangels, principalities (likewise...

...these same bright, mighty primordial grounds of creation, which John depicts as light and life), are through him.

Here too, people have agreed to transform "Heaven and Earth," the visible and the invisible, the first powers, angels, and archangels of the invisible world—into Jewish and pagan authorities <note>The author is criticizing contemporary scholars who tried to "demythologize" the Bible by interpreting references to angels and spiritual powers as mere metaphors for earthly governments or officials.</note> which Jesus created—no! not created, but—united, driven together into a beautiful new body of a single confession of faith: and with such word-chatter they fill the New Testament to the brim.

In Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria was a Jewish philosopher who blended Hebrew scripture with Greek philosophy, particularly the concept of the "Logos."</note> and beyond Philo, in the Orient and in all the languages of the world, "Heaven and Earth," the visible and the invisible, is the clearest, most common designation for the universe. In the passage of Colossians 1:16—let verses 18–20 speak of whatever they may—verse 15 is about the existence of Jesus before the world, verse 16 about his creation, and verse 17 about his preservation of all things. The connection is clamoring for this interpretation.

It is just as clamoring that the Orient, through angels, archangels, princes, thrones, ranks, and powers of the spirit realm, understands these all to form one kingdom, one active chain of beings. In the *Zend-Avesta* <note>The primary collection of religious texts of Zoroastrianism, an ancient Persian religion.</note>, the most common names are kings, princes, hosts, and heroes. "They are all images of God, kings like Him, workers and warriors for the good, as He is. The first seven at His throne are the supreme kings; their helpers and fellow warriors are powers, princes, authorities; all together they constitute one state, one kingdom, the heavenly population." c) Since the time of Chaldea <note>An ancient region in Babylonia; the author suggests that Jewish thought on angels was heavily influenced by their time in Babylonian exile.</note>, this was the language of the Jews, the Hellenists, Alexandrians, Platonists, and the entire age in which the Apostles wrote, until long before and long after d). Now I leave it to anyone to decide whether, if the words in every other writer and in every other case mean exactly that, they should here mean something else entirely—shoemakers and mayors—

...should, when—Jesus is being spoken of. The first, extremely misunderstood chapter of the Epistle to the Hebrews shows this with all the abundance of imagery that Hellenism <note>The blending of Greek culture and philosophy with other Middle Eastern traditions, which heavily influenced early Christian writing.</note> could grant it. It is not in the least the Apostle’s purpose to explain the Old Testament, or to dispute, or to show where Jesus is spoken of in the Old Testament, and it always becomes nonsensical against itself and its context if one assumes such a thing. But to praise, to proclaim the glory of the Son, with everything great he could find and say—that is what he wants. He struggles with images, with all the modes of representation in Hellenism, to show Jesus as the Unique One, incomparable to any angel or archangel, as the God of the entire spirit world. He bears all things with his mighty word! Therefore, he is not the Word as a mere tool, as a secondary cause; he is primal power.

Let one trace in Hellenism what it says of its merely personified Word <note>original: "λογος" (Logos)</note> <margin>e) See Grotius on John; Carpzov on Hebrews; Mangey on Philo; and all those who have collected [references] on the Logos. <note>Hugo Grotius, J.B. Carpzov, and Thomas Mangey were prominent 17th and 18th-century biblical scholars and philologists.</note></margin>. It was, according to this view, the eternal Wisdom—the helper, counselor, and master-builder of God before and as he created. It was the active Will, the outline and draft of creation, which flowed out into the visible world only as if it had thrown a veil around itself and lived within creation, all-working and all-shaping, as if in its own house and temple. The early Church Fathers developed this in part so sensorially that they even imagined in Jesus a prototype, an original image of creation, particularly of the human race. They spoke of a heavenly body according to whose form Adam was fashioned: Gnostics <note>A religious movement that emphasized secret knowledge and a complex hierarchy of spiritual beings.</note> and visionaries <note>original: "Schwärmer," a term for religious enthusiasts who claimed direct divine inspiration.</note> imagined more and more concerning the first heavenly man: the entire draft of creation finally took on human form. Dreams, to be sure, and exaggerations! Yet they all show that our most recent interpretation—"Jesus is in the year of the world <insert>4000</insert>

“born as God in the year 4000 or made God in the year 4034,” <note>The author is likely critiquing specific 18th-century chronological attempts to date the divinity of Christ or the creation of the world.</note> stands in opposition to all modes of representation and linguistic usage, like East to West, and destroys the entire structure of the expression.

The light shines here too in the darkness: the darkness cannot devour it and will not comprehend it. <note>A reference to John 1:5.</note> This is the explanation of creation—what it is and what it is to become. The rays of the most noble light flowed into the night, but were not lost. “Darkness,” personifies Zoroaster, <note>The ancient Persian prophet and founder of Zoroastrianism, whose dualistic philosophy focused on the battle between light (Asha) and darkness (Druj).</note> “saw the emerging light in its brilliance and beauty—rushed forward to defile it; but stared back into its own realm, the wasteland, and could do nothing against it. God spoke his heavenly Word and the eternal night with all its hosts sank into its abyss.” f) From the first beginning ray, this was the design of creation! Whatever is light and life in the world, however scattered it may be, however much it may seem to be in the dark; the darkness struggles: but the good in the world must overcome the evil, light must overcome the darkness! Here too, the Word provides us with an explanation for what would otherwise be only the beautiful chaos of creation.
