### III.

### In Jesus the Human Race is Chosen.

The Omniscient one, who foresaw sin and death within the human race, why did he create it? Why did he call it to life? Scripture does not engage in any philosophical "feeble justifications" <note>original: "Bettelgründe." This literally means "beggarly grounds," referring to weak or pathetic arguments used to explain away difficult theological problems.</note> regarding the permission or non-permission of evil; rather, it simply answers: in Jesus the human race was created and chosen. That is, God would not have called it into existence if he had counted on a certain self-sustaining righteousness that later betrayed his calculation; in Jesus we are ordained, even with our weak, fallible nature, to such higher purposes of wisdom and love <margin>a) Ephesians 1:3-6.</margin>.

When Adam falls, how petty it is to treat this fall as a blunder by God, as an accident that befell God's design! How petty to involve Goodness and Justice (two abstractions that are one in the Most High!) in a dispute over rank, as to whether sinful man should now be annihilated and rejected, or not? and then finally, in the pressure of unforeseen necessity, after a better design had failed, to have God take refuge in a second, as it were, inferior <term>emergency plan|Nothplan</term>—for the redemption of the world through Jesus! Hardly anything more unworthy toward God, more false toward humanity—as far as our concept of it goes—or finally more contradictory to the entire design of Scripture can be imagined. Satan becomes a monarch: the Creator becomes one who errs, and the Redeemer of the world becomes one subordinated to the cunning of Satan, forced into emergency plans—

<summary>This page argues that the human race was created with God’s full foreknowledge of sin, and that salvation through Jesus was the primary plan rather than a secondary "emergency" response to the Fall. The author rejects theological views that depict God as reacting to Satan's interference with a backup plan.</summary>

<keywords>election, Jesus, human race, original sin, Adam, divine plan, emergency plan, Satan, redemption, Ephesians 1</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>Continuity with previous page: The author concludes a critique of the "emergency plan" view of redemption—the idea that Jesus was a backup plan after Adam's failure. This page continues by contrasting that "petty" view with the expansive "philosophy" of the Apostle Paul, who saw humanity's fall and restoration as a single, unified divine design.</meta>

...petty, small-minded view. What a different philosophy Paul holds—as in all his letters, so too in the much-disputed and misunderstood fifth chapter of Romans. Sin and death also had their dominion assigned to them by God, but under Jesus; his higher grace and gift were intended precisely to bring life and bliss out of that mixture. The *forefather Adam*, bowed under sin, already carried within him the great theater of reconciliation. —

God effectively bound everything under sin, so that he might have mercy on all <margin>b) Romans 1–8; Galatians 2–4.</margin>. Law and wrath and transgression also had to precede in the ages of the human race, to prepare for and point toward Jesus. That God chose us through Jesus before the foundation of the world was laid, and accepted us in him, the Beloved! How this truth was kept silent—that is, not yet recognized in its full dignity—through all the preparatory ages; but now it has been revealed and shall be proclaimed everywhere, that everything comes together in Jesus and is contained in the design of which He is the head. This is the **world-encompassing Theodicy** <note>A "Theodicy" is a theological defense of God's goodness and providence in spite of the existence of evil in the world.</note> of the Apostles <margin>c) Ephesians 1:3–14; Colossians 1:18–21; 1 Corinthians 15:48–49.</margin>.

And of Jesus. The human race is given to him by God in a design of salvation. To enliven the world, to make sinners blessed, is his work which transcends all ages <margin>d) John 3:15–21; John 17:2–24.</margin>. *Savior! Word of God* concerning our destiny! The *cornerstone* of our election <margin>e) 1 Peter 2:4–9.</margin>. What the first Adam was for earthly life and for sin, Jesus was for spiritual life and for grace.

## Remarks.

Every small work of art created by a human, where multiple components serve a single purpose, must be designed with those multiple parts—that is, the whole—in mind. A musical composition, a play, a painting—if it consists of many individual elements but does not form a unified whole, it is a poor work of art. This is how we judge the works of men, and certainly God’s masterpiece, the **human race**, can be judged by no lesser rule of perfection. In its entirety, there must lie a purpose, a design, and a destiny, otherwise no individual part possesses any of these qualities. What follows is this:

1. The development of such a design is by no means an arbitrary philosophy <note>The author uses "eigenmächtige Philosophie" to describe a "self-authorized" or speculative system created in isolation by a single thinker.</note> emerging from a single brain, but rather the **history of all**. That which extends across the sequence of time can only be developed within that sequence of time, and can only be recognized from it. The destiny of every individual is a fraction of the whole, just as that individual is a part of the whole. The *forefather Adam*, certainly the most intuitive spirit, who felt his partial destiny in the name of all, must still have known very little of the purpose for which the entire host of his children was to exist. What a revelation has since been granted to him! What a revelation shall yet be granted to him!

2. As subsequent ages unfold, and as more individual cases and experiences yield results, the more—it is said—reason grows. The most general and robust reason can only be the result of the entire experience of the human race, and I doubt whether it has ever truly been able to be so until now. The ends of all human experience have certainly not yet been brought together and linked. The ages have, like colossi <note>A reference to "Colossuses" or giant statues, suggesting massive, overwhelming historical epochs that dwarf the individual.</note>, tumbled over one another and buried one another. The nations are like scattered flocks: what philosopher exists who can give an account of why they are there, or why they have been there? Not to mention the abyss of the future <note>original: "Zukunft". The author may be referring to the "abyss" of time generally, or perhaps the "past" (Vorzeit) given the phrase "behind us," but the text specifically says "future."</note> behind us! We stand on a rocky cliff between two immeasurable depths and wish to [achieve] the highest human—

<summary>The author argues that humanity must be viewed as a single, unified masterpiece of God, where the meaning of individual lives can only be understood through the lens of total human history. He reflects on how reason grows through collective experience, though history often appears as a series of disconnected, "buried" civilizations.</summary>
<keywords>human race, divine design, history, reason, Adam, experience, teleology, philosophy of history</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>Continuity with previous page: The author continues exploring the growth of human reason, moving from the idea that wisdom is a collective historical achievement rather than an individual one.</meta>

...of human reason, of the meaning of all experience for insight into the destiny of our race!

3. This reason itself, if it is only formed through the sequence of time, reveals that everything which has educated, taught, and advanced the human race has also formed that reason. A child develops its reason only through education: therefore, reason owes everything it has become to that which has educated the human race. It would be a frivolous game if we wanted to separate one from the other and consider reason as an independent abstraction, for in such a state, it is nothing. Well then! It is also a frivolous game to set reason in opposition to revelation, and to act against it as if reason were something self-sufficient. Just as the human race could not have come into being without creation, it could not have continued without divine assistance, nor known what it knows without divine education.

4. Furthermore, one must not measure this entire development by the yardstick <term>original: "Ellenmaas," a traditional German unit of measurement</term> of specific times and regions; for how many peoples are idle—and to all appearances have always been idle! Rather, it must be calculated intimately and from experience: which times and peoples God primarily chose for the enlightenment and advancement of the human race. These, then, are the shining points in the night! The leaven <note>A biblical metaphor (Matthew 13:33) for a small influence that spreads through and transforms a larger whole.</note> among the nations. And there, history and a glance at the world show us that these have been only a few, specifically those regions where the Jewish and Christian revelation took hold and continued to work. That is a historical fact. In other regions, self-acting human reason still lies in slumber.

5. Even on this narrow edge of the world's enlightenment, everything does not proceed in equal measure, step by step; rather, it moves in revolutions a). One point of development may last for centuries:

| | |
|---|---|
| a) In the *Zend-Avesta* <note>The primary collection of religious texts of Zoroastrianism.</note> it is a fundamental concept to regard the duration of the world as a structure of various, pre-determined revolutions or world-dominions, from which the later Aeons <note>original: "Aeonen," meaning ages or epochs of time</note> undoubtedly arose. Thus, there is a long Aeon where Ahriman <note>The spirit of evil or darkness in Zoroastrianism.</note> still rules, the age of temptation, after which his power shall be taken from him. Ormuzd <note>Also known as Ahura Mazda, the creator god and spirit of light/goodness in Zoroastrianism.</note> knows this, but Ahriman does not. This is precisely the concept of the *Olam* <note>original: "Olam," a Hebrew term referring to the world or a vast expanse of time/eternity.</note> and the Aeons that appear so often in the Chaldean parts of the Book of Daniel. | |

<meta>catchword: easily:</meta>

<summary>This page argues that human reason is not an innate, independent faculty but a product of historical divine education and revelation. It highlights that progress occurs through specific "chosen" nations and historical "revolutions" or epochs, drawing parallels between Christian history and Zoroastrian concepts of time.</summary>
<keywords>reason, education of the human race, divine revelation, Jewish revelation, Christian revelation, Zoroastrianism, Zend-Avesta, Ahriman, Ormuzd, Olam, Aeons, Book of Daniel</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>Continuity with previous page: The author continues discussing the historical development of human reason, concluding that reason is not an independent faculty but one shaped by divine education and revelation. The previous page ended with the observation that while many nations remained stagnant, those touched by revelation became the "leaven" of humanity.</meta>

...reasonableness.

Easily: with the light of its height, a people who might otherwise be superstitious can see further than a series of wiser nations around them; it can possess the treasure of a knowledge regarding the human race and yet use it the least. Such a history of the human race in its destiny is evidently the blueprint of our Bible, which has endured for millennia. Otherwise, it is neither the Quran, nor the Zend-Avesta <note>The primary collection of religious texts of Zoroastrianism.</note>, nor all the Brahmin books and teachings of Confucius in the world that evidently have this purpose. They are mythology, liturgy, beautiful morality, but nothing that is actually supposed to be revelation—an explanation of the human race in its destiny.

6. Our Bible is this in the simplest way. It contains history and teaching that a child understands, and a plan is developed within it that is for no one too cold, too high, or too difficult, and yet is the explanation of human nature—the sum of our expectations and wishes! It stirs all our soul-powers, occupies all our drives, and proceeds from the beginning of the human race into the holy darkness of the furthest future, losing itself in the eternal and in the light.

7. And Jesus is the center and cornerstone of the whole, the middle link of the calculation. In him Adam is created; by him the last of men is judged; through him the race of his brothers goes to God. That was the mystery <note>original: "Geheimniß," referring to the Pauline "mysterium" of Christ.</note>, says Paul, that all previous ages did not recognize, and which Jesus brought from the bosom of the Father. In him we are chosen. — Unworthy are those dogmas that do not build upon this foundation, but rather philosophize and then only take the words of the Bible along so that revelation and philosophy eternally remain two different things. Deficient is every morality that builds upon essential human holiness, self-grown integrity <term>original: "Rechtschaffenheit," here used to mean a self-sufficient moral uprightness</term> and the like: where does this holiness and integrity live and move? b) i.e., whole, bold competence.

---

<margin>
b) The word integrity <note>original: "Rechtschaffenheit"</note> means nothing other than competence <note>original: "Tüchtigkeit"</note>—that one is what one ought to be: primarily strength, courage, bravery. The concept of virtue before God and of human destiny is therefore not yet determined by it; it must first be brought into it: when is a person "upright" <note>original: "rechtschaffen"</note>, that is, when are they "whole" in what they are to be for eternity? (Currently, they are not), and through what do they become so?
</margin>

<summary>The author argues that the Bible is the only true revelation of human destiny, surpassing other world religions by providing a simple yet profound plan centered on Jesus Christ. He criticizes moral systems based on self-sufficient "integrity," arguing that true human wholeness is only achieved through the divine plan.</summary>

...righteousness of man before God? Finally, all philosophies of consolation <note>original: "Trostphilosophien"—referring to Enlightenment-era philosophical systems designed to provide moral comfort and purpose through reason alone.</note>, which draw and weave the eternal destiny of man in a straight line out of themselves—See! the spider leans upon its house, yet it does not stand; it holds onto it, and yet the house has no stability of its own. <note>An allusion to Job 8:14–15, comparing the trust of the godless to a spider’s web.</note> If philosophy is what it should be, then it will recognize its origin, its power, and its limits, and lose itself in revelation—that is, in the disclosure of the education of the human race <note>original: "Bildung des Menschengeschlechts"—a central concept in 18th-century German thought (notably used by Lessing and Herder) viewing human history as a divine pedagogical process.</note>, which has also formed philosophy itself. It is only the thin cloud of abstraction that has gradually risen over the fragrant plants of God's rich garden and is nothing without this garden.

**Vocabulary**
<term>Philosophies of consolation (Trostphilosophien)</term>: Systems of thought intended to provide comfort regarding death and suffering.
<term>Destiny of man (Bestimmung des Menschen)</term>: A popular 18th-century theological and philosophical theme regarding the purpose of human existence.
<term>Revelation (Offenbarung)</term>: The communication of truth from a divine source to humanity.
<term>Education of the human race (Bildung des Menschengeschlechts)</term>: The idea that God "educates" humanity through history and scripture.
<term>Abstraction (Abstraktion)</term>: The process of considering something independently of its associations or concrete reality.

<summary>This page argues that human philosophy is fragile and derivative, likening it to a spider's web or a cloud of steam that only exists because of the 'garden' of divine revelation. It positions philosophy not as an independent authority, but as a secondary reflection of the historical education provided to humanity by God.</summary>

easily: with the light of its heights, a perhaps otherwise superstitious people sees further than a series of wiser nations around them; they can possess the treasure of a knowledge concerning the human race and use it the least. 6. Our Bible is this in the simplest way. ### IV.
## The Kingdom of Jesus.

The human race was His! His work, His generation of formation <note>original: "Bildung"—this refers to the cultural, moral, and spiritual development of humanity over time.</note>; from the very beginning, according to the stages God decreed, He educated and refined it toward higher light and life. **He, the true Light, who enlightens all people who come into this world; He was in the world, and the world was made through Him, and the world knew Him not. He came unto His own:** ᵃ) <note>A paraphrase of John 1:9–11.</note> Creation was His house and His mantle; the human soul was to be His dwelling place and resting spot; and His own—the favorites of His kind and nature, mankind—did not receive Him. But as many as received Him, to them He gave power to become **children of God** of a higher nature through faith in Him, in His power and name, to be born into a kingdom of higher light and life ᵇ).

What acts with more purity, clarity, and speed than a rushing beam of light? Free from the sludge of sluggishness and clinging, restrictive matter: it does not feel itself; it is never in its own way; it cuts through another beam rushing on a different path without refraction, without envy <note>The author uses "without envy" (ohne Neid) to describe how light rays do not compete or displace one another, but coexist and pass through each other perfectly.</note>, and penetrates, enlightens, and warms everything that receives it. Just as it is of a divine nature everywhere and has its origin in every point, the whole

</margin>

### IV.
## The Kingdom of Jesus.

The human race was His! His work, his generation <note>original: "Geschlecht der Bildung"—the author suggests a lineage or race that is actively being formed or educated by God.</note> of formation; from the beginning, according to the stages God decreed, he educated and refined it toward higher light and life. But as many as received him, to them he gave the power to become children of God of a higher nature through faith in him, in his power and name, to be born into a kingdom of higher light and life ᵇ).

What acts with more purity, clarity, and speed than a ray of light as it hastens? Free from the sludge of inertia <note>original: "Träge"—here referring to the physical resistance of matter that slows down the spirit.</note> and from clinging, restraining matter: it does not feel itself; it is never in its own way; it cuts through another ray that hastens by another path without refraction, without envy, and penetrates, enlightens, and warms everything that receives it. # Notes.

## Notes.

“**W**hatsoever bestows wisdom, fertility, the germ of life, and blessing is, according to Zoroaster, the Word, whose immediate presence and “powers he teaches us to invoke beneath every veil. Law and religion are “the noblest veil” a).

“Just as God is light to his creatures in the Word: so too is the “Word the seed of light for the nature of God. Reason is the “divine germ <note>original: "Gotteskeim"—literally the 'seed of God,' implying an innate spiritual potential</note> with which we are born into the world; its purification is meant to “make us into children, into angels, into effective, radiant images of God. In Zoroaster’s language, word, light, seed, and Ormuzd <note>Ormuzd is the Middle Persian name for Ahura Mazda, the supreme creator deity in Zoroastrianism, representing light and truth.</note> “are essentially the development of the Word.”

“In the higher realm, all active beings are messengers of light, angels, “pure instruments, co-workers with God, ruling kings of the Good. “The Father’s will is the word of power toward which they work: Father “of light, the blessing Father is their highest model, and the all- “sustaining, active, power- and life-bestowing Word is the chain “that binds them to him. The angels of God are there as fellow brothers and “fellow warriors, toward whom they strive upward b). That I may become great, strong, holy, “pure, and excellent like an angel!” is the wish on every page here.

It is not our concern here to ask: how is this applied within the system of Oriental religion? But rather to observe that it is the basis for the language of rebirth <note>original: "Wiedergeburtssprache"—referring to the terminology used in the New Testament to describe spiritual transformation</note> in the New Testament, which has taken on such a different form in the minds of mystical monks than it had in its own time and place. Jesus, the true light that enlightens all people who come into this world, dwells within them all in the noblest light of their lives: the reason, the soul, the inner—

<margin>
a) Word, pr. Vol. III. p. 749. 750. Law p. 717. <note>These refer to Anquetil-Duperron’s French translation of the Zend-Avesta</note>
b) Amesha Spenta <note>Amesha Spenta are the six "divine sparks" or benevolent deities in Zoroastrianism</note> p. 627. p. 592-618. Summary of the Theological System p. 732. World p. 762. Purity p. 744-46. Ormuzd.
</margin>

Regarding everything I am now citing, I ask only that one consider the morality of a man who was certainly no fanatic or [conventional] Christian: Spinoza <note>Baruch Spinoza (1632–1677) was a philosopher who argued that God and Nature are one, and that the highest virtue is the use of reason.</note>. One will see that it is the highest morality of reason, which he himself found to be one with Christianity.

inner rays of the Godhead. He comes to all as if into his own property, to make himself more intimately known to them; beneath the veils of the Word of his appearances and blessings, he also came from without to his own. Whoever receives him is given the power, the glory, the **royal privilege** c), to become a child of a higher light and kingdom, which no earthly thing can reach d), though it still works within the earthly. This is the kingdom of the angels, the children of God in the nature of their Father, who only do good and bring happiness; they are powers and images of all-working love, and are undivided and pure. They all cling to the Word of God as the root of their strength: this is the seed within them, the imperishable seed! It is the light-germ of the divine birth, the soul of their soul, the sap of life to be what God is. All do the Father’s will, being his image according to the image of Jesus, bound together through love in the whole world—

<columns>2</columns>

<margin>
c) See Trommius <note>Abraham Trommius, author of a famous Greek-Hebrew concordance</note> and Montfaucon <note>Bernard de Montfaucon, a Benedictine scholar</note> on *authority* <note>original Greek: "εξουσια" (exousia)</note>. I am well aware that it can also simply mean privilege, and if one wishes, it may be left as such here. I am explaining the entire concept of this mode of representation from my source.
</margin>

<margin>
d) The words of John 1:13, *born not of blood, nor of the will of the flesh, nor of the will of man*, have been viewed as pleonasms <note>redundant expressions used for emphasis</note>, which would be strange in a preface full of such high, brief sentences. They are not redundant; rather, through the distinctions of the Oriental mode of thought, they most emphatically exclude every impure or sensual birth. *From blood* <note>original Greek: "αιματα" (aimata); Hebrew: "Damim"</note> is the well-known expression for unclean, even shed blood. (Leviticus 12:2, Exodus 4:25, 26. See L. Cappel, *Observations on the New Testament*, p. 58. In the Septuagint in the later books: 2 Kings 3:28, Chapter 16:7, 8, Chapter 21:1, and frequently elsewhere.) Thus, the problem regarding this word (see Michaelis’s *Introduction to the New Testament*, p. 71) was resolved long ago. A birth *from bloods* <note>original Greek: "εξ αιματων" (ex aimatōn)</note> was the most impure, most shameful, and completely under the
</margin>

->Explanations of the New Testament<-

<column-break/>

power of the Evil One. A woman *in bloods* <note>original Greek: "εν αιμασι" (en aimasi)</note> was, according to Zoroaster, separated from sunlight, air, and all pure elements: The Evil One had created the *bloods*; they had not existed in Paradise, and a woman had to strive for fertility to escape this devilish brood. The will of the flesh and the will of man are purer stages: (*Bundehesh* <note>A Middle Persian encyclopedic work on Zoroastrian cosmogony</note> Vol. III, p. 581: "The seed of the woman is a kind of blood; the seed of the male flows from the marrow and the head like fire, etc." <note>original French: "La semence de la femme c'est une espece de sang; la semence du mâle coule de la moëlle & de la tête comme du feu &c."</note>) but all are still impure and earthly, and entirely incomparable with the birth *from God* <note>original Greek: "εκ θεου" (ek theou)</note> out of the light-seed of heavenly life. One must keep in mind that in the original language, "Word," "Seed," "Light," and "Will" have a relationship to one another that we cannot fully express. John’s contrast thus becomes as full as it could possibly be: however, I do not wish for one to divide it and energetically paraphrase every single word. I am merely showing the origin of the expressions: they were commonly used turns of phrase at that time, which had lost their distinctness.

It contrasts physical, "impure" birth with the spiritual "divine birth" facilitated by the Word.</summary>

<header>* ( 0 ) *</header>

working together and undivided like the light — an all-encompassing, invisible kingdom of pure thoughts, free actions, and blessed impulses. — Language is pure like light and, like light, it seeks to make us pure, effective, kind, and blessed, to animate us as angels and stewards of God — truly not a mysticism of "rebirth-feelings" found in some dark, inactive chasm of death.

Therefore, the influence of Jesus is not contrary to reason: from the beginning of the world, wherever a pure spark of reason welled up, it was a ray from his undivided sea that flows through all beings. It beckons everywhere toward the same purification into light and life, and thus toward his kingdom.

This is where the great assembly in the Light-Kingdom of Jesus points, which Paul calls "fullness" <note>original Greek: "pleroma"</note>, the "united heaven and earth," and to which we are meant to come even while here on earth. "Not that terrifying assembly on Sinai: you enter into a Light-Kingdom, into a community of angels; but they are brothers, fellow-helpers, the firstborn, to whom Jesus has brought you, and He is the Head, the Judge—that is, the King and Center of all!" In his prayers and invocations, Zoroaster <note>The ancient Persian prophet, also known as Zarathustra</note> is always within this brilliant, living heavenly assembly, as he calls it e), to which all angels, the highest princes of heaven, all pure human souls, and even his own soul (if it is pure) belong. This was the undeniably dominant way of thinking of that entire era among both Jews and Heathens <note>non-Christians</note> in their own respective ways, which the Apostles utilize and ennoble for their own purpose. To them, the kingdom of Jesus is a Kingdom of Light, a realm of action, dominion, and blessing in God and his omnipresent Word f). Can reason conceive of a higher, indivisible existence, a purer light and life?

---

e) Brilliant, living assembly, etc. Vol. III. *Jescht* <note>original French: "Assemblée brillante, vivante"; refers to the Yashts, hymns from the Zoroastrian Avesta</note>. *Intelligible world* <note>original Greek: "Κοσμος νοητος" (Kosmos noetos)</note> among the Alexandrians <note>Platonic philosophers in Alexandria who blended Greek and Eastern thought</note>.

f) Instead of the countless passages I could cite, let one suffice, which has been terribly mangled even in the wording of the text because it was not understood. "The woman shall stand and pray in the assembly with modesty and discipline — for the sake of the angels!" <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 11:10</note> can be nothing other than this idea. Through prayer, they imagined heaven was opened: the assembly of Christians was (Hebrews 12:22-23 ff.) a gathering of pure heavenly hosts, a luminous assembly which they imagined, as we shall soon see, as warriors. Hence the *authority* <note>original Greek: "εξουσια" (exousia)</note>, the armor upon the head: the holy covering of modesty and discipline: "you are praying in an assembly of angels!"

### V.
### The Enemies of Jesus's Kingdom.

If the design <note>The German word *Entwurf* suggests a blueprint or foundational plan for humanity's destiny.</note> for the human race was that it should be purified through Jesus, moving from its lowly earthly existence toward higher light and life, then the dross <note>*Schlacken*: the stony waste matter separated from metals during smelting—a metaphor for spiritual impurity.</note> of the world—darkness, impurity, filth, sloth, and the curse—are his enemies. They stand in opposition to the light and truth, the activity and love, and the beneficence and blessing that constitute his kingdom.

Just as this kingdom of his, in its all-encompassing universality, works from the spiritual person upward through all classes and orders of the invisible world; so too does the kingdom of his enemies descend to the deepest darkness, dwelling in the powers and thrones of lies, malice, misery, sloth, cunning, and the rage of evil against everything that is good.

The kingdom of light is eternally at war with this night: and Jesus, the head of his members <note>Referring to the "Body of Christ," where individual believers are considered "members" or limbs through which the head acts.</note> through whom he performs all his works, has made it his design to destroy the gates of hell and to overcome the primal foundations of evil. He has accomplished this and stands as the head of his kingdom of light, so that everyone following him may arm themselves with prayer, the Word, and faith—the organs of his power. Even the most stubborn, deepest evil is cast away: darkness is transfigured into light. This is a high doctrine of godliness <term>Gottseligkeit: a state of living in harmony with God's will; true piety.</term> in its practical application.

## Remarks.

It is a principle of the new philology <note>Philology is the historical study of language and literature; here it refers to the Enlightenment-era critical study of religious texts.</note>: “the entire mess of devil-fables of the Jews is solely from Chaldea.” <note>Chaldea refers to the region of ancient Babylon, which scholars of this period believed was the source of many Jewish supernatural concepts following the Babylonian Exile.</note> If that is so, then Chaldea is at least the first key to the language on this point. And then it follows immediately that a great series of sayings, where the devil has been delicately exorcized away, still signify him nonetheless according to the concepts of the Orient (no matter how much we might feel ashamed of it). Let us cite a series of his names and predicates, with which the *Zend-Avesta* <note>The sacred scripture of Zoroastrianism, an ancient Persian religion centered on the struggle between the creator God and the spirit of evil.</note> is full in long litanies a).

“Adversary, Enemy, Prowler, Roamer <note>original: "qui rode pour frapper, qui court pour dechirer"—who prowls to strike, who runs to tear apart.</note> appearing now as a serpent, now as a wolf, now as a lion, etc.” The Adversary, who in 1 Peter 5:8 <note>Original text cites 5:7.</note> prowls around like a lion, is just like Satan, the one who roams through the land in the Book of Job; according to the imagery, then, he is not a persecutor of Christians, but Satan himself. They were likewise meant to resist him through the Word of God and prayer.

“Power-holder b) that is, King of Death, Father of Impurity, Sickness, and Plagues, who rules in evil and in misfortune as if in his own kingdom. From the very beginning, he spread himself into all elements, wanting to poison and ruin everything; he seduced the first humans, brought sin and death into the world, and generated diseases and loathsome vices. He rules over deserts, wastes, and corpses (Chief, King of Death <note>original: "Chef, Roi de la mort"</note>), the Chaldean Asmodi, whose name was also transformed into Aschmog.” <note>Asmodi (Asmodeus) is a demon known from the Book of Tobit; Aschmog refers to "Aeshma," the Zoroastrian demon of wrath.</note> The Power-holder, that is, the King of Death, the Devil, whom Jesus came to destroy (Hebrews 2:14), is thus not the holy, sinless idol of the Angel of Death that our interpreters prefer to take from the later Rabbis—simply to portray Jesus as following a mere delusion—

b) A modern writer has believed that Peter, when he wrote his first letter, had stolen from his "dear brother Paul" because he calls the authorities <term>ἐξουσίας ὑπερεχούσας</term> <note>original Greek: "exousias hyperechousas"—referring to "governing authorities" or "higher powers."</note>; in Hellenism, it is a common expression for angels, humans, and devils, which shall be explained elsewhere.

Murderer from the very beginning: “He has always struck: <note>original: "ce Daroudj, qui frappe toujours" — this Druj (demon), who always strikes</note> he desires to strike, as a devil: he runs to tear apart <note>original: "cet Ahriman, plein de mort" — this Ahriman (the spirit of evil), full of death</note>. His people are always called the people that destroy, break, corrupt, torment <note>original: "Dews, peuple qui frappe, brise, corrompt &c." — Daevas (demons), a people who strike, break, corrupt, etc.</note>.” — The blows of the angel of Satan who strikes Paul <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 12:7, where Paul speaks of a "messenger of Satan" sent to buffet or strike him.</note> are therefore neither gout nor migraine. In the Revelation of John, the expression of the plague-angels occurs so often, and yet neither the rivers, the trees, nor the third part of the sun that are struck <note>A reference to the apocalyptic judgments in the Book of Revelation.</note> have ever suffered from a headache. In Zoroaster’s writings, the expression appears a hundred times: he strikes fire, water, trees, all elements. "The Evil One will not touch him!" <note>1 John 5:18.</note> is precisely the same image.

->* * *<-

I could continue for a long time with explanations of this kind; more will follow elsewhere. But what is the result of all this? Is it a "fable of the times," a Chaldean fiction? "Our philosophy," it is said, "has proven these fairy tales to be false!" — I do not find that to be the case at all. Our philosophy has nothing to do with devils or with invisible forces: <term>natural science (Naturlehre)</term> and <term>psychology (Seelenlehre)</term> merely organize appearances and bring them under certain laws, which they then name according to their kind. No clever philosopher, however, has ever ventured to know what *force* itself is, or to categorize active beings according to their internal laws and states. We all use words like "inertia," "gravity," "cold," "matter," or "body"; but no one knows what these things are in their innermost essence. We see appearances and speak in generalities. Therefore, at the very least, nothing is accomplished when a modern thinker "quite properly and Christianly hands the Devil over to the philosophers" <note>A sarcastic jab at contemporary theologians who used Enlightenment philosophy to explain away the supernatural elements of the Bible.</note> from the field of <term>exegesis (Exegetik)</term> <note>The critical explanation or interpretation of scripture.</note>. They can do nothing with him: if he acts under what they call "laws of nature" (and he must always do so! he stands under God!), then to them he is simply a piece of the "best of all possible worlds," and they merely demonstrate his effects.

It is also possible that certain appearances, which were attributed to demons back then, have ceased—be that as it may. There are also fewer earthquakes now; can there not, therefore, have been more in the past? If there is peace now, could a war not have preceded it, so that we might now enjoy the victory? And if God really directed the times, the appearances, and the enlightenment of the ages toward the appearance...

...[order]ing of Jesus: could and must he not also (from wherever it might come) introduce concepts for this? To show the doctrine of the effects of darkness precisely then in the greatest light, or rather in the most sorrowful experiences? The greatest light was in the world, and there—as Jesus himself said so often—the powers of darkness revolted and fought until the very end, the thickest shadows against the greatest light.

No one can deny that all of this in the <term>New Testament (N. T.)</term> is stated clearly, as historical fact, and not merely as <term>metaphorical clothing (Einkleidung)</term> <note>original: "Einkleidung." In 18th-century theology, this referred to the idea that biblical truths were "clothed" in local myths or cultural idioms.</note>. Were Christ and the Apostles peddlers of fables—and such crude fables, upon which they build so much? Or must he who explicitly says that he came from heaven and teaches from heaven not see further into the system of the mixture of good and evil in the world than we, who understand not the slightest bit of it? What do we know of our earth? Of the formation of the earthly? Of the whole invisible realm of forces, from which alone the visible can even be conceived? What do we know of the whole great machine into which our small wheel of visibility engages? Of the chain on which this small, imperceptible link hangs? We stand on the first step above the animal, enjoying <term>reason (Vernunft)</term> in its first germ—*there are more things in heav'n and earth than are dream't of in your philosophy* <note>A famous quote from Shakespeare’s Hamlet (Act 1, Scene 5), used by the author to critique the limits of Enlightenment rationalism.</note>—and we wish to pass judgment on the infinite and the realm of invisibility! —

Furthermore, it seems to me that our age is most unsuited for experiences and investigations of this kind. We see and investigate only what is perceptible to the senses, what falls under number and measure; where things can be handled <term>mechanically</term>, we are the greatest masters. Experiences of the <term>soul (Seele)</term> have long ceased to be our sphere, because we all live in a uniform, artificial state, full of compulsion and rigid method. The invisible world is finally closed to us because we think mechanically, live physically with bodies, and calculate the concepts of good and evil themselves only according to convenience, number, and weight. We do not look with intuition, much less do we behold spirits and active <term>primal forces (Urkräfte)</term>; we look through a single pair of glasses and speak in generalities. — If an apostle, a prophet of God, an angel who sees through spirits were to appear: [one wonders] whether he would [not also remark] everywhere upon the moral coldness—

<meta>catchword: VI. Redemption [Erlö-]</meta>

...Should these bearers of power and truth, whom we praise, content themselves so with mere words, and not show an entirely different sense in the realm of good and evil than we do?

Certainly, this doctrine would be useful to us if we used it as the <term>New Testament (N. T.)</term> applies it: Morally. It is not as a modern hypothesis claims—that the devils in the New Testament appear as indifferent beings, like the Angel of Death among the Rabbis and elsewhere among the peoples of the East. In the teaching of Jesus, they are the actual symbol and the most stubborn primal drives of evil. Wherever Jesus sees an offensive, carnal thought, even in his [disciple] Petrus, he speaks: “Get thee behind me, Satan, thou art an offense unto me: for thou savourest not the things that be of God, but those that be of men” <note>A reference to Matthew 16:23. The author argues that Jesus uses the term "Satan" to personify thoughts that oppose divine will.</note>—where we would otherwise call it a righteous, clever, friendly, if only somewhat fearful piece of advice. The Son of God came to Earth so that he might destroy the works of the devil. He has broken the gates of the abyss, and Christians follow him in overcoming it.

The New Testament, and Paul in particular, also uses the favorite imagery of the East here: the holy clothing of the soul as a symbolic armor, where everything reminds them of the struggle against evil. Even with <term>Zoroaster</term> <note>The ancient Persian prophet also known as Zarathustra.</note>, this was the holy language. “The belt, the precious pledge of religion, was a battle-belt: the language of prayers and religious duties was martial: man, as he stood there in his entirety, was a skirmisher in the light against darkness and evil.” <margin>1) T. III, p. 616. Kosti 713. and throughout.</margin> See there the language of Paul, which must be explained neither by Roman military art, nor can his garment of light—his “armor of light” <margin>m) Armor of light <note>original: "Οπλα φωτος" (Hopla photos)</note>. See Michaelis, Introduction to the N.T., p. 73. The answer was already found by Jablonski, Prolegomena CXXXII.</margin>—be explained by baptismal robes. It was the widespread language of the Sanctuary; and also the unexplained “authority” <note>original: "εξουσια" (exousia). Reference to 1 Corinthians 11:10, where women are told to have "power" or a covering on their heads.</note> that the “woman must have on her head for the sake of the angels, the heavenly assembly,” originates from this. The holy covering (<term>Penom</term>) <note>The *Paitidana* or *Penom* is a piece of white cloth worn by Zoroastrian priests or practitioners over the mouth and nose to prevent "pollution" of sacred elements.</note>, the protection against evil, without which they were not permitted to eat, let alone pray, is here—without any superstition regarding demons—the covering against vice and insolence: the veil. “Let the woman have the holy covering of modesty upon her head: you pray in an assembly of angels.”

<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion of spiritual armor and the struggle against evil, referencing the Persian prophet Zoroaster. ### VI.
### Redemption of the World through Jesus.

If the human race was created for salvation in Jesus: then in the design of an eternity, in which everything is the present, he became the guarantor and executor of this plan. He stood, as it were, for his race: heaven and earth were to be reconciled—that is, united—and humans and angels were to become a brotherly race in the will, in the grace, and in the good pleasure of the Father. He stood for this plan.

It is no prophetic office, nor church reform, nor a good outward example, and even less a meaningless sacrificial ceremony or a purposeless legalistic masquerade <note>The original German uses "Mummerei," a derogatory term for hollow, theatrical rituals that lack spiritual substance.</note> that could accomplish this; otherwise, the Scripture would speak nonsense from one end to the other. Sin, imperfection, and positive decay were present. Darkness obscured the Divinity from unhappy creatures: it had to be lifted, the source of higher salvation <term>Seligkeit</term> <note>A central concept in German theology referring to both "blessedness" and "eternal salvation"—a state of perfect happiness in God.</note> had to be actively opened to them, and they had to be rescued from their opposing evil through a great, universal power—behold, a work of action like creation itself! The new, invisibly progressing higher creation; the creation of spirits into the images of God! Jesus became the Savior, the actual Rescuer, Liberator, Physician, and Bringer of Bliss to the world a).

And the path to this, according to the high concept of the Scripture, is not any individual, self-grown virtue, but faith. At this stage of straying powers, what virtue was there to come to God, to reconcile oneself with God? No way remained but the one and only, the best design that God chose, which the Scripture unfolds, and which all the members of Jesus <note>This refers to the "Body of Christ," the community of believers.</note> follow most intimately!

---

a) Matthew 1:21. John 3:17. Luke 1:68, 69. John 17:19. 2 Corinthians 5:21, 15. Ephesians 5:25-27. and so forth.

<summary>This page defines redemption as a "new creation" of the human spirit, arguing that Jesus acts as the eternal guarantor who restores the connection between humanity and the divine. It rejects the idea that outward rituals or human virtue alone can achieve salvation, pointing instead to the necessity of faith within God's universal plan.</summary>
<keywords>redemption, Jesus Christ, salvation, faith, divine plan, reconciliation, new creation, grace</keywords>

<meta>The previous page concluded by defining Jesus as the "Savior" and "Bringer of Bliss" who carries out a divine plan for the "new creation" of human spirits. This page, titled "Notes," serves as a targeted rebuttal against Enlightenment-era rationalists who sought to minimize the personal and religious necessity of Jesus's role in redemption.</meta>

# Notes.

There is a current teaching <note>The author is likely criticizing Enlightenment "Rationalism," which argued that religion should be based on reason alone and often viewed the specific mechanics of Jesus's sacrifice as unnecessary or illogical.</note> claiming "that the concept of the redemption of Jesus does not belong to religion at all: rather, it is a relationship between God and Jesus that one can simply leave to them"—surely, greater nonsense has never been uttered. The All-Sufficient One <note>A theological term for God, who lacks nothing and needs nothing from outside Himself.</note> was in no need of reconciliation or ransom; and if this work is not meant to concern human beings, then it would only concern the devils to whom Jesus paid a ransom, or no one at all. "Or," it is argued, "everyone should be allowed to imagine whatever they want about this!"—And what, then, is the purpose of the Bible? Why the New Testament, in which this doctrine is the very foundation, discussed with the most extraordinary diligence? "Or the doctrine is meant to be so unintelligible!"—and that, if one reads without prejudice, is the greatest falsehood: one simply must not think they know everything better in advance, otherwise, they should not read at all.

It is clear from the entire New Testament that Jesus is regarded as the first active source of purification, liberation, and the blessing of the world; not merely "as if" or in a symbolic way, but in the most effective sense. He is revealed before the creation of the world as the author of this design, since the salvation <term>Seligkeit</term> <note>While often translated as "salvation," *Seligkeit* specifically implies a state of supreme, heavenly happiness and eternal blessedness.</note> of humanity was resolved in him. This work is intended to be the active development of the human race: the path to this is the most active work of all intuitive, encompassing powers of the soul—namely, Faith <term>Glaube</term>—which must surely precede every determination in the natural world, and without which we would have no natural world. Thus, Jesus has now opened a spiritual world to us; and regardless of how many images and frames this is expressed in—anyone who remains stuck on the setting, the wrappings, and the casings is not worthy of the gemstone itself. Sin and salvation have many names in the Scripture; everyone can see that the work of Jesus had to relate to the imagery of the name used each time. Sometimes he has washed away, sometimes destroyed, sometimes crucified with himself, put to death, or buried; sometimes he is portrayed as a lamb, sometimes a friend, sometimes a guarantor, and a redeeming brother, a hero, a physician, a shepherd, a priest, or a king: but through it all, he remains the

<meta>catchword: Mediator,</meta>

<summary>This page defends the central importance of Jesus's redemption against rationalist critics, arguing that his role is not a private matter between him and God but the essential source of human spiritual liberation. The author explains that while the Bible uses various metaphors for this process—such as a physician or a sacrificial lamb—the core truth is the opening of a spiritual world through faith.</summary>
<keywords>redemption, rationalism, biblical metaphors, faith, divinity of Jesus, salvation, purification, mediator</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>Continuity: The previous page concluded by defining Jesus as the "Savior" and "Bringer of Bliss" who carries out a divine plan for the "new creation" of human spirits. This page continues that discussion by clarifying the specific titles and metaphors used for Jesus, arguing against overly legalistic or literal interpretations of atonement.</meta>

Mediator, Savior, Bringer of Bliss, Helper, a) by whatever images this might be expressed? Whoever stops only at the color <note>i.e., the surface-level or literal imagery</note>, whoever in thinking of *hilasmos* <note>original: *ἱλασμος*; atonement or propitiation</note> only ever thinks of the *hilasterion* <note>original: *ἱλαστηριον*; the "mercy seat" or place of atonement</note> and the bowl of blood that is sprayed into the face of an angry man; whoever, regarding "ransom," only has in mind the miser and the tyrant from whom the galley slave must be bought: that person is a child playing with colors, or a villain who, by picking at the paint, destroys the whole painting. To whom was the human race ransomed or bailed out? To God or to Satan? The first, Scripture does not say; on the contrary, we were reconciled with God, saved *unto* God. And truly! To bring a ransom to Satan, as some good Church Fathers <note>Refers to the "Ransom Theory of Atonement" held by figures like Origen and Gregory of Nyssa, which suggested Christ’s death was a ransom paid to the Devil.</note> claim, would be a worthy business for God indeed! People believe they have performed wonders when they have, for example, refuted a Grotius <note>Hugo Grotius (1583–1645), a Dutch jurist who wrote a defense of the satisfaction of Christ, applying legal concepts to theology.</note> regarding his satisfaction theory based on natural and international law! That legal historian—for whose philology, incidentally, I have the greatest respect—wanted to refute his own treatise in his old age and yet got no further! From the lawyer Tertullian <note>Tertullian (c. 155–220 AD) was a North African theologian trained in law who introduced many legal terms into Latin Christianity.</note> onward, throughout all the feudal ages, people could not bring enough poor concepts of fiefdom and the courtroom to clothe the doctrine:

<column-break/>

a) Mediator <note>original: *μεσιτης* (*mesitēs*)</note> means an intermediary, an active friend perhaps, who actually procures something for us, whatever it may be: thus Jesus is the Mediator between God and men, so that God's grace—that is, the source of a new bliss—may actively flow onto humanity. Savior <note>original: *σωτηρ* (*sōtēr*)</note> is a real helper, rescuer, deliverer, whether he be a friend, a hero, or a physician (see Zorn, *Sacred Works* <note>original: *Zorn. Opp. sacr.* (Opuscula Sacra) by Peter Zorn (1682–1746).</note>, Vol. II, p. 575) and Benefactor <note>original: *ευεργετης* (*euergetēs*)</note>, whether he be king or slave. Head <note>originals: *αρχηγος* (*archēgos*), *αρχη* (*archē*), *κεφαλη* (*kephalē*)</note> is an active author, the first source—of whatever it may be: lifeblood, light, or the way. The phrase "before God" <note>originals: *ενωπιον* (*enōpion*), *εναντιον του θεου* (*enantion tou theou*)</note> is not merely a legal term meaning "before the judgment seat," but a Hebrew idiom for the most active relationships and influences. See Vorst, *Sacred Philology* <note>original: *Vorst. Phil. S.* likely referring to Johannes Vorstius's *Philologia Sacra* (1658).</note>, p. 393, etc., etc. For since God is always and eternally the fundamental source of all existence, all power, and the bliss of the human race, from whom every sin actively distances us: so it must always be the primary expression of Jesus's work of redemption that he brings us back from that state of distance—which is itself an ever-increasing punishment—and brings us into the active grace and fellowship of God. The sum total of how all this became possible and real is called the reconciliation, the redemption of Jesus!—truly the worthiest and truest concept, without which I understand nothing of the state of my race!

<summary>This page argues that the titles given to Jesus (Mediator, Savior, etc.) should be understood as relational and restorative rather than strictly legalistic or literal. The author critiques historical theologians like Tertullian and Grotius for "clothing" the doctrine of redemption in overly rigid legal and feudal concepts.</summary>
<keywords>redemption, atonement, Mediator, Savior, Grotius, Tertullian, legalism, reconciliation, philology, Greek terminology</keywords>

...tion: one may test and separate them however one wishes; but the doctrine itself is the essential concept of Scripture.

"Or is all of this merely the sacrificial language of the Jews! Adventurous, festive sacrificial language, and nothing more!" And even that is not the case! Sacrifice is not the only word for the work of Jesus; let alone the primary or unique one, for it is an image! But an image without meaning is nothing. I hope at another time to show the dignity and significance of this image, especially in the relationship of humans to God; here, I can make much clear and manifest from other modes of representation that are neither judicial nor sacrificial. The Jewish sacrificial language itself had to be so stretched, transformed, and Chaldeanized <note>A reference to the way Jewish thought and language evolved, particularly after the Babylonian/Chaldean exile, becoming more abstract or symbolic.</note> that it only expressed the concept of the work of Jesus from afar, and entire books required no sacrificial imagery at all. "What a Jewish letter!" people cry out, for example, regarding the **Epistle to the Hebrews**, and I would like to shout: no letter is *less* Jewish than this one! The Apostle truly struggles with the parables, runs into the most opposing allegories, pairs **Melchizedek**, **Moses**, and **Aaron** and all the prophets, only to bring out with their helping ideas that which Jesus was—completely incomparable to all of them. He entangles and, here and elsewhere, with a fiery mind often tears only a single feature from history and rushes onward. Then the philosophical, and what is recently even worse, the rhetorical sophists come upon him, paraphrase, paste over, and—as they say—bring the images into line according to their own bad tropical <note>In this context, "tropical" refers to "tropes" or figurative language. The author is critiquing those who try to force poetic biblical metaphors into dry, literal prose.</note> taste; they make them crawl where they once flew, and where the images will not crawl, they—the paraphrasts themselves!—crawl in between! Poor Paul, what can become of you? A **Benson-Clarke-Taylor**-ish <note>Refers to George Benson, Samuel Clarke, and John Taylor, 18th-century English theologians known for their rationalistic paraphrases of the New Testament which the author finds reductive.</note> paraphrase!

Rest well, **Word of God**! Living seed (as our great **Luther** says, who sacrificed himself for this doctrine). Even under snow and ice, endure your time in the earth. Your awakening spring is coming, and you shall turn green!

"But shouldn't: *Jesus has reconciled earth and heaven!* mean approximately as much as: *he has united the Jews and the Gentiles?*"

### VII. In

...there were [those] who dreamed that there were two lawgivers, Moses and Christ, who, as it were, established human <term>civil orders</term> <note>original: "πολιτείας" (politeias) — Greek for the administrative form of a city-state or a way of life governed by laws.</note> in which people might live in a civil manner. Nor did Mohammed <note>The author refers to the Prophet Muhammad, viewing his teachings through the lens of 16th-century Reformation polemics.</note> believe that Christ was sent for any other reason than to bring laws of a kind that would join the Gentiles to the Jews— — Furthermore, the Gospel teaches a far different righteousness, etc., etc. c)

### VII.
### In the fullness of time, Jesus appeared.

Out of love God gave his Son, and not out of anger ᵃ). He, the all-sufficient <note>original: "Allgenugsame" — a theological term referring to God's self-sufficiency, implying He is complete in Himself and lacks nothing.</note>, all-good, and unchangeable one, was reconciled when he chose and created the human race in Jesus; he created it for blessedness! <note>original: "Seligkeit" — refers to a state of spiritual salvation and eternal happiness.</note> His will was the eternal well-being of his creatures. Now, however, the human race was also to be truly reconciled with God—that is, brought back to him, and united with him out of its depth of sin and death. To this end, God gave his Son! An image of the highest devotion and fatherly love, and of his earnest will for their recovery.

The eternal Son of God gave himself up, became their physician, redeemer, and savior, and—so that he could be everything—their brother. The highest act of self-renunciation <note>original: "Entäußerung" — a term often relating to *kenosis*, or the act of Christ emptying himself of divine form to assume human nature.</note>, sacrifice, and the most active love!

In his humanity, Jesus became an image of God, and as it were, the seed and focal point <note>original: "Kraftpunkt" — literally a "point of power" or "nucleus."</note> for working light and life into his race. We saw his glory, a glory of the only-begotten, full of grace and truth—in him shone the radiant, kind, and human-blessing countenance, the source of all knowledge and delight. He spoke heavenly truth, worked heavenly powers, bestowed the blessedness of heaven; he was the visible love of the Father for the salvation of mankind. This he was in the most vivid simplicity, lowliness, and humility: living and dying, his life was life, light, and love for all the world.

<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page’s focus on Jesus as the manifestation of divine love and the "focal point" of life, this page moves into a specific theological defense of Christ’s divinity based on the writings of the Apostle Paul, specifically addressing the nature of Christ's pre-existence and his "emptying" of himself into human form.</meta>

# Notes.

Paul says: let each one be of the same mind as Jesus was, who, though he was in the form of God, did not believe he had to hold onto it as booty, or believe he had to clothe himself with equality with God as if with a plunder; but rather emptied himself <note>original: "äußerte sich selbst" — likely a variation of "entäußerte," referring to the theological concept of *kenosis*, where Christ sets aside his divine attributes to become human.</note> and so forth; and everyone sees that it would be nonsense if Jesus had not possessed this form, this equality with God, before he became man. It is therefore the most barren mutilation if it is claimed: "he, who also *could* have appeared in the authority of God!" He actually appeared in that authority, appeals to it even in the midst of his form as a servant, and the Gospel of John actually has the purpose of depicting him always in this authority and as one who appeals to it.

Glory, or the "form of God," is for the Seventy <note>The "Seventy" (Septuagint) refers to the ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note> the majesty, the fiery radiant shell in which God revealed himself when he did not appear as the Angel of the Lord. Jesus was that too, says the Apostle; he had appeared in the form and likeness of God (because God could not reveal himself essentially); but not now. He became a messenger, servant, and minister in wretched obedience unto death. That is what the verse says, and only now does it strike home! Jesus becomes the greatest model of self-sacrifice and humility ᵃ).

<meta>catchword: Who</meta>

---

<columns>2</columns>

ᵃ) The expression "to hold as a robbery!" which some, in a rush of ignorance, have regarded as a Cretism <note>A term referring to a linguistic quirk or, pejoratively, a "deception" associated with the ancient Cretans.</note>, jealousy, or the like, is either literally the Hebrew <term>shalal (booty/prey)</term> as found in Jeremiah 38:2 and 39:18: "his soul shall be to him for a prey," that is, he will rescue it, hold onto it, and preserve it (which in Hellenistic Greek is <term>heurein (to find/attain)</term>, to find as a plunder, as opposed to <term>apolesai psychēn (to lose one's life)</term>). And then it stands in contrast to the Apostle's "emptying" or "denial" (<term>kenosis</term>).

<column-break/>

Or it is the common Greek <term>harpagma (booty/thing seized)</term>, also used a few times by others as <term>harpagmos</term>, meaning robbery or booty with which one adorns oneself. In either sense, there is no talk of "laying aside" divinity, divine authority, or the like; nor of jealousy and greed. What one already possesses, what no one can steal from us—of that one is neither jealous nor greedy.

<summary>This page provides a scholarly and theological defense of Christ’s pre-existent divinity, arguing that Jesus did not "seize" equality with God but possessed it before choosing the humility of human life. It offers a detailed linguistic analysis of the biblical terms for "robbery" and "emptying" to refute contemporary rationalist interpretations.</summary>
<keywords>Christology, Philippians 2:5-8, Kenosis, pre-existence of Christ, Septuagint, Greek linguistics, Hebrew idioms, humility, divinity of Jesus</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page’s discussion of Christ’s "emptying" of himself (kenosis), the author now argues that only such a divine-human being could bridge the gap between God and humanity. The text transitions from Paul's theology to the Gospel of John, examining how John’s language—specifically "the Word became flesh"—resonated with both Jewish tradition and Pythagorean philosophy.</meta>

Who was it then, if we—according to the preceding sections—have any conception of God’s design or the work of redemption, which could reconcile mankind to God (that is, open up the full abundance of blessing to them again), other than He whom the Scripture names? No man! No angel! as it explicitly says according to the nature of the matter. — As soon as one has the true, high conception of the connection and the nature of the work, the design, and the purpose: then one stands at the proper vantage point for everything. At that point, nothing will seem more foolish to us than that fatal "absolutism" <note>In 18th-century theology, "absolutism" referred to the idea that God acts out of sheer arbitrary will rather than a rational or moral order; the author argues here that Christ's role was a necessary part of a logical divine plan.</note> which has been applied here, as everywhere, to the highest work into which even angels long to look — excellently refuted in Ernesti’s *Defense of Divine Will* <note>original: "vindic. arbitr. div." referring to Johann August Ernesti’s *Vindiciae arbitrii divini in oecononmia salutis*, a theological treatise from 1734.</note> (Theological Works p. 190.)

*The Word became flesh.*) A modern writer has abandoned this "harsh expression"; however, even to the Pythagoreans <note>b) Followers of the philosopher Pythagoras, who often viewed the body as a vessel for the soul or the divine.</note> man—whose inner word they revered as a deity inhabiting the earthly temple—was already the *incarnate mind, a spiritual vessel, a destined instrument* <note>original: "νῦς σεσαρκωμένος, ἀγγεῖον πνευματικόν, ὄργανον ὡρισμένον"</note>; and John, for his higher Gospel, could use no stronger or more worthy word.

*And dwelt among Us.*) Those same admirers of human reason, along with others, had already so sanctified the expressions "tabernacle," "dwelling," and "temple of the body" <note>original: "σκηνος, οἴκημα, ναὸς" (skenos, oikema, naos)</note> as a vessel for the deity, that John—without even needing to think of the *Shekinah* <note>The "Shekinah" is the dwelling or settled presence of the divine glory of God in Jewish tradition.</note> or the Feast of Tabernacles—could again find nothing more worthy for his higher word. Among the Church Fathers, "tabernacle" or "tent of God" simply signifies the body of Jesus; and one recognizes John's favorite trait, his subtle double-meanings <term>dilogies: literary devices where a word or phrase carries two simultaneous meanings, a common feature in the Gospel of John</term>, such as: "Destroy this temple! — but he was speaking of the temple of his body."

### Annotations.

Paul says: let each be of the same mind as Jesus was, who, though he was in the form of God, did not think it necessary to cling to it as booty, or to clothe himself with equality with God as if with plunder; but emptied himself, and so on. <note>The author is paraphrasing Philippians 2:5-7, a central text in "Kenotic theology," which discusses Christ setting aside his divine glory to become human.</note> Anyone can see that it would be nonsense if Jesus had not possessed this form, this equality with God, before he became man. It is therefore the starkest mutilation of the text when people claim it should mean: "he, who could also have appeared in the authority of God!" In truth, he *did* appear in that authority, and he appealed to it even in the midst of his form as a servant; indeed, the Gospel of John essentially has the purpose of depicting him as always being in this authority and appealing to it.

"Glory," or the "form of God," is defined by the Seventy <note>The "Seventy" (or Septuagint) refers to the oldest Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note> as the Majesty—the fiery veil of radiance in which God revealed himself when he did not appear as the Angel of the Lord. The Apostle says that Jesus was also this; He had appeared in the form and equality of God (because God could not reveal Himself in His essential essence); but He did not remain so. He became a messenger, a servant, a minister in wretched obedience unto death. This is what the passage says, and only now does its meaning strike home! Jesus becomes the greatest model of self-sacrifice and humility a).

<meta>catchword: Who</meta>

---

<columns>2</columns>

a) The expression "to hold as plunder!" <note>original: "Raub halten!"</note> which some, in a rush of ignorance, have viewed as Cretism <note>The author likely refers to "Kretismos"—the act of lying or deception associated with the ancient Cretans—suggesting some scholars thought the language was deceptive or idiomatic.</note>, jealousy, or the like, is either literally the Hebrew <term>שלל</term> <note>original: "Shalal," meaning booty or prey.</note> Jeremiah 38:2; 39:18: "his soul shall be to him for a prey," which is to say, he will save it, hold it fast, and preserve it. (In Hellenistic Greek, this is <term>ευρειν</term>, "to find," specifically to find as a prize, in contrast to <term>απολεσαι ψυχην</term>, "to lose one's life.") In this sense, it stands in opposition to the Apostle's concept of "laying aside" or "denial" (<term>κενωσις</term>) <note>Kenosis, the "emptying" of Christ.</note>.

<column-break/>

Or it is the common Greek <term>αρπαγμα</term> <note>Harpagma: a thing seized.</note>—also written a few times by others as <term>αρπαγμος</term> <note>Harpagmos: the act of seizing.</note>—meaning plunder or booty with which one adorns oneself. In either sense, there is no talk here of "laying aside" the Godhead, divine authority, or the like; nor of jealousy or greed. What one already possesses, and what no one can steal from us, is something one is neither jealous of nor greedy for.

**The Word became Flesh.**) A certain modern writer has abandoned this "harsh" expression; but already for the Pythagoreans <margin>b) Secundus's Sentences in Gale's *Collection of Physical and Ethical Mythology*, page 636. (A response to Michaelis's *Introduction to the New Testament*, page 70.) The word **flesh** <note>original Greek: "σαρξ" (sarx)</note> is Hebrew <note>The author suggests that while the language is Greek, the underlying concept follows the Hebrew "basar," referring to human frailty.</note>.</margin> man—whose inner "Word" they revered as a deity inhabiting an earthly temple—was the **incarnate mind**, the **spiritual vessel**, the **living instrument** <note>original: "νυς σεσαρκωμενος, αγγειον πνευματικον, οργανον ωσεομενον"</note>. Thus, John could use no stronger or worthier word for his higher Gospel.

**And dwelt among Us.**) Those same admirers of human reason, and others like them, had already so sanctified the expressions "tabernacle," "dwelling," and "temple of the body" <note>original Greek: "σκηνος, οικημα, ναος" (skenos, oikema, naos)</note> as a vessel for the deity, that John—even without thinking of the Shekhinah <note>The *Shekhinah* is the Hebrew term for the dwelling or settling divine presence of God.</note> or the Feast of Tabernacles—could again use nothing worthier for his higher Word. Among the Church Fathers, "tabernacle" or "tent of God" simply means the body of Jesus; and one recognizes John's favorite trait, his subtle double-meanings, in this: "destroy this temple!—but he was speaking of the temple of his body."

<summary>The author argues that only a divine being, not a man or angel, could achieve redemption and explores how the Gospel of John uses philosophical terms like "flesh" and "temple" to describe the Incarnation.</summary>
<keywords>Redemption, Johann August Ernesti, Incarnation, John 1:14, Pythagorean philosophy, Shekhinah, Temple of the Body, Philology</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the theological exploration of Christ’s nature, moving from the concept of the Word becoming flesh to a deep linguistic analysis of "glory," "fullness," and "grace" as understood through the lens of Near Eastern light-metaphorics rather than Western legalistic or mechanical definitions.</meta>

->* ( 0 ) *<-

**“We beheld his glory, a glory of the Only-begotten, full of grace and truth!”** These words mean nothing less than a sincere, polite benevolence—as if Jesus were merely a "fine gentleman" and such things. **Glory**, **favor**, and **truth** are the favorite and most powerful words of John and of the Chaldaizing Orient <note>Refers to the cultural and linguistic influence of the "Chaldeans" or ancient Near Eastern traditions on the New Testament’s Greek style.</note>. “The Glorious One! He who is enveloped in light, abundance, bliss, and perfection: pure, true, shining, excellent, the source of all good; from him flows grace and truth!”—this is the description of heaven <note>original: "Behesch, Beheschtem," likely referring to the Persian word *Behesht*, meaning Paradise.</note>, of the grace and glory of God, the sight of which was otherwise closed off, but which here shone forth in its reflected image. The context and the widespread linguistic usage confirm this.

**“Of his fullness have we all received, grace for grace.”** These words are often wrongly explained because people, following German or Latin expressions, imagined "fullness" <note>original Greek: "$\pi\lambda\eta\rho\omega\mu\alpha$" (pleroma)</note> as a well with a bucket. Throughout the entire Orient, **Fullness** means Light: first the Primal Light in which God dwelt, and here His full image, the Sun—Jesus. Light, therefore, cannot be grasped in its favor and beauty, but rather received <margin>d) See Wolf, Deyling, Glassius, and nearly any biblical commentary.</margin>, mutually received; it radiates itself everywhere. And that is the meaning of the words: “We, his disciples and brothers, are transfigured in his radiance: we have received the reflection of his favor and mildness: the counter-grace <note>original Greek: "$\alpha\nu\tau\iota\chi\alpha\rho\iota\varsigma$" (anticharis), meaning a grace given in exchange for grace, or a reciprocal favor.</note> of his beauty shines in us!” Exactly the same thing that Paul says on this very matter: In us, the Lord’s clarity is reflected with an unveiled face, and we become

One should always keep the image of Light as the foundation for what follows.

...are transformed into that same image of radiance, from one level of clarity to another, as by the Lord, who is the Spirit. <note>This is a paraphrase of 2 Corinthians 3:18, which contrasts the "veil" of Moses with the "unveiled" glory of Christ.</note> Just as the Spirit contrasts the open, light-streaming countenance with the veiled face of Moses, and the ministry of the Spirit—of light, of life, and of essence—with the ministry of the letter, of shadow, and of death: so John cries out: “The law, the veil, was given through Moses; grace and truth, the open sight of the fullness of God, has come to us through Jesus Christ. No one has ever seen God, the Unseeable; the Only-begotten Son, who is from and in the bosom of the Father, he has revealed Him to us.” <note>original: "εξηγήσατο" (exēgēsato), translated as "revealed" or "declared."</note> His messenger, the Baptist, could only cry out: "He was before me, he was earlier than I"; but we! We all have become bright reflections of his sun; we pass from one glory to another!

Sit down, student of John—one who can feel his own soul—at this now fully explained entrance, as if at a quiet source of light. Forget all the distracting dross and veils in which I had to show his light, and sink your gaze yourself into this gentle teaching, welling up in pure waves and rhythmic pullings-back. It is the key to the New Testament, to the blueprint of God's love in Jesus, and to the entirety of human history.

f) How anyone in the world could say that the words in John 1:16–18 are spoken by John the Baptist is incomprehensible to me. Where did he say that? In whose name does he speak here? Was that—according to all historians and even this Evangelist—within his character, since as the greatest of prophets he is still always distinguished from the Kingdom of Jesus? And how would these words fit here, since the Baptist only begins to identify him later (v. 19–36), and in such a different and distant manner? Not to mention the feeling of progress, of the "swing" in the tone of the Evangelist, who in his prologue floats ever higher and more precisely from the origins of eternity through creation, eras, the incarnation, and the immediate effect upon himself and his brothers! — But if **Socinianism** <note>A rationalist Christian movement that denied the pre-existence of Christ; the author argues their interpretation makes the prologue "nonsense."</note> is true, then the prologue—even if these were the words of the Baptist—is the clearest nonsense.

# I.
## The Birth of Jesus.

Beyond the course of nature, through the overshadowing power of God, he was conceived, so that the Holy One who would be born of Mary might be the second, purer Adam. <note>The "second Adam" is a theological concept comparing Jesus to the biblical Adam; where the first Adam brought sin, the "second Adam" (Jesus) is seen as bringing spiritual life.</note> The announcement of the angel, his name, and the hymn of the heavenly hosts on the night of his birth were the quietest dawn of his kingdom, which could not come with splendor and noise, because it was not of this world.

Jesus became our brother; the most intimate, poorest, and most defining name, "**Son of Man!**" <margin>a) Schöttgen, *Hebrew Hours*, p. 90. <note>original: "hor. Hebr." refers to *Horae Hebraicae et Talmudicae*, a scholarly 18th-century work by Christian Schöttgen that explains the New Testament through Jewish tradition.</note></margin> became his name. He spoke and acted, felt and suffered, as humans feel. Tempted in every way, as we are, and yet always without sin, he became the first pure spiritual man on earth, the author and finisher of faith, the first to break through from the dark depths into the light.

His life is a pure human life without restrictions, prejudices, veils, folly, or vice: lived in innocence, simplicity, active peace, and sublime greatness. Everything contributes to this—place and time, duration and obstacles, sufferings and joys, friends and persecutions—and finally the early, strange, despised, and seemingly most incomplete departure <note>The "departure" (original: *Ausgang*) refers to the death of Jesus.</note> itself. Whoever is human, let them reflect themselves in the mirror of Jesus's humanity, and become what He was.

To such a life also belonged such Evangelists. The simplest man on earth is also described in the simplest manner, <margin>b) Xenophon’s *Memorabilia of Socrates*, otherwise the most popular biography, is artificial compared to the simplicity of the Evangelists.</margin> most understandable and heartfelt to every simple soul.
