### I.
## The Birth of Jesus.

Beyond the natural course of things, through the overshadowing power of God, he was conceived, so that the Holy One born of Mary might be the second, purer Adam. The announcement of the angel, his name, and the hymn of praise from the heavenly hosts on the night of his birth were the quietest dawning of his kingdom—a kingdom that could not come with splendor and noise, for it was not of this world.

Jesus became our brother; the most intimate, poorest, and most defining name, "Son of Man!" ᵃ) became his name. He spoke and acted, felt and suffered what humans can feel. Tempted in all ways as we are, and yet always without sin, he became the first pure spiritual man on earth, the author and perfecter of faith, the first to break through from the dark depths into the light.

His life is a pure human life without limitations, prejudices, veils, folly, or vice: lived in innocence, simplicity, effective tranquility, and sublime greatness. Everything contributes to this: place and time, duration and obstacles, sufferings and joys, friends and persecutions; and finally, the early, strange, despised, and—to all appearances—most unfinished end itself. Whoever is human, let them see themselves reflected in the mirror of Jesus's humanity, and become what He was.

To such a life belonged such Evangelists. The simplest man on earth is also described in the simplest way, ᵇ) most understandable and heartfelt to every simple person.

---

b) Xenophon's *Memorabilia of Socrates* <note>original: *Denkwürdigkeiten*</note>, otherwise the most popular biography, is mere artifice compared to the simplicity of the Evangelists.
</margin>

## Notes.

### 1. Concerning the Angels, the Servants at the Birth of Jesus.

Before Jesus or even his forerunner <note>John the Baptist</note> appears, the kingdom of heaven approaches the earth: the voice of the angels, which had been silent for so long, returns. This occurred as naturally as it had ceased, and in a manner that could be understood according to human concepts of time. Gabriel, Michael, and Raphael are Chaldean <note>An ancient region in Mesopotamia associated with Babylon, where the Israelites lived during their exile.</note> names for angels, just as are the seven spirits before the throne in Zechariah, the holy watchers, the "swift Gabriel," ᵃ) King Michael, and the entire city and order of spirits ᵇ) in the book of Daniel, who lived in Chaldea. In our source of explanation, we see the connection between these images. “They are powers of God, kings, heroes, messengers sent to bless and to help throughout all the spaces of creation: swift, living, effective, and mighty. They are called watchers, far-seeing, darting forth, and glorious. Their form like lightning and their clothing white as snow is thus their most natural description and explanation; the highest of the seven angels, who look upon all lands from before His omnipresent throne to offer help, is called the King of Heaven, the ‘Might of God.’” ᶜ) —

And this "Might of God" <note>The literal meaning of the name Gabriel</note>, the first of the heavenly princes, became a servant at the birth of Jesus. He who was to be born was therefore the Lord of the highest beings in creation.

How loftily and yet with such humility he speaks of Him; to Mary with kindness and love; and of the whole undertaking as a mystery of heaven reaching into eternity. What the language of human—

---

ᵃ) Whom someone in our own time has turned into a panting figure, weary with sweat, whereas the Chaldean image of the angel is that of a darting, tireless ray of light.

ᵇ) <unclear>ir</unclear> <note>original Hebrew: עיר</note> therefore also the <unclear>er</unclear> or <unclear>ir</unclear> <note>original Hebrew: ער or עיר</note> as "Watchers." In the teachings of Zoroaster <note>Ancient Persian prophet</note> they are called *Samkars*, "always waking, attentive, seeing from afar" <note>original French: *toujours veillans, attentif, voyant de loin*</note>—the widespread Oriental image of angels, stars, and heavenly hosts as watchful orders, or as a shining city. The *Egregors* <note>From the Greek *egrēgoroi*, meaning "the watchful ones"; prominent figures in the apocryphal Book of Enoch</note>, about whom so much has been written and speculated, originate from this.

ᶜ) see *Zend-Avesta* <note>The primary collection of religious texts of Zoroastrianism</note> Vol. II, pp. 81–82.

To be sure, at that time the angel's prophecy was not yet understood in its deep sense. The Savior of all the world was not to be the physical, national savior of one people or all peoples, but rather of *his* people, whatever they might understand by that. Since the [Babylonian] captivity, the Jews were to be weaned from their strict particularism <term>Particularism: the theological belief that God's favor is restricted to a specific chosen group</term>, their hard shell broken with effort; the last prophets clearly intended to portray the kingdom of the Messiah as a benefit for many—indeed, for all peoples—and specifically as an unearthly, spiritual benefit, though depicted in earthly colors. The element that each angel ruled was called his "people," where it was his office to do exactly what Jesus was to do here for his people: to be King, to rescue his element from evil, calamity, and impurity, and to promote it toward purity, fruitfulness, blessing, and higher light and life. So universal and pure was this language—not the cloistered concepts of a Savior!

### 2. On the Wise Men from the East.

By Magi, we understand neither Arabs, nor Indians, nor Chinese, nor Japanese: one cannot speculate about historically known names. Magi belonged to Chaldean and Persian wisdom; their region, as shall be proven elsewhere, was also originally called the East (Kedem). Theirs is the act of worship (<term>προσκυνειν</term> <note>original Greek: proskynein; a gesture of prostration or bowing in deep reverence before a superior or a deity</note>), the science of the stars, and the offering of such gifts. In our source of explanation, we are therefore actually in the region of their origin.

Throughout the entire Book of Daniel, we find that during the time of the Jews' captivity in Chaldea, Magism was in full bloom, and the Divinity arranged things so that Daniel himself soon came closer to their rank. If the Jews took so much from Chaldea, then one cannot imagine any adoption of ideas in the world without a reciprocal sharing; whether much or little, gain or loss, the Magi also received something from them.

And if they received anything, even just one idea, it was the Messianic hope. Upon this the Jewish tribe rested; for this promise, they had been shaped desde Abraham through all ages. Toward this, the prophets had pointed. To this end, Cyrus <note>The Persian King who ended the Babylonian captivity</note> released them to await their hopes. With this singular outlook of comfort, as we see from the last prophet, they went about the construction of their temple amidst misery and sorrow; they built it for the Angel of the Lord, whom they desired and who was now soon to arrive. If Chaldea knew even one thing of the Jews, it was this.

And it was precisely in Chaldea at that time that the birth of the Savior of the world was determined through numerical symbols <note>original: Zahlbildern; likely referring to the use of astronomical or numerological calculations used by the Magi to calculate the timing of the Messiah's birth</note>, which had never happened before.

<vocab>Magi, The East (Orient), Chaldea, Daniel, Messianic Hope, Abraham, Cyrus, Astronomy (Science of the Stars)</vocab>

<summary>This page identifies the "Wise Men" as practitioners of Chaldean and Persian Magism and argues that their arrival was the result of a long-standing exchange of ideas between Jewish exiles and Eastern scholars. It suggests that the Magi's knowledge of the coming Messiah was derived from Jewish hope combined with their own astronomical and numerical calculations.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the Magi, specifically focusing on how the prophet Daniel, as a high-ranking official in Babylon, influenced Chaldean thought. It bridges the gap between Jewish prophecy and Persian/Chaldean astronomy, leading toward the appearance of the Star of Bethlehem.</meta>

And these images were entirely Chaldean: as we see from Ezekiel, and especially Daniel and Zechariah, when compared with our source of the Magian religion <note>The author suggests that the prophetic imagery used by these biblical prophets shares a cultural vocabulary with the ancient Near Eastern traditions of the Magi</note>.

And if Daniel was the Chief Magian <note>original: "Archimagus"; the head of the priestly or scholarly caste in Babylon</note> and received his revelations in Chaldean imagery: then it could not fail that his "seventy sevens" <note>A reference to the prophecy of the seventy weeks in Daniel 9:24-27, which many interpreted as a chronological countdown to the Messiah</note> remained understandable not only in Chaldea, but was even preserved much more purely there—even if only as a legend or a visionary hope—than in Judea. This mixing of "fire and water"—blending Chaldean Magianism with the simple sense of the Old Testament—immediately began to confuse everything. They <note>referring to the Jewish scholars in Judea</note> entangled the Old Testament with such a cobweb of foreign ideas that they entirely missed the timing and the nature of the Messiah. That, too, was God’s counsel. — — Chaldea had no such opportunity or reason for such obscurations: thus they simply and faithfully retained what had been left to them.

And that a Jewish Messiah was expected throughout the entire Orient <note>original: "Morgenlande"; literally "the lands of the morning"</note> at that time is something we know from so many legends—even from Roman writers <note>Likely referring to historians like Tacitus and Suetonius, who recorded a widespread belief that a ruler from Judea would soon govern the world</note>, who, of all people, would have viewed such legends with the most skepticism. The Sibylline leaves <note>The Sibylline Oracles; ancient collections of prophecies often used by early Christians to point toward Christ</note> that claimed to speak of Christ—whatever they were or wherever they came from—were at least widely circulated tales, as the Christians began to cite them shortly thereafter. And they all ultimately point back to Chaldea. Throughout the entirety of Apocryphal Hellenism <note>Jewish literature written in Greek during the period between the Old and New Testaments</note>, for instance, Balaam was such a well-known figure with his alleged prophecies and sins <margin>h) see Fabricius, *Codex Pseudepigraphus*, Vol. I, p. 807.</margin>. As a matter of fact, no philosophically-minded wit can deny this.

And behold! Here we are at the star. \* *Star-science, astronomy, astrology, star-superstition* (call it what you will!) was, according to the general testimony of antiquity, the very element—or at least the language—of Chaldean wisdom. This wisdom had originally arisen from star-worship, or Sabaeism <note>The ancient worship of celestial bodies as deities or divine spirits</note>, and had purified itself into a religion of light. Zoroaster’s <note>The founding prophet of the Persian religion</note> name itself means "living star" or "star of life" <margin>i) Zeretoschtro, see *Life of Zoroaster*, Vol. II, p. 4.</margin>. To him, as to the entire ancient Orient, the stars are angels, warriors,

Warriors, messengers of God, leaders, heavenly guardians <margin>k) See the *Bundahishn* <note>A collection of Zoroastrian cosmogony and cosmology; the author refers to it here as "Cosmogony of the Persians"</note>, Vol. III, p. 387. Milton has Mary relate it beautifully:

*Paradise Regained*.</margin>. The God who once elevated Joseph in Egypt and Daniel in Chaldea through dreams—because that was the "handle" <note>The specific cultural method or psychological entry point</note> by which He could grasp the soul of those lands—was it unbecoming for Him to lead these Wise Men by a star to the manger of His Son, since they recognized in stars such guardians, heavenly leaders, and messengers of God?

Behold, the simple resolution in the tone of the Evangelist. And what a worthy language of the Divinity to the souls of men! The outstretched heavens are His book: stars are His words.

<note>original Greek: "Ως αρ’ εφη, τοισι δε θεος τερας εγγυαλιζεν / Αισιον, ω και παντες επευφημησαν ιδοντες / Στελλεσθαι την δ’ οιμον· επι προ ολκος ετυχθη / Ουρανιης ακτινος, οπη και προσιμον ηεν —" This is an excerpt from ancient poetry (likely Nonnus) describing a divine sign.</note>
"Thus he spoke, and to them the god displayed a favorable omen, at which all who saw it cheered; they set out on their way; a trail of heavenly light was formed before them, marking the path where it led—"

I do not know who could know the spirit of antiquity, especially in the Orient <note>original: "Morgenlande"; literally "the lands of the morning"</note>, and not feel this!

One must put oneself in the position of these men: what resolve, what simple confidence, and what pure devotion were necessary to undertake and complete the journey! How many national prejudices of their own country and religion had to be overcome! Or how many spiritual concepts—at least premonitions—of a King of all nations must have preceded them, so that they ventured into the land of the despised Jewish religion: allowing themselves to be stared at, perhaps laughed at, and sluggishly directed, and yet still trusting their star. In truth, it was a "journey of the stars" <note>An idiomatic way of saying their journey mirrored the purity and certainty of the stars themselves</note> in its simplicity, certainty, and devotion; that we find it today so unpolitical, unastronomical, superstitious, foolish, and ridiculous only shows that we would likely not have been worthy of the language of this star. The "sundial" of the Scribes <note>Referring to the Jewish religious scholars who knew the prophecies</note> pointed toward Bethlehem: but they did not join the journey. The "sole politician" Herod wanted to come and worship the child as well; but that honor was refused <note>original: "verbeten"; politely but firmly declined or forbidden</note>.

Moreover, history is often burdened with absurdities that it knows nothing about. They went to the capital of the country because they were seeking a king; they did not rely on the claim that a star had guided them there while hovering only three cubits <note>Approximately 4.5 feet; the author is mocking the literalist idea that the star was floating just above their heads</note> above their heads. Their own identification of the land where Jesus was to be born was, as has been shown, derived from quite different sources. They were directed toward Bethlehem; and again, the star did not show them the way there. They traveled the path naturally, through human guides. Thus they found the house—for in the small village of Bethlehem, the birth of Jesus must have been well known enough due to the story of the shepherds and the angels!—because it was pointed out to them as the place of a wonderful, heavenly child. And now—blessings upon them! A happy prophecy!—now they saw the star again, their heavenly leader. They were not mistaken: the star stood there where the child was; the child had to be where the star was. How simply and powerfully Matthew describes this, he who was happily ignorant of all learned astronomy and the political wisdom of nations! I do not know who could have read of the appearance of a star, a meteor, or a happy, striking omen in a pagan poet or historian and not feel this. Homer and Virgil, Apollonius and Orpheus, Herodotus, and even Plutarch are full of passages of this kind.

“When they saw the star,” says the Evangelist in joyful irregularities of speech <note>original: "Solocismen" (solecisms). The author suggests the simple, unpolished grammar of the Gospel reflects genuine emotion</note>, “they were exceedingly joyful, and went into the house and found the child with Mary his mother, and fell down, and opened their treasures and presented him with gold, frankincense, and myrrh.” Their gifts take on the noblest interpretation when viewed through our Zoroaster. To them, gold was the image of light, of purity, of royal dignity, and of divinity. “Golden King,” “Golden Mithra,” “Golden Hom,” <note>original: "Roi d’or, Mithra d’or, Hom d’or." Hom or Haoma is a sacred plant and divinity in Zoroastrianism</note> and “Golden Soul” are common praises here. The writings of John, which are written entirely in the imagery of this language <note>The author likely refers to the symbolic, almost mystical language of the Gospel of John or the Book of Revelation, which shares themes with the Persian "Magian" thought being discussed</note>, become clear. — And now we also see from where the ancient legend got the idea to turn them into kings? In the religion of the Magi <note>Zoroastrianism, the ancient Persian religion</note>, this was the expression of dignity that ordered and bound everything together. God, the seven highest angels of creation, every angel in its own element, every member of the religion (<term>Herbed<note>A junior rank of priest in the Zoroastrian tradition</note></term>), and every father of a house was a "king." A blessing, struggling king! In this image lay his benefits and his duties: this is how the "Holy Three Kings" came to be.

But why did they come? What fruit did their journey bear? Their path was marked with blood: and yet the sound of their arrival achieved nothing! — But it *was* meant to achieve something! The language of God—who always speaks to the masses through events because He cannot speak to them in any other way—was also here a testimony against them! — Chaldea <note>An ancient region in Mesopotamia, often associated with the Babylonian exile and the birthplace of astronomy and magic</note> was the oldest, wisest, and most famous pagan religion, to which the Jews had once been led, and from which they had adopted so much; and look! From this, their former high school of wisdom, came wise men to awaken and shame them. Imagine their surprising procession: it was like the loud, resonant ringing of a bell from an ancient, holy temple in their ears. The murder of the innocents, the flowing blood of the infants—who became related to Jesus through their deaths—and the cries of the mothers on the mountains: "they are no more!" <note>A reference to Matthew 2:18 and Jeremiah 31:15: "Rachel weeping for her children... because they are no more"</note> impressed the event even more deeply from another side. "I will move all heathens, heaven, and earth, when the Treasure of the Nations <note>original: "Kleinod der Völker"; a reference to the messianic prophecy in Haggai 2:7</note> comes." Thus the voice of the prophet had once called out from Chaldea, and here were the firstfruits of the nations. Judea slept. The kingdom was to be taken from them and given to the Gentiles. They persecuted the newborn and drove him out of the land before his eyes even knew men: from the feast of Pentecost onward, history showed that the Gospel went out from Judea into the diaspora of the nations.

If I could only point out here with a single hint how the Evangelist also silently sketches the chosen circumstances of Jesus's childhood and upbringing! That a foreign land, Egypt, the

### II.
## The Baptism of John.

He had a great forerunner, the Elijah <note>original: "Elias," the Greek/Latin form of the Hebrew name Elijah</note> of the New Testament, who received the command of baptism to prepare the way for Him. John’s baptism was water, a cleansing deluge <note>original: "Sündfluth"—literally "the Sin-Flood," the German term for the Great Flood of Noah—here used to describe a powerful spiritual washing</note> that washed away in rushing torrents so that fire might come—the fire that enlightened, warmed, and consumed all that was earthly ᵃ).

Everything John did made the people active and eager for the Messiah. Not only the great multitude, but even the hypocritical Pharisees warmed themselves by his sweet hopes and skipped, like children, in the brilliance of his light; ᵇ) rather, he also had crowds of ardent disciples until his death: even long afterward and up to the present day, his name has not been extinguished. But he did not wish to found a religion or a sect; he was only the herald of Jesus, whom he announced, pointed out, and to whom he directed his first disciples. Of Him he spoke with a reverence, and of himself with a humility, that was fitting for the first among those born of women. He could not die in peace if any part of him still clung to the shadow and not everything to Jesus, the Light.

Short and glorious was his life, like a flash of lightning in the night, like a purifying storm wind in the desert: he was Elijah in his life as in his death. A great man! Just, strong, selfless, humble, fiery, and joyfully striving: Jesus called him the greatest of men. Yet the Spirit was not in his hand: the least in the kingdom of Jesus's powers <note>The author uses "Reich der Kräfte" (Kingdom of Powers) to describe the spiritual realm or the Kingdom of Heaven established by Christ</note> was greater than he! ᶜ)

which hearty, simple, people-loving Galilean spirit <note>original: "Galiläismus," a term the author uses to describe the unique, unpretentious, and inclusive character Jesus derived from his upbringing in Galilee</note> then also bears witness throughout his entire life.

<sig>K 2</sig>

## II.
## The Baptism of John.

He <note>Referring to Jesus</note> had a great herald, the Elijah <note>original: "Elias," the Hebrew prophet who was prophesied to return before the arrival of the Messiah</note> of the New Testament, who received the command of baptism to prepare the way for Him. John’s baptism was water, a great flood <note>original: "Sündfluth," referring to the biblical Deluge of Noah</note> that washed away in rushing torrents, so that fire might come—a fire that enlightened, warmed, and consumed all that was earthly ᵃ).

John stirred everyone to action and made them eager for the Messiah. Not only the common crowd, but even the hypocritical Pharisees warmed themselves by his sweet hopes and skipped, like children, in the brilliance of his light; ᵇ) moreover, he had crowds of ardent disciples until his death: long afterward and even to this day, his name has not been extinguished. But he did not wish to found a religion or a sect; he was merely the herald of Jesus, whom he announced, pointed out, and to whom he assigned his first disciples. He spoke of Jesus with a reverence, and of himself with a humility, that befitted the first among those born of women. He could not die in peace if any part of him still clung to the shadow and not everything to Jesus, the Light.

Short and glorious was his life, like a lightning bolt in the night, like a cleansing storm wind in the desert: he was Elijah in his life as in his death. A great man! Just, strong, selfless, humble, fiery, joyfully striving: Jesus called him the greatest among men. But the Spirit was not in his hand: the least in the kingdom of Jesus's powers was greater than he! ᶜ)

---

a) Matt. 3. &emsp; b) John 5:31-35. &emsp; c) Matt. 11:1-17.

<meta>This page transitions from the narrative of John the Baptist's life to a scholarly commentary on the cultural and historical roots of baptism. It seeks to bridge the gap between Old Testament washings and New Testament rituals by looking toward Persian influences.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>Notes.</header>

### 1. On the Origin of John’s Baptism.

Much has been debated on both sides, often to excess, regarding this matter ᵃ); I believe that, alongside my source, I am now closer to the perspective from which the origin of both Jewish baptism and the baptism of John becomes clear.

Namely, before the Babylonian Captivity <note>The period in the 6th century BCE when the Jews were exiled to Babylon</note> in the Old Testament, the baptism of the Jews is certainly unproven: they had ritual washings in the Law of Moses, but no baptism. After the Captivity, and especially in the New Testament, everything is full of baptisms—Chaldea <note>A region in ancient Mesopotamia, often used here to refer to the broader Persian sphere of influence</note> is therefore likely the origin, and evidently so.

„Since this religion and wisdom <note>Referring to the ancient Persian faith of Zoroastrianism</note> formed everything from concepts of purity and light, washing naturally became the gateway to light, the distant first image of the earthly purification of the elements. Water, therefore—as it was expressed in their transfiguring philosophy—the gentle, golden, helpful water was the daughter of God, the love of all good spirits, created by Ormuzd <note>original: "Ormuzd," the Zoroastrian deity of light and goodness, also known as Ahura Mazda</note> immediately after the light. It flowed from heaven from his bosom, springing there eternally to flow around the earth, purifying and enlivening all of nature; it carries all the seeds of life within itself and is the holy armor that God gave to man against impurity, death, rot, and decay. Water used in conjunction with the Word, they said, destroys the powers of evil and resists the impure spirits: it was invoked, and all consecrations from evil and anointings toward higher perfection were performed through baptism ᵇ). The oldest accounts we have of the Mithraic Mysteries ᶜ) <note>A Roman mystery religion centered on the god Mithras, with roots in Persian tradition</note>, though extremely fragmented, speak of baptismal customs, and the current liturgies of Zoroaster <note>The ancient Persian prophet Zarathustra</note> show their nature and simple origin.“ With concepts of this kind, the Jews returned home, and the Pharisaism of the New Testament is full of consecrations, washings, and baptisms.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of baptism, moving from the broad Persian (Zoroastrian) influence discussed on the previous page to the specific Jewish context of the First Century. It argues that baptism was already a familiar concept to the Jewish people before John the Baptist appeared.</meta>

<page-num>77</page-num>
<sig>K 3</sig>

If they were baptizing everything then, would they not have baptized the proselytes <note>Non-Jews who converted to Judaism</note> who joined them, since they were indeed making proselytes at that time? It becomes so difficult here to reject all Jewish testimonies without any basis, when we see in the New Testament how popular the images of baptism are and how much was expressed through them. Paul would never have used the beautiful image of the "baptism" of the Jews under Moses <note>Referring to 1 Corinthians 10:2, where Paul describes the Israelites being "baptized into Moses" in the cloud and the sea</note>—especially for the purpose of speaking about the misuse of spiritual gifts—if Pharisaism <note>The religious and political movement of the Pharisees</note> had not possessed similar customs and ways of thinking. So too Peter, with the analogy of baptism and the Great Flood. So too other passages and parables of baptism (or "baptisms") as a well-known and holy thing. 

Josephus <margin>d) Josephus, *Antiquities of the Jews*, Book 18, Chapter 7. <note>original: "Antiq. L. 18, c, 7." This refers to the historian Flavius Josephus; modern editions usually locate the passage on John the Baptist in Book 18, Chapter 5.</note></margin> also speaks in this way about the baptism of John. Similarly, in the Gospel history, the priests send messengers to him, not to ask *what* baptism is, but *why* he—to people already of their religion—is baptizing them as proselytes of a *new* baptism? They ask whether he might be the Messiah? — — here, the high concepts that people held regarding baptism clearly shine through, which remained among the people long after John’s death. Yes, finally, why would God have commanded baptism to John in order to start a revolution of the land toward Christ if no one had known it, or if no one had connected higher concepts with it? I can imagine almost no bolder historical denial than to claim otherwise, especially based on such wretched counter-arguments!

But what does proselyte baptism actually have to do with the baptism of John? John clearly testified that he was not "making proselytes" in the traditional sense. Chaldeanism <note>The cultural and religious systems of ancient Mesopotamia</note> and the Persian religion made no converts, yet they still practiced baptism. People cling to a secondary idea <note>the specific Jewish ritual for converts</note> and thus fail to recognize the practice as a whole. John's baptism was (whether proselytes were baptized or not!) a solemn, sharp, and holy consecration. As Josephus and the Evangelists say ten times over: it was a baptism toward a strict, new righteousness—a baptism of repentance. He showed a perverse generation that the decayed religion of their fathers would not help them. He declared this with the sharpest voice to all social classes. The Messiah was

...near, but with fire for destruction, if they did not leave all their old ways, and so forth.

“But did he then baptize unto Jesus?” In action, indeed: he consecrated and purified the land with the baptism of this strict righteousness and self-denial; he paved the way for him—that is, as Luke says with incomparably beautiful words: those who accepted the counsel of God, according to which He had also sent this John to them, and set out upon his path, those were truly the best-prepared disciples of Jesus. Whoever had passed through the "Deluge" of the denial of all habitual sins and old prejudices, and through the strict piety that John demanded, would certainly not have taken offense at the outward person of Jesus and was fit for the reception of the Spirit and the Kingdom of God itself. This, however, was no implicit charcoal burner’s faith <term>charcoal burner’s faith: a German idiom (Kohlerglaube) meaning a blind, unquestioning faith held by the uneducated</term> in a Jesus they did not yet know.

John could therefore still continue his work even when Jesus was present: he preached righteousness and pointed toward him. The disciples of Jesus baptized before the Spirit—as it is often stated—was present: for Jesus was not yet glorified. It came before the Pharisees that Jesus made more disciples than John <margin>e) John 4:1 <note>original: "Joh. 4, 1."</note></margin> and they properly preferred the latter over the former: for John, with his hopes and with his purification <term>lustration: a ritual cleansing or purification</term> for repentance, was certainly not as displeasing to them as the Jesus in whom they saw all their expectations frustrated. A dispute arose between the disciples of John and the Jews <margin>f) John 3:25. Following my explanation, I may aptly retain the "of the Jews" <note>original: "Ἰουδαίων" (Ioudaion)</note>.</margin> regarding baptisms and consecrations—where was this all leading? Was the despised Jesus truly the Messiah? — — John’s answer puts this meaning beyond doubt.

Accordingly, a number of passages become clear. John, says Jesus, is Elijah, the greatest of those born of women; but the least in the Kingdom of Heaven—the kingdom of higher gifts, powers, and wonders—is greater than he. The Law and the Prophets prophesied until John; as the forerunner, he still belongs to the Old Testament; the baptism of the Spirit was not in his hand, nor was he meant to...

...did not live to see it either. It is said that John performed no sign, and he died while Jesus's kingdom was still most under pressure. (Matthew 11:10-25.)

John was a burning and shining lamp <note>original: "$\lambda\upsilon\chi\nu o\varsigma$" (lychnos), meaning a portable lamp or candle</note>, and for a while you skipped like children in his light: you rejoiced in his hopes for the Messiah and his great gifts. Matthew 11:16-17), but also all meaning and context vanishes: for throughout the Gospels, Jesus appeals to the testimony of John as a teacher who was revered by them and recognized by God, not one who was mocked.

The best man <term>Paranymphus: from the Greek *paranymphos*, a ritual assistant or 'friend of the bridegroom' who escorted the couple</term> escorted the bride: the procession went with shouts of joy, the bridegroom's voice leading the way, and friends and relatives followed. Indeed, since children were considered the greatest blessing of Ormuzd <margin>h) The woman is saved through childbearing: this was entirely in the spirit of the Orient. This condition is also mentioned in the Apostle's saying. <note>Refers to 1 Timothy 2:15.</note></margin> <note>Ormuzd: Another name for Ahura Mazda, the creator deity in Zoroastrianism, whose influence the author sees in Chaldean/Persian culture.</note> and the bridge to heaven, the childless man would escort the bride to another in his name, so that he might 'grow' through him; thus he rejoiced over the bridegroom's joy, for his own joy was now fulfilled"—how noble, how striking the image becomes here! ...did not even live to see it. John performed no signs, it is said, and he died while the Kingdom of Jesus was still under the greatest pressure. (Matt. 11:10-25.)

John was a burning and shining lamp <note>original: λυχνος (lychnos), a portable lamp or candle</note>, and for a while you skipped about like children in his light: you rejoiced in his messianic hopes and great gifts. Now that I am someone other than you imagined, you do not want to come to me, the light <note>original: φως (phos), the source of light or the sun</note>, and receive in reality what he promised you from afar—not from himself, but from me. — — A modern scholar <note>likely referring to a contemporary rationalist theologian</note> has devised a learned Arabism <term>Arabismus: a philological interpretation based on Arabic linguistic roots</term>, according to which it is supposed to mean: *you mocked John*—whereby not only the fine imagery is lost, along with the punishment of a childish age known from elsewhere—which rejoiced and was vexed like boys (cf. Matt. 11:16, 17)—but also all meaning and context falls away: for Jesus, throughout the Gospel, appeals to the testimony of John as a teacher who was honored by them and divinely recognized, not mocked.

John says: he is the bridegroom, I am only the bridegroom’s friend. But the friend of the bridegroom stands at a distance and rejoices greatly at the bridegroom's voice—an image that has been abhorrently interpreted based on alleged customs of the bedchamber. According to Chaldean customs, <margin>g) See *Zend-Avesta* Vol. III, p. 558, 559. Civil and religious usages of the Zend. Cf. Schöttgen p. 334 f.</margin> in the spirit of which this entire Gospel is written, and which had largely become common in Judea as well, "marriage was a festival of joy for the bride, the bridegroom, and all friends. The **paranymph** <term>paranymphus: a friend of the bridegroom who acted as a "best man" or master of ceremonies</term> led the bride in; the procession went with shouts of joy, the bridegroom's voice leading the way, and friends and relatives followed. Indeed, since children were considered the greatest blessing of **Ormuzd** <note>Ormuzd: the supreme deity in Zoroastrianism, also known as Ahura Mazda</note> and the bridge to heaven, the childless man led the bride to another in his name, so that he might grow through him, and thus rejoiced in the bridegroom’s joy: for his own joy was now fulfilled"—how noble, how striking the image becomes here! *He must increase*, etc.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the cultural and religious context of John the Baptist, specifically examining how ancient Near Eastern traditions—such as Sabaism and Zoroastrianism—might have influenced the way his followers and the early Church understood his role as a divine messenger.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>
<sig>III. The</sig>
<meta>catchword: III. The</meta>

> “Sabaism <term>Sabaism: an ancient religion of the Near East often associated with star worship, but here linked specifically to the Mandaeans or 'Disciples of John'</term> had, namely in the depths of time, a John (Oannes <note>original: Ωαννης; a mythical being in Babylonian mythology who taught humanity wisdom</note> according to Berosus, Alexander Polyhistor, etc.) to whom they owed the knowledge of God and the origin of the world, and all moral arts, just as the Greeks owed them to Orpheus, the Egyptians to Hermes, and so on. A historical-mythological person who was the first messenger, prophet, and lawgiver to them. In the case of our Zoroaster, it is Hom (Heomo) <note>original: Haoma; both a sacred plant and a divinity in Zoroastrianism, associated with strength and immortality</note> who had humbled the Evil One, directed the course of the waters, and fertilized the earth. Hom, who received the holy belt of struggle and preached the law upon the mountains. Hom, who now dwells on the holy mountains in the splendor of Paradise, but still often appears to explain the law: he is the bread of life, the holy plant of immortality, the juice <note>referring to the sacred pressed extract of the Haoma plant</note> through which the dead shall one day live—all this is the ancient Chaldean John!” 1)

May I add one word as to why the Disciples of John <term>Johannesjünger: a term used here for the Mandaeans, a group that venerates John the Baptist as the true Messiah and maintains ancient Gnostic traditions</term> have, from the very beginning, clung to this doctrine? Why John <note>referring to the author of the Gospel of John</note> thus describes his Gospel for them and the Sabaeans? — From chapter to chapter, his own nature is explained. This John was more than Elijah, or one of the prophets. He was so similar in several striking respects to the Baptist and teacher of the law with the belt in the wilderness. The time back then was full of the reappearance of holy persons: “Are you Elijah? Are you a prophet?” In short, I wish for further news from Kerman <note>a city in Iran where the author believes remnants of these sects still live</note>, where the Disciples of John and the Sabaeans still remain, to clarify the most beautiful Gospel and perhaps the most beautiful book in all the world.

### III.
## The Baptism of Jesus.

Jesus, too, who had grown up like a noble tree in the desert <note>This imagery suggests Jesus's hidden years of growth in spiritual isolation before his public appearance</note>, came to John’s baptism. That was the holy moment: the heavens broke open, and all the powers of the Kingdom of Light <term>Lichtreich: a term frequent in mystical theology referring to the spiritual realm of God’s presence</term> settled upon their center, which had now become visible. The still, gentle dove flowed down upon him, and the voice spoke: This is my beloved Son, my inner joy! — Behold! this is my servant, whom I have chosen, and my beloved, in whom my soul is well pleased. I will put my Spirit upon him, and he shall be the King of the nations. He will not wrangle or cry out. The bruised reed he will not break, and the smoldering wick he will not quench. ᵃ) Behold there: the person, the work, the character of Jesus in his entire life! That is what the voice signifies! The gentle, heavenly dove! The outflowing of heavenly powers—sublime, still, and glorious.

In this Spirit, Jesus wandered as the Lamb of God, to bear the sins of the world and to prepare his own people for the powers of the Kingdom of Heaven. The Spirit, which flowed upon him without measure, was intended to one day live and work with still majesty in all his members <note>referring to the "members" of the Church or the body of believers</note>.

But for Jesus, too, the glory of initiation had to be followed immediately by a state of hard struggle. The Hero between Spirit and Earth, Heaven and World, overcame ᵇ) and walked the high path of his life in the Spirit and not in the flesh—for the eternal blessedness of his brothers, and not for selfishness, honor, comfort, or earthly joy. His life is the life of all Christians who are one in him.

|---|---|
| a) Matthew 12:18. | b) Matthew 4:1-11. |

# Notes.

Since we are speaking here for the first time in detail about the **Spirit of God**: let us develop the expression more closely from the linguistic usage of the time and the regions.

Zechariah saw the **seven flames** ᵃ) and the answer given to him was: these are the **seven Spirits of God** sent out into all lands, or the flaming gazes of the Omnipresent One, which traverse the globe, and which nothing can resist. “Who are you, great mountain, that before Zerubbabel <note>A governor of the Persian province of Judah who led the first wave of Jewish exiles returning from Babylon</note> must become a plain. It shall not happen through army and power, but through my **Spirit**, says the Lord.” Explained thus from our source of elucidation—where the seven spirits of God, angels, and heavenly princes are everything through which God rules, blesses, and acts in creation—we see that the outgoing Spirit was, as Hellenism <note>The spread of Greek culture and language across the Near East following the conquests of Alexander the Great</note> so often says, the **all-encompassing spirit** ᵇ) <note>original Greek: πνευμα παντα δεχομενον (*pneuma panta dechomenon*), meaning a spirit that receives or contains all things</note>, the personification of God's all-embracing action.

Zechariah saw the **two olive trees** ᶜ), from which gold—the purest oil—flowed; and they were the two anointed with the Spirit, who always stand before the Lord of the earth and serve him. This is a symbol, then, of the gentle mildness and the uniquely-flowing utility of the Spirit.

Zechariah received the promise: ᵈ) “the Lord will shield Jerusalem; the weakest among them shall be like David, and the house of David like God's house, like the angel of the Lord before them. And I will pour out upon the house of David my **Spirit of humility**, of gentle pleading.” Gentle mildness, therefore, is the character of the Spirit. — And behold there, in Jesus here are all these images! The wide-open heaven, the encompassing powers of light and fire! The voice: *my Son, the Well-Beloved!* will do and accomplish it—and the **gentle dove**! Neither a symbol of luck, nor of prophecy

---

ᵃ) 4:1–10.
ᵇ) Hesychius <note>Likely Hesychius of Alexandria, a 5th-century grammarian who compiled a vast lexicon of Greek words</note> also explains the **Paraclete** <term>Paraclete: from the Greek 'parakletos', meaning 'advocate' or 'helper', a title used in the Gospel of John for the Holy Spirit</term> in this way.
ᶜ) Zechariah 4:11–14.
ᵈ) Chapter 12:10.

prophecy was it here, like among the pagans perhaps the swan of Apollo: but according to many passages reflecting the favorite way of thinking among Jewish writers <margin>e) See Schöttgen. <note>Christian Schöttgen (1687–1751) was a German scholar who specialized in explaining the New Testament through the lens of ancient Jewish literature and Rabbinic tradition.</note></margin> e) it was the image of spiritual gifts, of love and gentleness. Exactly that is what the voice said. It contained everything within itself: to designate Jesus as the eternal image, the imprint, the Son of joy and of God’s favor; Jesus as the creator of the world and of the human race, in whom God looked upon creation with pleasure; Jesus as the brother and Son of Man, who came in the name of all to fulfill the Father’s will, in whom all shall fulfill it and be made pleasing to the Father through the Beloved; Jesus, in whom it is the Father’s will that nothing remains behind, but that he gives life to all whom the Father gives him. Read what I have stammered in the plan of the First Book and concerning the song of the angels; grasp it together in one view and feeling, and this word will become—the symbol of Jesus’s baptism, of his whole life, his entire person and redemption. The Father spoke these words on the Mountain of Transfiguration near the end of his life! The angels sang them at his manger! Here they resounded in the middle of his career! — Holy symbol of Jesus's baptism.

We see that the language of the following chapters of John still hovers around this image of Jesus’s baptism. When he says soon afterward to Nathanael: <margin>f) John 1:51.</margin> f) “you will see greater things than this! the opened heaven and the **fiery forces** and messengers of creation flowing down and up all around through all beings!” How this holy, high, unexplained saying has been tortured by interpretations, when people looked for climbing angels with wings. And throughout the whole of John, Jesus hovers in the opened heaven. The Gospel proceeds from this image and lets Jesus always move, speak, and act as the image of the Father expressed in grace and truth—the visible center of all powers, all love, all movement and effect, blessing and animation. <margin>g) John 5:17-29. That was also the concept of the angels: the messengers and rays of God acting through all beings.</margin> g) Thus he always stands in high tranquility, completes his work, lifts

the beloved finally transfigured with himself into heaven, and he goes there and suffers. Hence also the language of rebirth <note>original: "Wiedergeburtsprache"</note> of the New Testament: how we are born anew, <margin>h) John 3:1-21.</margin> anointed with the Spirit, enter the kingdom of God, become heavenly, and—as children, as angels, as dear sons, indeed without separation, one with Jesus—do the will of God as his well-beloved son, the joy of the Father! We are meant to feel and be heaven deep within our souls, just as he was—noble language of rebirth, what have you become? And language of new righteousness and philosophical conversion, what are you? —

Let us for once hear a piece of Jesus’s conversation with Nicodemus according to the new "proselyte hypothesis" <note>The "proselyte hypothesis" was a contemporary scholarly theory suggesting that Jesus’s talk of "rebirth" was not a spiritual mystery, but merely a reference to the Jewish custom of calling a convert (a proselyte) a "newly born child."</note> of our time, which has been repeated ten times and is to be repeated ten thousand times: I will render it as faithfully as I know how:

| | |
|---|---|
| **Nicodemus** | Rabbi, we know that you have come from God to teach us: for no one can do the extraordinary things that you do, etc. |
| **Jesus** | And I say to you most solemnly: if you are not baptized, that is, born again—to use the expression you apply to your proselytes—then you cannot be admitted into my new religious society <note>original: "Religionssocietät"</note>. |
| **Nicodemus** | But how can a man be born again—that is, still be baptized as a proselyte—when he is old? Can he go back into his mother’s womb again to—be baptized as a proselyte? |
| **Jesus** | And I assure you once more: unless someone is born again of water and spirit—that is, following the spirit of the Jewish laws <note>original: "suivant l’esprit des loix Judaiques"</note> (as Montesquieu calls his book *The Spirit of the Laws*)—just as you say of your proselytes: then I cannot [admit] him into my new society |

<meta>This page continues the critique of Enlightenment-era rationalist theology. The author has just introduced a satirical "translation" of the dialogue between Jesus and Nicodemus to demonstrate how contemporary scholars strip the Gospel of its mystical and spiritual depth by reducing the concept of "rebirth" to a mere social formality or "proselyte hypothesis."</meta>

Join my society. What is physically born as a Jew, remains physically born as a Jew; but what is baptized according to the spirit of the Levitical law, that is baptized according to the spirit of the Levitical law and may enter into my new society. — Do not be surprised that I said to you: you must be baptized, that is, born anew, just as you say of your proselytes <note>A "proselyte" is a person who has converted from one religion or opinion to another; here referring to converts to Judaism.</note>. The wind blows from whichever side it wishes, even if you do not exactly know from where it comes now? It is just so with everyone who is baptized as my proselyte according to the spirit of the Levitical law—and so forth.

This is the spirit of our new interpretations, paraphrases, dictionaries, and the like. This is what we call removing tropes <note>In this context, "tropes" refers to metaphors or figurative language.</note>, translating and interpreting in a way that is understandable and rational for our age. With a hundred thousand pleonasms <note>The use of more words than are necessary to convey meaning.</note>, nothing remains in the New Testament but: become Christians! And that means: accept my confession and be sure to be baptized! Join my new society, whether you be Jews or Heathens!

Our Chaldean Magus <note>Likely referring to Zoroaster (Zarathustra), the ancient Persian prophet, or the archetype of the Eastern sage.</note> was no Christian, much less Christ. The expressions: *to be born again*, even with the double meaning of *anothen* <note>original Greek: "$\alpha\nu\omega\vartheta\epsilon\nu$"; meaning both "from above" and "anew."</note>—*from above* and *anew*—the *Kingdom of God*, the *communion of heaven*, *birth from the seed of Ormuzd* <note>In Zoroastrianism, Ormuzd (Ahura Mazda) is the creator and lord of light.</note>, *Son of God*, *children of God* i) belong to him, and came from Chaldea to the Jews. In his work, they possess a sense so simple and natural, based on how he conceived the invisible universe in the childhood of the world; he perceived that our soul, as an invisible sister to the spirits, must also strive toward that realm with all good beings. His religion made no proselytes, and he knew nothing of the Levitical law in its "philosophical spirit" <note>original: "esprit philosophique"; a reference to the intellectual approach of Enlightenment thinkers like Montesquieu.</note>, nor had he read Montesquieu. Yet, I would like to see the "earthly pot" <note>A derogatory term for a person who lacks spiritual or intellectual depth.</note> who, in his presence or among the Platonists, Alexandrians, Jews, and Heathens, would understand all these concepts—as multifaceted as they may appear—so shallowly, flatly, and senselessly! And this in the presence of the spiritual, heavenly Christ!

<meta>This page continues the author’s exploration of the spiritual and mystical dimensions of baptism, moving from a critique of rationalist interpretations to a deeper investigation of the Early Church's understanding of "rebirth." The earliest language of baptism and rebirth among Christians revolves, in the manner shown above, around the imagery of the baptism of Jesus. Peter says it is not a mere washing away of external sins from the flesh <note>Referencing 1 Peter 3:21, which contrasts physical washing with a spiritual "appeal to God."</note> (like the Great Flood and the baptism of John: even in contrast, there is no mention of bodily filth); but rather, just as the waters of the Flood once brought death to the flesh, k) and as Christ was put to death in the flesh so that the Spirit might live for God: so baptism clings and connects to God in all goodness through the resurrection of Jesus. As Paul often states, it opens heaven, unites us with Christ, transports us into a heavenly being, and serves as the sacrament and symbol of the death of the earthly and the putting on of the kingdom of light and the armor of Jesus. To be enlightened through water, to taste the heavenly gift of the Spirit and the powers of the kingdom of heaven, were likewise descriptors and experiences of baptism in the Early Church. Are we really to think only of external confessions, documents, or at most legal declarations in all these sayings and passages?

In Christ himself, do we not see immediately where the Spirit—not merely declared, but truly imparted—drove him? Into the desert, specifically, for forty days of prayer, the enjoyment and contemplation of God. — After this ended, the fragment from the soul of Jesus shows us—even in the state of his temptation, brief and fragmented as it appears before us—in which Kingdom of God, in which heaven, and in which spirit Jesus existed.

It was not external sin that the Tempter (whom we have already identified above) was concerned with, but rather the internal turning away from God, the sinking down from the spirit into the earthly, leading to mistrust, or to foolhardiness, or even to the worship of him; the consequences were indifferent to him, provided the cause was present. The slightest shift of Jesus from his center—that pure activity within the will of God for which Jesus existed—was meant to precede and

(Vol. III. p. 358. 359.) The tradition regarding the first ages of the world remains similar across all mythologies.

Jesus is a great model, both for all the temptations of our life and for that which should be <term>rebirth</term> <note>German: *Wiedergeburt*; in the theology of this period, it refers to the inward spiritual transformation of a believer.</note>, spirit, and the <term>Kingdom of God</term> within us. When did he ever serve himself? With what word, or with what half-hearted pretense, did he seek his own comfort and honor? Why? Because he remained within the will of God! To do the Father's will was his food and drink. What he had begun in the spirit, he did not wish to complete in the flesh. He was the express image of [God's] pleasure, of the act of God. And when did he ever become a model for forced, dark, or mystical states? Where in his life is there any hint to follow such a path or to force oneself upon it? And yet, it was he who brought the entirety of rebirth and the fullness of the Kingdom of God! Imagine Jesus at his baptism, during his temptation: in the greatest darkness, how clearly determined, how bright with light! All arbitrary models and restrictions based on patterns of the imagination must vanish before you: for behold! the purest language of rebirth comes from this, and not from the dreams of beggars! Behold Christ!

Throughout the Gospel of John, therefore, the baptism of Jesus remains one of the primary moments of his life. "He comes with water and blood!" says the disciple in his letter—(an expression that was borrowed from the <term>Chaldean Sanctuary</term> <note>The author suggests a link between biblical imagery and ancient Near Eastern or Persian religious concepts.</note>). "He who comes with water and fire!" was the title of the serving priest <margin>l) See Volume III, *Zend-Avesta*, page 568. Raspe.</margin>. Water and blood, baptism and death, were the two sanctuaries of the life of Jesus.

This explains the disputed and misunderstood passage regarding the three witnesses—that Jesus is the Son of God—in heaven and on earth <margin>m) 1 John 5:6-8.</margin>. These must be actual events or persons, for opinions and customs—for example, the blood of martyrs or the baptismal ceremonies of Christians—cannot bear witness. And what is now more manifest than the three main events in the life of Jesus, which no one could deny and around which everything turns? The water, the baptism, where God himself testified of him as his Son: throughout the Gospel of John, Jesus appeals—

The early language used by Christians to describe baptism and rebirth revolves, in the manner shown above, around the images of Jesus's own baptism. It is not, says Peter, a mere rinsing away of external sins from the flesh <note>A reference to 1 Peter 3:21, which contrasts the "filth of the flesh" with the "pledge of a good conscience toward God."</note> (like the Great Flood or the baptism of John—physical impurities are not the point of contrast here); rather, just as the waters of the Great Flood once brought death to the flesh, **k)** and just as Christ was put to death in the flesh so that he might live for God in the Spirit: so does baptism attach and join one to God in all goodness through the resurrection of Jesus. As Paul often says, it opens heaven, unites one with Christ, transports one into a heavenly state, and serves as the sacrament and symbol of the killing of the earthly nature and the donning of Jesus’s kingdom of light and spiritual armor. Being enlightened through water, tasting the heavenly gift of the Spirit and the powers of the kingdom of heaven—these were the descriptions and experiences of baptism in the early Church. Should we really think of all these sayings and passages as referring merely to external confessions, documents, or at most, legal declarations?

*Clarifications on the New Testament*

### IV.
### The Works of Jesus.

To do the will of the Father ᵃ) was the reason the dearly beloved Son was here on earth: he rested upon it as if upon his very roots. In thoughts, wor<unclear>ds,</unclear> deeds, and in his entire purpose for the benefit of the world, he was the expressive image of the design and the all-working love of God for the spiritual bliss <note>The German *Glückseligkeit* refers to a state of supreme happiness or blessedness, often used in a theological context to describe the ultimate state of the soul.</note> of His creatures.

When he wished to explain to humanity the all-encompassing, infinitely kind activity and effect of his existence, he chose the most comprehensive, most effective, and yet the gentlest and most hidden metaphors. He spoke of the serpent in the wilderness ᵇ), <note>A reference to Numbers 21:8–9, where looking upon a bronze serpent brought healing, used by Jesus as a metaphor for his own lifting up on the cross.</note> of the water, bread, and drink of universal, inexhaustible eternal life, ᶜ) of the sap of the vine ᵈ) with which all its branches are one, of the shepherd ᵉ) who gives himself for the full happiness of his flock, of the forerunner ᶠ) who draws them all after him into the dwellings of God, and so forth. Every one of his words was a mustard seed in which the mystery of the kingdom of heaven rested.

From this kingdom, his miracles also flowed. They were performed neither to satisfy curiosity, nor as mere proofs of the miraculous—for which Jesus never performed them, no matter how much people demanded it—nor were they merely soft displays of affection or earthly medicines. Jesus performed them for faith: it was faith that was the hand that grasped the chain of fire <note>The "chain of fire" (*Feuerkette*) is a metaphor likely drawn from early scientific experiments with electricity, suggesting faith acts as a conductor for divine energy.</note> so that the heavenly spark could flow out. Jesus first forgave sins, and then he healed the illness; thus, they were demonstrations of the power of the kingdom of God in the spiritual realm, and the physical healing was the consequence. To those who grasped and touched him in faith, the gate of heavenly powers opened of its own accord.

<meta>This page transitions from a discussion of Jesus’s metaphors and the "kingdom of heaven" to a specific critical essay (Note 1) regarding contemporary philosophical attempts to explain away miracles using mechanical or deterministic theories.</meta>

## Notes.

### 1. On the Philosophical Theories of Miracles of our Time.

They seem to me mostly systematic word games, which push what they cannot explain to the end of the world and believe that it is now explained—or—that it is no longer there.

What is it supposed to mean: “here lay pre-established seeds <note>The author is likely critiquing the "Pre-established Harmony" theory of Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz, which argued that God designed the universe so that every event follows a predetermined internal program.</note> for the miracle!” or “a pre-established miracle was in the plan of creation here!” what is it supposed to mean? If the water were already pre-established wine, then it was not water, which everyone recognized it to be: the spring from which they brought it was certainly not a pre-established grapevine. If Lazarus was dead, truly dead, four days dead: then in his destroyed body there was death and not life. The miracle of the awakening could not have happened otherwise than through creation! through an actual new infusion of life!

Therefore, in the New Testament, the source of miraculous power is never pushed to the end of the world, but is pinned quite properly to the immediacy, the inwardness, and the activity of *this moment*, and therein precisely the miracle is placed. Jesus never performed signs in the heavens, <note>In the Gospels (e.g., Matthew 16:1), the religious authorities often demanded a "sign from heaven"—a spectacular celestial omen—which Jesus consistently refused to provide.</note> though the philosophers of his time demanded it often enough. Why? The event would have been something to marvel at, but not something that could be overseen or understood, and some pre-established fool of our time might have said: “what was the state of astronomy and physics back then! The sign was meant to show itself! Pre-established seeds lay there!” And there they may well have lain; but Jesus never performed such miracles. They were all within the human circle, involving present things: which everyone had seen before and saw now: the spark of effect jumped immediately into the eye. And it is precisely the spirit and purpose of the Evangelists, especially John, to properly highlight and note this immediacy and the striking nature of the word and the moment.

Our philosophical world-theories, which set everything mechanically from eternity and let it run like a roasting spit, <note>The German *Bratenwender* refers to a mechanical jack or turnspit used for roasting meat, symbolizing a purely clockwork, automated universe.</note> know better. They have established generalities, laws of nature, and the like, with which they calculate as if with numbers—and they let God calculate with them just as thoughtlessly, so that he might not break his own wooden clock and miscalculate.

<summary>This page presents a critique of Enlightenment-era "pre-established" theories of miracles, arguing that Jesus’s wonders were immediate acts of new creation rather than predetermined mechanical events.</summary>
<keywords>miracles, Leibniz, pre-established harmony, Lazarus, Gospel of John, laws of nature, deism, mechanical philosophy</keywords>

## The Works of Jesus.

To do the will of the Father ᵃ) was the reason the dearly beloved Son was here below: he rested upon it as if upon his own root. In his thoughts, wor<unclear>ds, and</unclear> deeds, and in his entire purpose for the betterment of the world, he was nothing but the expressed image of God’s design and of that all-working love intended for the happiness <note>The original "Glückseligkeit" implies a state of blessedness or supreme well-being beyond simple happiness.</note> of His creatures.

Therefore, when he wished to explain to people the all-encompassing, infinitely kind activity and effect of his existence, he chose the most comprehensive, most effective, and yet most gentle and hidden images. He spoke of the serpent in the wilderness ᵇ), of the water, bread, and drink of universal, inexhaustible eternal life ᶜ), of the sap of the vine ᵈ) with which all its branches are one, of the shepherd ᵉ) who gives himself for the full happiness of his flock, of the forerunner ᶠ) who draws them all after him into the dwellings of God, and so forth. Every one of his words was a mustard seed in which the mystery of the kingdom of heaven rested.

His miracles also sprang from this kingdom. They were intended neither for curiosity nor as mere proofs of the supernatural—for which Jesus never performed them, no matter how much people demanded it—nor were they merely sentimental acts of love or earthly medicines. Jesus performed them for faith: it was the hand that grasped the chain of fire, <note>The author uses the metaphor of a "Feuerkette" (fire-chain) to describe how faith acts as a conductor for divine energy, similar to an electrical circuit.</note> allowing the heavenly spark to flow out. Jesus first forgave sins and then he healed the sickness: thus, the miracles were demonstrations of the Kingdom of God in the spiritual realm, and the physical healing was the consequence. To whoever grasped and touched him in faith, the gate of heavenly powers opened of its own accord.

<meta>This page continues the author’s discussion of Jesus’s miracles, moving from the symbolic descriptions on the previous page to a direct critique of Enlightenment-era philosophers who tried to explain away the "immediate" power of the divine through mechanical or "pre-established" natural laws.</meta>

# Notes.

### 1. On the Philosophical Theories of Miracles of Our Time.

They seem to me mostly systematic wordplay; they take what they cannot explain and push it off to the "end of the world," believing that now it is explained—or that it is simply no longer there.

What is it supposed to mean when they say: "Here lay pre-established seeds for the miracle!" or "In the plan of creation, there was a pre-established miracle here!" <note>The author is critiquing a common 18th-century philosophical view (likely influenced by Leibniz’s "pre-established harmony") that miracles were not interruptions of nature, but events "pre-programmed" into the universe's clockwork from the beginning.</note> What is that supposed to mean? If the water was already pre-established wine, then it was not water, though everyone recognized it as such; the spring from which they drew the water was certainly not a pre-established grapevine. If Lazarus was dead, truly dead, dead for four days: then in his decayed body there was death and not life. The miracle of the resurrection could not have happened otherwise than through creation! Through a real, new quickening! <note>The term "quickening" (Belebung) refers to the act of bringing something to life.</note>

For this reason, in the New Testament, the source of miraculous power is never pushed off to the end of the world, but is quite properly tethered to the immediate, the intimate, and the active power of the present moment; it is in this very thing that the miracle is placed. Jesus never performed signs in the heavens, though the philosophers of his time demanded it often enough. Why? Such an event would have been a cause for wonder, but it could not have been overlooked or truly understood; and a pre-established fool of our time might have said: "What was the state of astronomy and physics back then! The sign was meant to show itself! There lay the pre-established seeds!" And there they may well have lain; but Jesus never performed such miracles. They were all within the human circle, involving present things that everyone had seen before and saw now: the spark of the effect leaped immediately into the eye. And it is precisely the spirit and purpose of the Evangelists, especially John, to clearly highlight and note this immediacy and the striking nature of the word and the moment.

Our philosophical theories of the world, which mechanically set everything in place from eternity and let it run like a mechanical turnspit, <note>A "Bratenwender" was a clockwork-like device used to rotate meat over a fire; here, it serves as a metaphor for a universe that functions like a machine without divine intervention.</note> know better. They have established generalities, "Laws of Nature," and the like, with which they calculate as if with numbers—and they let God calculate with them just as thoughtlessly, so that He might not break His wooden clock or miscalculate.

<vocab>Philosophical theories of miracles, pre-established seeds, creation, Lazarus, miraculous power, New Testament, Evangelists, John, Laws of Nature</vocab>

<summary>The author critiques contemporary philosophers who use the concept of "pre-established" natural laws to explain away miracles, arguing instead that the miracles of Jesus were immediate acts of divine creation occurring within the human sphere.</summary>
<keywords>miracles, philosophy of religion, Enlightenment critique, Lazarus, Gospel of John, Laws of Nature, mechanical world view</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s philosophical defense of miracles. Having previously rejected the idea that miracles were "pre-programmed" into nature's clockwork, the author now argues that Jesus’s power comes from an immediate, eternal connection to God, requiring only the "receptivity" of faith to manifest.</meta>

<header>* C J *</header>

reckon. Miserable clouds of generalities! We are like flies who do not know the true nature of beings; we dance and wear ourselves out on surfaces, measuring masses and appearances with our fly-eyes, and think ourselves wonders of wisdom when we have added up a few numbers and produced a schoolboy’s total. <note>The author uses "Knabenfacit" (schoolboy's total) to mock philosophers who think complex reality can be reduced to simple math.</note> The Creator sees forces, beings, individual powers; with Him, every moment is present without the constraints of time or space—that is how He acts. The Son can do nothing of himself, for what he sees the Father doing *now!* in an eternal Now, that the Son does also. Just as the Father raises the dead, gives life, and acts unswervingly through all spaces and times of creation: so also the Son. a) He is in heaven, b) eternally in heaven; the angels and powers of God flow out of him into all beings c)—this is Jesus’s only theory of miracles. He is no "pre-established" magician <note>A reference to "pre-established harmony," a theory by Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz suggesting God set the universe in motion like a perfect clock.</note> who merely eavesdrops on and steals from nature: but the Son of God! The present image of the Almighty, the All-active, the Omnipresent. Everything he did was a miracle, God’s power reigning over all the world.

But why weren’t signs (<term>signs</term> <note>original: "σημεῖα"</note>, <term>wonders</term> <note>original: "τέρατα"</note>) visible at every single moment? Jesus said it often enough: it lacked the point of contact, the vessel into which he could pour it, the pure receptivity—of faith. Yes! Of faith—and I dare to use that mocked, misunderstood word. Jesus did not demand reasoning mental powers (which is to say, folly and doubt), nor a "beautiful moral uprightness"—for the Pharisees had plenty of both—but rather faith. "You could not perform the miracle because of your unbelief! He himself could not perform many miracles among his countrymen because of their unbelief!" Unbelief, then, locked everything. Faith, however, opened the door to life itself: it grasped the chain and the miraculous spark of healing jumped across. "Who touched me? Be of good cheer, my daughter! (Not I) your faith has helped you! The leper came and gave thanks. Go, says Jesus, and praise God! (Not I) your faith has helped you!" Nothing, therefore, does Jesus praise so much

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding Jesus’s miracles. ->* ( ✠ ) *<-

...very much, and he demands it, praises it, and promises everything to it—that is, Faith. Faith was the pure contact and the grasping of God’s presence, the hand of all the soul’s reaching powers when they took hold of God. It was in Jesus, the author and finisher of faith <note>A reference to Hebrews 12:2, "Jesus the author and finisher of our faith."</note>, in whom the Godhead dwelt entirely, undivided, and bodily. It was in his disciples, upon whom the gifts of the Spirit rested, who were just becoming accustomed to feeling the Godhead ever more fully and completely present to them. It was in the one who received the miracle, when he saw clearly that the Godhead stood before him, and believed. Through faith, he passed over into the kingdom of heaven; to him came healing.

Let us note a few moments of Jesus’s miracles, without which we would view them all merely as "prodigies" <note>The author uses the Latin-derived "Prodigien" to mean mere spectacles or hollow marvels without spiritual substance.</note> (and that is truly not much!). The first miracle that Jesus performed was upon an inanimate thing where there could be no resistance. He did it when his time came, but still—it was the faith of Mary that awakened him as the external receptivity. —

Jesus drove out demons without demanding or expecting faith. The sick man was bound; he was a mere object of compassion, like the dead. From those who brought him, however, he demanded faith. When he performed no miracles in Nazareth because of their unbelief, he healed those with severe illnesses who were close to death by the laying on of hands.

“Unless you see signs and wonders, you will not believe!” Jesus thus actually required a faith without miracles: he performed the miracles only to accustom people to a more difficult, unearthly, spiritual faith. Indeed, the physical here was also only a consequence of the invisible. “Your sins are forgiven you!” Jesus said first, before he spoke: “Rise and walk!” The examples of the possessed show this most clearly, and he also brings them most into the light: that in all miracles he was acting upon an invisible realm of good or evil forces. What he loosed on Earth had to first be loose in Heaven. <note>A variation on Matthew 16:19, emphasizing that the spiritual reality precedes the physical manifestation.</note>

“The man believed, and at that hour the fever left him.” The example of this royal official (<term>basilikos</term> <note>original: "βασιλικὸς", referring to a royal official or nobleman, often identified with the centurion in other accounts</note>), which all four Evangelists tell, each in his own way, brings it to light with soldierly strength and simplicity: “that Jesus’s word, even more than the command of a subordinate soldier, must inevitably take effect even at a distance.”

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the necessity of faith as the receptive vessel for divine miracles. Having established that Jesus required faith to act, the author now explores the internal nature of that faith—its simplicity and the grave consequences of consciously rejecting the "light of conviction" (the sin against the Holy Spirit).</meta>

->* (  ) *<-

And Jesus praises that great faith—which was still new back then (at the beginning of his ministry!)—as a confidence that was still foreign and difficult even for the disciples themselves. He sought nothing more, and found nothing harder to cultivate, than this eternal, lasting, inward confidence in God that beholds him even in the darkness. It drove out vice and malice just as much as it did the sickly uncertainty and rationalizing brooding of the soul. For this reason, Jesus also chose the simple and the unlearned—the strong simplicity of believing disciples. In a man of worldly wisdom, that openness of countenance, mind, and heart is lost—an openness that, in a child-like angel, is the foundation of all primary goodness. Around Jesus there floated total simplicity, an immediate feeling of God’s presence that captured hearts and awakened confidence.

Where prejudices, vices, or hard barriers existed so that he could not achieve his purpose, there Jesus punished unbelief, hardness of heart, doubting, and the weakness of believing powers. But when his enemies once tried to pass off his divine works as the works of the devil, that was the sin against the Holy Spirit—against the Word within them. It was an unforgivable, knowing malice, which he explicitly distinguishes from sin against the exterior of his person and what belongs to it (and thus also against the exterior appearance of his miracles). We have so over-defined this sin that, thank God, it supposedly can no longer be committed, because no Son of Man now stands before us performing miracles. This is exactly contrary to Jesus's own distinction. In our time, too, many sins and even blasphemies against the Son of Man—that is, against the person of Christ—can occur out of hard, insurmountable prejudices, and these receive forgiveness. But the sin against the Holy Spirit—knowing malice against the pressing light of conviction, in whatever form it may appear (even if, admittedly, not as pure, powerful, and clear as it was before the face of the Son of God!)—that is what Jesus places under an irrevocable curse. The person robs themselves of the very means of truth. They tear out their own eye; how then can they see and be healed?

The omnipotence <term>omnipotence: the quality of having unlimited power</term> that Jesus ascribes to prayer and faith, especially to united prayer, also becomes clear through this. For faith unites itself with him and enters into the center of the immed= <note>The catchword "unmit=" likely completes to "unmittelbaren" (immediate) on the following page.</note>
