...thought, it is not expected at all, and how could, how should Josephus <note>Flavius Josephus, the first-century Jewish historian. The author suggests Josephus didn't record certain miracles because they didn't fit his historical framework.</note> even think in his history of such a thing that was not even there?

John’s style of writing is a **Chaldaism** <note>original: *Chaldaismus*; a term used by 18th-century scholars to describe the Aramaic or Eastern linguistic and cultural influence on the Gospel of John.</note>. “There God works in everything through angels. All His powers of blessing are angels: every element has its own, and the angel who has power over the water (see the **Revelation of John**!) is one of the first. Since, as we have seen, according to their natural philosophy the water flowed from the throne of the Highest as the life-source of the entire earthly creation—full of fertility, revitalization, prosperity, and recovery—so, they say <margin>e)</margin>, at times His angel descends from the throne of God to stir it; there the healing powers surge! Creation is renewed!” And the angel works, according to that very **Zend-Avesta** <note>original: *Zend-Avesta*; the primary collection of religious texts of Zoroastrianism. The author links Persian ideas of elemental spirits to the Gospel's imagery.</note>, through the stirring wind and so forth. Behold! The entire mystery is there. Whether there were many or few healing powers in the pool, one now understands the miracle and the speech of Jesus, which builds precisely upon this all-working and all-stirring of the Father through all times and spaces of creation. Otherwise, one does not grasp how he now comes to the idea that he awakens the dead and pours life-force into everything: one sees it! He makes himself into the express image of God’s working, and the Jews understood him quite correctly: “he makes himself equal to God!” <note>A reference to John 5:18.</note>—that is, into the visible impulse of the all-animation, all-action, and all-blessing, though in the most silent rest <margin>f)</margin>.

2. Jesus cursed a fig tree; people have been very annoyed by this, and especially since it was explicitly not the time for figs to be present, they have mutilated the text. This is quite unnecessary. **Shaw** <note>Thomas Shaw (1694–1751), an English traveler whose accounts of the Levant were often used to provide geographical and botanical context for the Bible.</note> cites a type of fig that does not fall off but remains on the tree throughout the year <margin>g)</margin>. Jesus sought such a "latecomer," knowing well that it was not yet time for fresh figs, and since he found none (he never performed a miracle for his own hunger!), he established in it a monument to the people, upon whom God also sought even miserable latecomers in vain.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the linguistic and cultural context of the New Testament, specifically linking the descriptions of demonic possession to the Zoroastrian traditions of the "Zend-Avesta" mentioned on the previous page. It explores whether these miracles should be understood as literal spiritual battles or medical treatments.</meta>

N 2

3. Concerning the **possessed**, there is the greatest cause for offense. One hears now that in the language of the Orient—in the **Zend-Avesta** <note>original: *Zend-Avesta*; the primary collection of religious texts of Zoroastrianism. The author suggests a cultural link between Persian demonology and the language used in the Gospels.</note>, for example—absolutely all diseases are called sins, works, and children of demons. One finds that the entire language of the Evangelists prevails here: spirits <margin>h) see: *Dews, Daroudjs, people of Ahriman* etc. <note>In Persian mythology, Dews (Daevas) and Daroudjs (Druj) are demonic spirits serving Ahriman, the principle of evil.</note></margin> of disease that make one blind, deaf, mute, or weak; spirits that love deserts, graves, corpses, rot, and unclean animals. Even the expression **to bind** <margin>i) Vol. III, p. 133-142.</margin> is characteristic of it: for instance, a barren woman is said to be "one whose side is bound by the demon," and his prayers (**Nerengs** and **Tavids** <note>Zoroastrian liturgical prayers and formulas used for protection or exorcism.</note>) "loosen the bound limb" and "bind the demon." How some will then triumph: "There you see it! Nothing but devil-work, Chaldean nonsense!"

I, however, believe that these very expressions should convince us all the more that Christ and the Apostles, in their own era, truly meant **demons** and not mere allegorical clothing. The devils know Jesus and name him, as if from another realm; he rebukes them, telling them to be silent, and so forth. All of this is either a deception and the grossest superstition—or it is the **truth**; I see no third option. And I have already spoken further on this above. Moreover, the appearance of these cases was, without any need for scholarly demonstration, **sickness**: the Evangelist lists the possessed under no other category than that of the most wretchedly ill. Jesus heals them as such, and even if he had healed nothing in them but **epileptics**, **lunatics**, and **melancholic maniacs** <note>original Latin: *maniacos melancholicos*.</note> (as Dr. Alder was already able to say in Toulouse in 1723, and yet was not burned like Calas <note>Jean Calas (1698–1762) was a French merchant who was tortured and executed in a famous case of religious intolerance; the author notes that Dr. Alder survived his own controversial medical claims about possession.</note>)—then they were outwardly complete miracles, just as the recoveries from other diseases were miracles. A spirit of interpretation, however, that allows itself **fictions** <note>original: *Figmente*.</note> here, will allow them in everything. Generally, the entire perception of the **miracles of Jesus** descends into a fog if one always insists on viewing them only as portents <note>original Latin: *Portente*; meaning signs, wonders, or omens.</note>, from the outermost, shallowest, sensory end, and fails to perceive what concepts **He** connected with them.

<summary>The author argues that the Gospel accounts of demonic possession should be taken literally rather than as mere allegories, despite their linguistic similarities to Zoroastrian (Persian) medical and spiritual beliefs. He asserts that while the possessed were truly ill, Jesus's healings remain miracles regardless of whether they are viewed through a spiritual or medical lens.</summary>

<keywords>demonic possession, Zend-Avesta, Zoroastrianism, miracles of Jesus, medical history, exorcism, Jean Calas, Chaldean influence, spiritual vs. physical illness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the theological reflection on the nature of Jesus’s miracles. While the previous page addressed the cultural and linguistic context of "possession" and demonic illness, this page transitions into the spiritual purpose of Christ’s work, emphasizing that his physical miracles were merely outward signs of a much larger, eternal transformation of the human spirit.</meta>

<header>->☩ ( ) ☩<-</header>

Thus we arrive, as shown above, precisely at the point that everything happened in the Spirit; that it was his lasting, ongoing miracle to do the works of his Father and to destroy the works of the devil. Had it been possible and according to God’s order, he would have filled everything at once with bliss, life, and recovery; but now, because the **Prince of the World** <note>A traditional title for Satan, frequently used in the Gospel of John to describe the spiritual power opposing Christ's mission.</note> still reigned, he could only pour drops from his sea onto receptive places here and there. He gathered for himself **firstlings** <note>original: *Erstlinge*; a biblical term for the "first fruits" of a harvest, referring here to the first group of disciples and believers.</note>, whom he drew to himself, transfigured into his image, and in whom he wished to leave behind a seed—this he calls his **God-work**! This is the ongoing, greatest, and most misunderstood miracle. It remains so until the development of the world at the end of days. The visible miracles merely flowed from this.

### 2. Concerning the metaphors under which Jesus spoke of his work.

Since it was a work that reached beyond the human race into infinity and eternity, no human eye could oversee it, and no human word could express it. Jesus, therefore, gave only **images** and **parables**, but those of the greatest and clearest substance.

His primary sentiment was: **The Father’s will!** To distribute a higher life, to save sinners, and to make the unfortunate happy in a more spiritual existence and the Kingdom of Heaven. It was for this that he appeared! Through this he overcame temptation; he was like his Father: an all-encompassing, misunderstood goodness and love, working in the highest tranquility. **The Father’s will!** is the watchword of his angels and of those earthly members whom he brings to life as angels. **The Father’s will!** shall one day be the word of life and bliss for the dead, and the elevation of his own into his glory and delight k).

Soon he becomes the life of the world, the original seed of its higher creation! Soon the physician, to heal the sick; and because he appeared so inconspicuously, and worked recovery only through obscure faith,

<margin>
k) Already in **Psalm 40**, the arrival of Jesus was announced under this expression, where "doing the will" can no more mean "sacrifices" than it does in the entire New Testament. What a different substance this word has when one reads the whole of **John**! His life was the expressed living image of the Father’s will.
</margin>

It emphasizes that visible miracles were secondary to the eternal work of establishing the Kingdom of Heaven.</summary>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the spiritual metaphors used by Jesus. While the previous page focused on the "God-work" of transfiguring humanity and the broader context of his miracles, this page specifically examines the cultural and "Chaldean" (ancient Near Eastern) origins of the imagery found in the Gospel of John, such as the Bread of Life and the Water of Immortality.</meta>

(A beautiful image!) the serpent of Moses in the wilderness. Soon the shepherd, who with the sacrifice of his life leads his flocks to eternal pasture, and (the most striking images!) the bread of immortality! the water of life! — Since both images are so dear to the Evangelist John and he develops so much from them, yet they are also understood least in their fullness: let us hear their origin and their meaning.

In Chaldea <note>The author uses "Chaldea" to refer generally to the ancient Mesopotamian and Persian regions, which 18th-century scholars often linked to the mystical background of the New Testament.</note> there was indeed a bread of life, a water of immortality, of which people spoke in the very same terms that the Evangelist uses. The former was called heavenly and life-giving bread <note>original: "pain celeste, plein de vie"</note>, the symbol of blessing, life, and the communion of all heavenly good spirits—that is, of purity, life-forces, and prosperity. Indeed, this very bread was their ancient **Hom** <note>original: "Hom"; likely a reference to *Haoma*, the sacred plant and ritual drink of ancient Persian/Zoroastrian tradition.</note> of John. (Pure Hom, golden Hom, full of life! <note>original: "Hom pur, Hom d' or, plein de vie"</note>) —) Now, it has already been noted above how John wrote his Gospel against the belief that the Baptist—who was presumably conflated with this **Hom** of life by his disciples—was the Savior of the world: therefore, the Evangelist applies all these images to Jesus. — That the image is not taken from the Manna of the Jews is testified by Jesus himself, as he distinguishes it from the Manna.

Now, this food of life in the sanctuaries of Chaldea was sometimes bread, sometimes flesh and drink <margin>l) Volume III, pp. 727, 728. m) *Darouns* were the names of the breads, *Miezd* the food and drink <note>These are terms from Zoroastrian liturgy; *Dron* (Darun) is a ceremonial bread and *Myazda* is a communal meal.</note>.</margin>. One sees, therefore, how John could make the transition—which seems harsh to us—from the enjoying of bread to the eating and drinking of the flesh and blood in the Son of Man. In his language, it was not a transition at all <margin>n) John 6.</margin>.

Finally, and most significantly, the waters of life are not merely, as they are often interpreted, the bubbling waters of any ordinary well: rather (as the Revelation of John and the Chaldean prophets <margin>o) Revelation 22; Ezekiel 47; Zechariah 14:8-10.</margin> can already show us!) they are the water of immortality, the sap from the tree of life.

<summary>This page explores the ancient Near Eastern and Zoroastrian linguistic roots of the Gospel of John's imagery, specifically regarding the "Bread of Life" and "Water of Immortality." The author argues that John repurposed these "Chaldean" concepts to distinguish Jesus's divine role from that of John the Baptist.</summary>

<keywords>Gospel of John, Chaldea, Bread of Life, Water of Immortality, Haoma, John the Baptist, Eucharist, Zoroastrianism, Manna, Biblical metaphors</keywords>

“The ancient description of the world in Chaldea, for instance, imagined water 
“as a heavenly element flowing from the throne of Ormuzd <note>The author uses 'Ormuzd' for Ahura Mazda, the supreme deity of Zoroastrianism.</note>. Upon the 
“holy mountains it had its descent, the fountain of immortality, 
“where they imagined the center of the world. There was the palace and 
“the mother of streams; from there it poured forth—pregnant with the 
“seeds of life for all things—around the earth, over the entire nature of Ormuzd, 
“and gave life to trees, animals, and humans. In the midst of this spring, 
“growing from its innermost seed and sap, was the plant of 
“immortality (Hom, who drives away death <note>original: "Hom, qui eloigne la mort"</note>, thus once again John), the king 
“of all trees. Its sap they drank as a holy symbol: it will 
“give life to the dead on the last day, and so forth.” p) Now 
one can first begin to understand John, Ezekiel, Zechariah, and the 
Revelation, each in its own way—and how magnificent the image of 
Jesus becomes! *If you knew the gift of God, and who it is 
who says to you, ‘Give me a drink,’ you would have asked him and he would 
have given you living water. Whoever drinks of the water that I 
give him will never thirst; but the water that I 
shall give him will become in him a fountain of life 
welling up into eternal life. Whoever believes in me, from 
him shall flow new streams of living water.* This 
he said, however, concerning the Spirit, and so forth. q). According to this 
all-encompassing image, Jesus becomes the center of the world to be enlivened, 
the sea, the outflow of heaven, the primal source of all 
streams flowing therefrom. r). In each of his members the stream becomes a new, 
unfailing primal source, flowing onto others and streaming into eternal life.

<column-break/>

p) Zor. T. III. 633. Ardouiss. 695. Hom, tree. <note>These are references to 18th-century scholarship on the Zend-Avesta, the primary collection of religious texts of Zoroastrianism.</note>

q) John 4, 14. Ch. 7, 37. 38.

r) Albordj <note>The Alborz mountains in Persian mythology.</note>, the holy mountains were imagined as the center of the world (<term>navel of the world</term> <note>original: "umbilicus", "μεσος ομφαλος" (mesos omphalos)</note>), just as the sources of the Nile were for the Egyptians, the sources of the Ganges for the Indians, or Jerusalem according to the imagery of Zechariah and Ezekiel. There was Ardouissour <note>Ardvī Sūrā Anāhitā, the Zoroastrian divinity of the Waters.</note>, the mother of streams, the great Abyss, which in Aristotle is still called the <term>interior cavity</term> <note>original: "κοιλια" (koilia)</note>. (Meteorol. c. 13. p. m. 26.) This primal source of life is Jesus here, who flows so much into all his members that in each one a new spring (<term>inner source</term> <note>original: "κοιλια" (koilia)</note>) of running streams arises. It is unbearable when the ignorant fools translate this as "belly," "navel," or "private parts." <note>The author is criticizing literalist translations of John 7:38, which says "out of his belly (koilia) shall flow rivers of living water." He argues the word refers to an inner, mystical spring based on ancient cosmology.</note>

<meta>This page continues the author’s exploration of the Gospel of John through the lens of Ancient Near Eastern and Persian (Zoroastrian) thought. Having previously discussed the "Water of Life" as a cosmic element, the author now transitions to explaining the metaphysical meaning of the "Name of Jesus" and the specific linguistic roots of the imagery used in the Fourth Gospel.</meta>

->* ( ) *<-

Let us explain another way of speaking that is just as misunderstood and gives us the greatest concept of the work of Jesus: it is **his Name in all those who are his**. According to the way of thinking in the Orient <note>German: *Morgenland*</note>, every spirit, angel, soul, and good or evil being has its **name** <s>—that is, its office, its position, the means through which it is invoked, appears, and acts. This is the primary concept of this word. The people who cast out demons in the name of Jesus did so in his power, by invoking his name; thus, as Jesus also accepts it, they acted in his stead. When they succeeded in the miracle, they always bore witness to the presence and the effective power of his kingdom. When Jesus spoke, taught, or performed miracles in the name of his Father, he explained clearly enough that it was through His power, out of and for God's kingdom, in God's stead, and not in his own. Therefore, to pray to God in the name of Jesus cannot mean that I simply append and use the expression “in the name of Jesus”; rather, it means praying in his power! With his purity, intention, and effect—in short, in his stead! To baptize in the name of Jesus, to perform something, and so forth, is not merely to do something with his permission—and why should I multiply examples? —

<column-break/>

...places <note>The sentence continues from the previous page's column</note> (John 7:37, 38) and picks apart with great emphasis. A hollow reservoir of waters <note>original Latin: *conceptaculum aquarum*</note> is called *koilia* <note>original Greek: *κοιλια*, usually translated as "belly" or "womb," but here interpreted as a hollow space or cistern</note>, and even the belly only takes its name from that. In John 4:14, Jesus explained it himself: “the water that I shall give him will become in him a new spring, welling up into eternal life,” where even the expression *hallomenon* <note>original Greek: *αλλομενον*, meaning "leaping" or "springing up"</note> is neither Latin nor German, but a true Chaldaic idiom <note>The author refers to the influence of "Chaldean" (Aramaic/Mesopotamian) linguistic structures on the Greek of the New Testament</note>. Also here, in the works of Zoroaster, *aller* <note>French: to go/flow</note> and *marcher* <note>French: to march/move</note> are always used for the concept of "fullness." *To make the springs, the soul, the life, the fruits, the pleasures flow* <note>original French: *Faire aller les sources, l'ame, la vie, les fruits, les plaisirs*</note> is, according to him, the business of all good spirits within their elements (Volume II, pages 81, 83). *It goes and goes* <note>original French: *Il va et va*</note> is the idiom for: it wells up! it rises! it grows ever higher! —

Even in Hesychius <note>A 5th-century Greek grammarian and lexicographer</note> (Volume II, page 294, Alberti edition), I suspect an uncorrected passage under the entry for *koilias*. What is the meaning of *koilia* as a "conduit in Cyrene"? <note>original Greek: *κοιλια οχετος εν Κυρηνη*</note> Since everyone understands *koilia* as "mistress of conduits," "validation," <note>original Greek: *κοιλια οχετων κυρια, κυρωσις*</note> or something similar: in Aristotle, *koilia* refers to the Abyss <note>original Latin: *Abyssus*</note>, the mother of streams. John therefore uses once again the most comprehensive, greatest image—sacred to the Chaldeans—and the most worthy expression.

<s> See the *Yashts* <note>original: *Jeschts*</note>, that is, the invocations of the heavenly names, III. 143-314, and the list of the powers and offices of each. The name of **Ormuzd** <note>The supreme deity in Zoroastrianism, Ahura Mazda</note> and of his Word is the most effective and the first. The manifold use of the word "Name" among the Jews takes its definition from this, with the help of the Old Testament.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the "Name of Jesus," transitioning from the linguistic and cultural origins of the term to its practical application in Christian life and ethics. It builds on the previous page's argument that the "Name" represents a state of being and power rather than a mere title.</meta>

### V. The

->* ( ) *<-

No small duty of life exists that Jesus has not ennobled with this high sentiment: that it can be performed *in him!* and *in his name.* And in whose name someone performs a deed, they shall also receive that person's reward. The "cup of cold water" <note>A reference to Matthew 10:42: "And whoever gives only a cup of cold water to one of these little ones because he is a disciple... he shall by no means lose his reward."</note> shall be repaid, not merely as an external act of charity, but because it was given "in the name of the righteous man" or "the prophet"—that is, according to the power, disposition, and level of purity and love with which I offer it. The highest level of purity, love, humility—and therefore also of impact, power, and reward—is the Name of Jesus. "Whoever welcomes a child in my name, welcomes me; and whoever welcomes me, welcomes him who sent me!" <note>Mark 9:37; here the author emphasizes that the "Name" acts as a spiritual conduit between the believer, Christ, and God.</note> No higher sentiment of union, purity, reward, power, or efficacy could be granted!

"You did this for me!" <note>Referring to the "Sheep and the Goats" in Matthew 25:40: "Truly I tell you, whatever you did for one of the least of these brothers and sisters of mine, you did for me."</note> is the only formula of recognition on that final day; every other work is lost. We should not merely imitate individual actions of Jesus externally, but rather *be* in him—vines drawing from his sap, members of his spirit, springs flowing from his source. Even there, all separation should vanish; in every good act we are capable of, we should flow together as if we were but one in Him. We should seek the "True" rather than various "truths"—that is, to recognize the undivided, good God everywhere, and to love "the Good" itself rather than just "good things," seeing in all of them the single, undivided Jesus. "That they all may be one, even as you, Father, are in me and I in them!" <note>John 17:21</note>—this becomes the very spirit, doctrine, sap, and strength of Christianity. Thus the existence, the love, and the work of Jesus were undivided and active for all! In this way, through purity and love in the name of Jesus, I can overcome the whole world and, through all my brothers, perform the good that I cannot accomplish alone.

<vocab>Jesus, duty, reward, Name of Jesus, purity, love, humility, union, Christianity</vocab>

<summary>This page interprets the "Name of Jesus" as a spiritual disposition of purity and love that ennobles even the smallest deeds. It argues for a mystical union where believers act not by external imitation, but as part of a single, undivided "spirit and sap" flowing from Christ.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the mystical union with the "Name of Jesus" described on the previous page to a concise summary of His actual doctrine. It concludes the previous line of thought regarding the internal spiritual state and begins Section V, focusing on the character of Jesus’s teaching and the function of the Lord’s Prayer as a model for the Christian soul.</meta>

<header>Explanations of the N. T. Notes=</header>

->* ( ⚜ ) *<-

### V.
## The Teaching and the Prayer of Jesus.

That [teaching] is very brief: be heaven and not earth! Be, like God, active and kind and hidden, and learn it from me, His image! Let your essence be life, love, humility, and the path thither self-denial, purification, death!

Far indeed, infinitely far, is the notion that Jesus became a lawgiver of a merely external, civic virtue as such; he always casts such things aside as pagan virtues, as raw earthly dross. "Do not begin with them! Do not dwell on them! If you strive only for them, then your reward is gone!" <note>The author suggests that focusing on outward "good citizen" behavior misses the internal spiritual transformation Jesus required.</note> What then? And what if, in a particular age, one wishes to know Christ as nothing other than a lawgiver, a teacher of virtue, or a unifier of religions? If the salt is tasteless <note>original: "dumm"; in the German of this period, "dumm" (foolish) was used to translate the Greek *mōranthē* in Matthew 5:13, which refers to salt losing its saltiness or becoming "insipid."</note>, says Jesus, and has lost its inner, seasoning power, then let it be thrown out and let the people trample it.

Kingdom of God! Kingdom of Heaven! An unshakeable, hidden life in Him with the kind, powerful, all-filling Godhead—that is the root of His teaching and duties. This is shown by His excellent prayer <note>The Lord's Prayer (the Our Father).</note>, the best image of a Christian's soul, as it should be at every moment with every deed.

<meta>This page transitions from the general spiritual principles of Jesus’s teaching (the "Kingdom of Heaven" as a state of being) to a specific scholarly commentary on the Gospel of Matthew and the linguistic roots of the Sermon on the Mount.</meta>

<header>Notes.</header>

### I. On Jesus’s Sermon on the Mount.

I have explained so little of the Evangelist Matthew as of yet that it pleases me to dedicate this entire section to him. He is the most down-to-earth, just as John is the most spiritual of the Evangelists. In him, the Galilean speaks truly; and if he indeed wrote his Gospel in the actual mother tongue of Jesus—the Syro-Chaldaic dialect <note>This refers to Aramaic, the Semitic language spoken by Jesus and his disciples.</note>—it was expressed entirely in the simple idiom of the common people. The single discourse that I am now explaining—one should consult the commentators on Judaism for this a)—was folksy in every expression! Even Zoroaster <note>The ancient Persian prophet; the author is suggesting that many "Jewish" concepts of the time were influenced by Persian/Zoroastrian thought during the Babylonian exile.</note> stands as a witness. Most of these expressions are found in him, in his formulas and prayers; thus one sees from whence Pharisaism <note>The religious movement of the Pharisees, emphasizing strict adherence to traditional and written law.</note> derived its works and its essence.

I will no longer repeat the terms "Kingdom of God" or "Kingdom of Heaven"; I will only note immediately that the name "Righteousness" (for example, of the Kingdom of Heaven) does not mean what many understand by it in a legalistic sense. Instead, it signifies the fulfillment of the Law specifically through love, mercy, and charity. In the Apocryphal writings, in Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize the Torah with Greek philosophy.</note>, etc., the word appears merely to mean alms-giving, or good-naturedness and purity of heart. Even the temple of the Chaldean religion was called simply the "Place of Righteousness" b), that is, the place where they were meant to appear perfect, like Ormuzd <note>original: "Ormuzd"; also known as Ahura Mazda, the supreme god of Zoroastrianism representing light and goodness.</note>. And even this "perfection" was no metaphysical virtue as we interpret it, but rather goodness of heart, forgiveness, purity, and gentleness. A king, for example, should be more perfect and greater than all seven spirits of heaven <note>In Zoroastrianism, these are the Amesha Spentas, the "Bounteous Immortals" who represent divine attributes.</note>—that is, equal in goodness, purity, and blessing to God, the Primal Father and Primal King. This is the concept of Jesus’s Sermon on the Mount, as anyone can find by comparison and within the context of the meaning (Chapter 5, 6, 10, 23, 24, 44, 45, 58; and Chapter 6:1, where the variant reading is found). Even the Pharisaic concept of holiness through works is

<columns>2</columns>

a) Specifically, Lightfoot and Schöttgen <note>John Lightfoot and Christian Schöttgen were prominent 17th and 18th-century scholars who wrote "Hebraica et Talmudica" commentaries, illustrating the New Testament using Jewish tradition.</note> on the Sermon on the Mount and Matthew.

<column-break/>

b) *Derimher* <note>original: "Derimher"; likely a transliteration of the Persian *Dar-e Mehr*, meaning "Gate of Mithra" or "Gate of Justice/Grace," referring to a Zoroastrian temple.</note>. The word "just" is described in the Zend-Avesta <note>The primary collection of religious texts of Zoroastrianism.</note> as that which is not too much and not too little.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the term "Righteousness" from the previous page, arguing that its biblical meaning is less legalistic and more about moral character than later medieval interpretations suggest.</meta>

[Righteousness] is not nearly as legalistic as it was made by the monk- and feudal times, where everything rested upon the recognition of justice before a court. I wish that someone would demonstrate this with strength and scholarship.

To be "poor in spirit" means to be humble and modest, even if—as in the Gospel of Luke—the word "poor" stood alone <note>original: c)</note>. <margin>c) *ptōchos* <note>original: $\pi\tau\omega\chi\acute{o}\varsigma$</note> is the term used in Hellenistic Greek on its own. See I. H. Michaelis on the usage of the 70 <note>The Septuagint, the earliest Greek translation of the Old Testament</note> etc.</margin> Jesus never preached an earthly poverty or monastic abstinence without spiritual purpose; the blessedness of this poverty also stands among several other purely spiritual duties. — To be "pure in heart" is, in Zoroaster’s teachings, the luminous quality of the highest spirit at the throne who beholds God <note>original: d)</note>: <margin>d) **Bahman**, Holy disposition of the heart. <note>Bahman, or Vohu Manah, is the Zoroastrian "archangel" associated with the "Good Mind" and purity.</note></margin> that is also the promise here. He of the "lowly heart" <note>original: e)</note> <margin>e) **Sapandomad**, an excellent and servant-like disposition of the heart. Vol. II p. 81. <note>Sapandomad, or Spenta Armaiti, is the Zoroastrian spirit associated with "Holy Devotion" and the Earth.</note></margin> is his angel of the Earth, and possesses the land <note>original: f)</note> <margin>f) To be at large, to have spacious and fertile places.</margin> — a common folk expression for happiness, space, and breadth! — "Peacemaker" is the expression translated from the Chaldaic <note>The author uses "Chaldaic" to refer to Aramaic, the language spoken by Jesus.</note> for a spreader of bliss, prosperity, and peace; these were, according to the actual predicate, "children of God" <note>original: g)</note>, <margin>g) See Chief, King, people of Ormuzd <note>Ormuzd, or Ahura Mazda, is the supreme creator deity in Zoroastrianism.</note>, cooperators. Vol. III p. 607-609. p. 652. etc.</margin> images of their Father in heaven, who does nothing but bless. Every expression is thus appropriate and tailored to the common people.

The much-contested "abolishing and fulfilling of the law" is Chaldaic. To "fulfill the law" means, for Zoroaster, to give it strength, dignity, and completeness in its proclamation <note>original: h)</note>, <margin>h) To give praise <note>original: *Setaesch*</note> to the law, to give it strength and greatness.</margin> and Jesus did exactly that in the following interpretation, where he fulfilled its meaning—that is, made it complete—by always piercing through to the spirit. The Pharisees did not do this; rather, they "dissolved" it, diminished it, remained stuck on external formulas, and explained away the meaning. To "fulfill" in deed means to perform the law in its full extent <note>original: i)</note>. <margin>i) To perform the whole extent of the Law. III, 610. II. 22.</margin> The Pharisees did not do that either; therefore, the righteousness of the disciples was to be more complete, just as the following examples [show]...

<summary>The author analyzes the Sermon on the Mount by connecting Jesus’s beatitudes to Aramaic idioms and Zoroastrian concepts of purity and humility. He argues that "fulfilling the law" meant deepening its spiritual meaning rather than strict legalistic adherence.</summary>
<keywords>Sermon on the Mount, Beatitudes, Zoroastrianism, Aramaic (Chaldaic), Pharisaism, Biblical Exegesis, Bahman, Sapandomad, Fulfilling the Law</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s exploration of how Jesus’s moral teachings utilized the existing cultural and religious vocabulary of his time—specifically drawing parallels with Zoroastrian concepts—to refine the spiritual understanding of his audience.</meta>

...examples show. Jesus knew of no separation between teaching and doing.

“You fool!” k) (Matthew 5:22) <note>The OCR cites verse 23, but the "You fool!" warning appears in Matthew 5:22.</note> was the worst curse word, used to name a blasphemer or the devil; it thus stands above <term>Raka<note>original: "Rakah"; an Aramaic term of contempt meaning "empty-headed" or "worthless."</note></term>, which otherwise might seem identical. — Hell as a dungeon l) was a common concept, upon which Christ—in the "popular" Matthew—builds many beautiful folk parables about debtors and the like. — That retribution m) prevails on Judgment Day was just as common. Every sin that was not forgiven and made right, especially unforgiveness and harshness, did not allow one across the bridge. Zoroaster classifies sins accordingly into what is expiable and what is inexpiable n), and with what punishment they are settled. These are the "sins unto death" and "not unto death" in John, where no one should pray for the former; likewise, Jesus's division of sins into those that “will be forgiven!” and those that “will not be forgiven, neither in this world nor in the next!” Christ drew many noble applications from this way of thinking—He, who hated nothing more than reward-seeking and legalistic merit; yet we neglect these applications entirely.

In general, one must not think that when Jesus uses all these folk expressions, he uses them in the same sense as the common people or their Pharisees; quite the opposite. And in this, too, he is a noble teacher of the people! He teaches the people using their own words, but shapes, guides, and improves their thoughts. In Zoroaster, for example, one finds endless chatter about prayers “that our reward and desire may become public!” Christ builds on the idea that only secret, hidden virtue can become public—what an application! There was always the babbling prayer “that the way, the bridge to heaven, might become wide for us!” o) Jesus shows: “it is very narrow and small.” Where the treasure is, there the heart is p), must have been a proverb: what an application Jesus makes of it! — The “good and evil eye” is, even now, a widespread concept throughout the Orient

<column-break/>

**k)** <note>original: "Indigne, fourbe"</note> Unworthy, rogue: this is always the name for **Belial** <note>A term used in the Hebrew Bible to personify worthlessness or wickedness, later identified as a demon or the devil.</note>.
**l)** <term>Douzakh<note>The Middle Persian/Zoroastrian term for Hell.</note></term> III. 668.
**m)** Resurrection III. 768.
**n)** <term>Tanafour<note>A Zoroastrian term for a specific grade of sin or transgression.</note></term> III. 787. An action that prevents one from crossing the **Chinvat Bridge** <note>original: "Tschinevad"; the sifting bridge in Zoroastrianism that leads to the afterlife.</note>.
**o)** <note>original: "Rends-moi large le chemin au Ciel, donnés-moi le pont Tschinevad large"</note> “Make the way to heaven wide for me, give me the wide Chinvat bridge.”
**p)** III. 438.

...mode of speech—what an application [Jesus made] from the mire of superstition to the thought of how the clear, good eye becomes the source of light for the whole body. The "Lord-Lord" sayers and the exorcists were a plague of the land at that time—and so forth. Oh, if only we could see the excellent speech of Jesus, and the entire folk-evangelist Matthew in general, in the urgent clarity and word-perfect simplicity of his time: would we then mutilate "harmonies" <note>A "Gospel Harmony" is a text that attempts to merge the four separate Gospels into a single, chronological narrative.</note>? And collect concordances to Jesus's speech from all the wise men of the world? Or even preach for eight long years on the eight Beatitudes? We would preach that same Gospel of the spirit, rather than of legalistic merit, to our people as simply, perfectly, and powerfully as Jesus did to his people in his own time. This first and single speech <note>Referring to the Sermon on the Mount.</note> is a treasure and a model of unattainable content.

### 2. On the Lord's Prayer.

Jesus says: you shall not <term>battologize<note>original: "battologiren"; from the Greek *battologeo*, meaning to speak idly, babble, or use "vain repetitions" in prayer (Matthew 6:7).</note></term> like the heathens; the learned interpreters are responsible for the fact that they themselves have "battologized" so dreadfully over this word. Have I explained "babbling" well if I say: "and there was a person, and another, and another—a beggar, a peasant, a poet, a king, who was named <term>Battus<note>A legendary figure in Greek mythology, sometimes associated with a king of Cyrene who had a stutter, or a shepherd who betrayed Hermes and was turned to stone.</note></term> and—he stammered." Are stammering and babbling the same thing? One flies, the other creeps; one cannot say enough, the other cannot speak at all—is that what we call explaining?

And why did it have to be explained [this way]? In all languages, "babbling" mimics the sound of babbling: why could it not have been a provincial word? Or a word retained by the translator from the original Chaldean <note>Historically, "Chaldean" was often used by scholars to refer to Aramaic, the language Jesus actually spoke.</note> language q)? Especially since Matthew is so "popular" <note>Meaning "of the people" or written in a common, accessible style.</note> and Jesus here takes everything from the mouth of the common folk.

And look! It is exactly so. The babbling prayer formulas of the heathens, against which Christ warned, were themselves called *Badjo* <note>The author likely refers to the *Baja* or rhythmic, repetitive chants found in various Eastern liturgical traditions.</note>. All previous scholarship on the matter thus falls into the well.

<margin>q) <note>original Hebrew: "בטל" (betel)</note>, It has been useless: <note>original Hebrew: "בטלין" (betelin)</note> useless words, idle chatter. In the original language, there truly seems to have</margin>

<column-break/>

been a wordplay, as Jesus transforms the actual name of the prayers into "idle chatter."

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of the cultural and linguistic context of Jesus's teachings, specifically focusing on the origins of the "vain repetitions" Jesus condemned and moving into an analysis of the "Lord's Prayer."</meta>

Against which heathens should Jesus have warned, if not those from whom the Pharisees had adopted most of their practices, and who were still held in such high regard for their philosophy? These were neither the hated Greeks nor the Romans, but the <term>Chaldeans<note>The author uses this term to refer to the ancient Persians and followers of the Zoroastrian religion.</note></term>. In the <term>Zend-Avesta<note>The primary collection of religious texts of Zoroastrianism.</note></term>, *Vadj* is the name for all the horribly talkative prayer formulas used for every occurrence in life. *Vadj* is said when someone lights a light, when one relieves oneself, when one sleeps with one’s wife, and so on. And all of these are addressed to angels, spirits, and souls with such a terrifying abundance of words that from mere liturgical chatter—where every formula is repeated starting only with the opening word—they fill two <term>quarto volumes<note>Large books where each sheet of paper is folded twice to form four leaves.</note></term> of prattling. Furthermore, these prayers were prayed softly (*en Vadj*), which is to say, they were babbled with the lips. Indeed, *Vadj* was the name for the entire tribute: the perceived necessity to commend oneself to all the saints and souls at every incident <margin>r) Vadj Vol. III. p. 113-142. c. 796.</margin>. — Could the speech of Jesus strike more sharply? "You shall not <term>battologize<note>original: "battologiren"; meaning to repeat the same thing over and over, or to babble idly in prayer.</note></term> like the heathens: for they believe they will be heard if they use many words. You shall not battologize as a tribute: for your Father knows what you need before you ask Him." Jesus thus uses the precise word, just as he did with the "Lord-Lord" saying, which carried its own mockery. And now he teaches them the "Our Father," which consumes all battologies like a fire.

If only I could succeed in setting its simplicity in such a light—drawn from the language of that time and people—that everyone, when they prayed it, would feel it entirely as a treasure of the heart, without foreign formulas!

**Our Father in the heavens.** "Heaven" was, as shown above <margin>s) Section VII. Book I. Note b. See Zoroaster Vol. III. 744. Ormuzd. Behesch. <note>Ormuzd (Ahura Mazda) is the supreme deity in Zoroastrianism; Behesch (Behisht) is the Persian word for Paradise.</note></margin>, the sum of all-pervading action, glory, goodness, and bliss in a single image: precisely what the conclusion expresses, "thine is the kingdom," etc. Just as God is the Infinite in every point of the invisible world—infinitely active—and is the infinity of all angels, beings, and active and happy creatures within Himself: He in everything, and everything exists in Him! The all-good, blessing Father. Luke, therefore, still captures the primary feeling when he omits the predicate and encompasses everything in the word *Father*. I approach the highest, all-encompassing, glorious Being, only through light

Light and blessing and the source of all to the God known to me, in whose radiance I—though but dust—also journey toward bliss <note>original: "Glückseligkeit"; a term implying both earthly happiness and eternal, spiritual blessedness.</note> — Name Him, with everything that feels, thinks, and exists for bliss: **Our Father!** and Jesus, His firstborn, the highest archetype of the Son—which I too am meant to be—steps before me in prayer.

Hallowed be Thy name! Thy kingdom come! Thy will be done on earth as it is in heaven! — All of these are essentially just a single request. God’s name <margin>t) Section IV. B. II. Note a.</margin>—that is, His work, office, and power—is, according to all the praises found in linguistic usage, found only in His kingdom <margin>u) Section IV. B. I. Thus do the people of heaven <margin>y) The people of Ormuzd, Vol. III, p. 755.</margin>; they praise His name, sustain, act, and bless: and they *are* His kingdom. Thus should the earth also do and become the people of heaven. The Father-of-All’s name is then glorious in all who act through Him; everything brings bliss and is blessed. — This is what the words mean according to the simplest concepts of that time. They are therefore the entire content of the life of Jesus, the blueprint for the bliss of all: one request explains the other. Luke could thus once again omit the third formula, and the prayer remained complete. In Matthew, everything is presented in its well-rounded clarity.

Great, pure, comprehensive request! I feel like an unearthly angel created in the image of God; I feel myself within it, alongside countless other beings created for these very same purposes, with everyone in one kingdom, on one level, toward one great and immeasurable progression in clinging to God, through the stages of bliss by means of purity, love, and the practice of His will! My eternal existence, the eternally advancing existence of my brothers, of all powers and good beings—is the highest praise! <margin>z) The *Izeshne*.</margin> Should one think such a thing could come from the mouth of an earthworm?

### VI. VII.

## On the Transfiguration, the Last Supper, and the Death of Jesus.

As soon as Jesus had nurtured the tender plant of faith in his disciples to the point where he could reveal it to them, he told them of his departure ᵃ). Three of the most prominent among them <note>Peter, James, and John</note> were soon after required to be witnesses to the Transfiguration, to further arm them for what was to come. The closer the time approached, the more Jesus’s soul was filled with images of the end ᵇ), and finally, in those most precious hours of life: behold! there he gathered and consecrated for himself the final circle of love and friendship, the chosen memorial for his own until the end of the world ᶜ).

He instituted the <term>Sacrament</term> <note>The Eucharist or Holy Communion, viewed here as a foundational bond for the community.</note> of his death: as a preliminary memorial to arm them and to consecrate them to his new covenant, made with blood and death. In this act, he left himself behind, as it were, entirely and actively within them; he consecrated them as living instruments and reflections of himself, wishing to be with them until the end of days. He chose the most intimate means of nourishing natural life <note>bread and wine</note> to communicate himself, his body, and his blood, to them. As inwardly as it was outward, they were thereby made into One Body in him and among one another. And behold! after the loudest triumph of comfort, love, promise, and praise, he went toward his suffering.

Then followed the dark hour of prayer in the garden <note>Gethsemane</note>. There he went to meet his enemies with composure, and offered himself up amidst all oppressions from without and within, clinging to God with the quietest majesty. He died; neither as a hero, nor a prophet, nor a martyr: but as the <term>Redeemer</term> <note>Erlöser; one who pays a price to set others free, used here to distinguish Jesus's death from that of a political or social hero.</note> of the human race, in the deepest silence and humility.

### Notes.

### I. On the Transfiguration of Jesus.

Six days after Jesus had clearly announced his suffering to the disciples, and they did not—with all that it meant for them as well—understand it, or did not want to understand it, Jesus took the first three of them <note>Peter, James, and John</note> up a high mountain and, while praying, passed over into Paradise before their eyes. The circumstances of the Transfiguration were all such as their way of thinking could grasp. They imagined Paradise in the radiance of a sun that never sets, upon transfigured, blessed mountains ᵃ). The form into which Jesus transitioned appeared as the angels appeared. Two of the first blessed ones who had entered Paradise with their bodies <note>Moses and Elijah</note> were around him, speaking with him; he was with them in a single element. The disciples, however, weighed down by their earthly burden, felt distant; they spoke out of a dark, unspeakably delightful feeling of joy and bliss; yet they spoke as earthly men. Finally, the bright cloud overshadowed them; the voice spoke; and when Jesus woke them from their stupor: he was alone! —

This story is full of revelation in every detail, though that cannot be our purpose here. What kind of Jesus was this, who, while still in a mortal body, could so calmly and prayerfully pass over into the glory of the transfigured! Even after his death, his body was not permitted to decay; only through being killed did he pass into his spiritual, heavenly state! — How far removed the apostles still felt from this! What still had to take place within them before they could share in that glory! How vain, therefore, are a multitude of demands, wishes, conjectures, and hopes—but all of that is not my purpose here.

A more recent, brilliant, and noble writer ᵇ), who [describes] the virtues of Jesus as virtues from without (perhaps without the power and source from whence they [flow]...)

...they flowed) is truly beautifully drawn, includes in his system the strange idea as if Jesus suffered only under the yoke of a dark, blind fate, without any insight into what he or his suffering was destined for? <note>This likely refers to the "system" of Karl Friedrich Bahrdt or other Enlightenment rationalists who portrayed Jesus as a tragic figure caught in political circumstances or secret societies rather than a divine plan.</note> and as if he died in the faith of a cold and yet faithful despair — — Should a person who has no "system," and who reads the Evangelists—especially **John** from beginning to end, and **Matthew** from the sixteenth chapter onward, etc.—not find the clear, clear, clear opposite? Who went more clearly, more composedly, and with a more definite purpose for the redemption of the world toward his suffering than Jesus? Could a dark, coldly despairing Jesus speak a single word of what Jesus spoke in the final circle of the disciples (John 13–17)? Could he pray the prayer with which Jesus gave himself up (John 17)? Who predicted the smallest, external, arbitrary circumstances (crucifixion, for example) more precisely than Jesus? And what did he speak about with Moses and Elijah on the holy mountain? What he told the disciples so often: would he himself not have known it?

Everything in the final chapters of Matthew is full of departure, haste, and ruin; full of teachings on vigilance, full of dread and the premonition of death. In John, the final holy circle of Jesus’s hours is filled with rays of love, comfort, and a magnanimity that gives itself freely; God’s counsel, to which Jesus’s death belonged, is clearly drawn from a heart of the gentlest sympathy, in a way the disciples could bear. Indeed, what does [it] testify to—

### 2. The Memorial of His Death, the Last Supper,

insofar as it belongs here? It was the hour of his departure, the night when he was betrayed. Premonitions and indications of his death appeared in all his speeches: he drank for the last time with them from the earthly fruit of the vine. Those who imagine a bloody sacrificial meal in this regard abuse both language and imagery. The blood of sacrifices was never drunk in the Old Testament <note>original: "A. T." (Altes Testament)</note>; sacrificial language was not so common even then: it was not intended to be a nocturnal blood covenant, like a conspiracy. But *my blood will flow! my body will be given up!* clearly means: “Out of love, I go to death for you. My death is necessary for the atonement <note>original: "Entsündigung," literally the removal of sin or purification</note> of the world!”

This is also the concept of the early Church, which viewed the Last Supper as a mystery: Paul, for example, did not merely signify <note>original: "significirte." To treat as a mere symbol.</note>, but partook.

### 3. On the Suffering of Jesus in the Garden.

Immediately afterward, as Jesus left the circle of disciples with such praise and dedicated himself to his Father as a sacrifice of love, a dark hour of solitude, anxiety, and mourning begins—one that all the Evangelists describe carefully. They, at least, must not have been ashamed of it.

"So it was an hour of the fear of death, which so many martyrs—who gave themselves up with enthusiasm—did not even feel!" And if that were the case, yet Christ nonetheless suffered so willingly and sublimely, as anyone can read in all the Evangelists, what would be more significant? A drunken state of enthusiasm <note>The author uses "enthusiasm" in its older sense of religious ecstasy or being "possessed" by a divine frenzy.</note>, even if it were a divine drunkenness, or a cold, even weakened, exhausted human sensation that endures through its own strength? Immediately afterward, he gives himself up willingly, thinks of his disciples and not of himself, stands before his judges as their judge—so he goes to his death, so he dies. — The "cold" Christ <note>Meaning a Christ who feels the full, chilling weight of human reality and mortality without the "numbing" effect of religious ecstasy.</note> would, in this respect, be even greater than the "enthusiastic" Christ.

But do we not see from other circumstances that it was something more? What is the purpose of the angel who strengthens him, when the result is only that he prays more vehemently and struggles more deeply? If for only a single moment a glimpse of heaven or a feeling of the divine messenger <margin>e) Zoroaster III. p. 627. <term>Amesha Spenta</term> <note>original: "Amshasp." The immediate, inward feeling of the Father's presence is far from him: and we see him in this state even on the Cross, near his death, where everything regarding pain and outward suffering was already decided. He feels his earthly dissolution is near, and yet he sighs: *My God! why have you forsaken me!* — what becomes clearer than the sentiment of the Psalm from which he prays? He feels himself God-forsaken, robbed of the Kingdom of Heaven in these hours: "melted wax," "an earthen shard." <note>These are references to Psalm 22, traditionally seen as a prophecy of the Crucifixion.</note> The external enemies were merely sacrificers, executors of the will of God; it was this will of God with which he struggled—no, not struggled, but under which he patiently huddled! In the deepest state of temptation, robbed of the support of the Kingdom of Heaven, he lay on the ground like a shard; and behold, the earthen shard stood on the abyss of the immeasurable, bore the counsel of God in the infinite, trembled three times, and did not waver! — A state beyond all expression, which we know only from its bloody effects that burst the very vessel of humanity, and which persisted through all his sufferings, until—

### 4. The Hour of Jesus’s Condemnation.

This has been given up on as a desperate case, as there appears to be an obvious conflict between the Evangelists <note>The "Evangelists" are the authors of the four Gospels: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John.</note>. It is not so. Three clearly stand on one side and explicitly name or point toward the third hour. John clearly names his sixth hour also as an early morning hour, and thus he must calculate from midnight. And who calculated in such a way? <note>The author is addressing a famous chronological difficulty: the Synoptic Gospels say Jesus was crucified at the "third hour" (9:00 AM), while John 19:14 says he was still on trial at the "sixth hour." The author suggests John used the Roman "civil day" (starting at midnight) rather than the Jewish "natural day" (starting at sunrise).</note>

Not for the Romans in their courts, for whom he did not write; but precisely for those for whom he did write, in whose language of the sanctuary he wrote: Chaldea <note>The author uses "Chaldea" to refer generally to the ancient Near Eastern and Persian cultural sphere, particularly Zoroastrianism.</note>. Among them, this holy division of time g) was the revolution of all angels and powers. **Gah Ushahin**, at midnight, when the divine service began; **Gah Havan** (John’s sixth hour), the entire early morning; **Gah Rapithwin** (when the sun was darkened), midday; and **Gah Uzayirin** (when Jesus died), approximately at three in the afternoon. Thus, this calculation of time—along with many other unacknowledged subtleties—accords with the spirit and purpose of John’s language; however, as long as this does not emerge clearly into the light, it will continue to find contradiction. ### 5. Hyssop, with which Jesus was given drink,

in the Gospel of John. This is nothing other than the "reed" upon which the sponge was placed, according to the other Evangelists. Hyssop was famously known as the bundle used for purification; in the Chaldean sanctuary, however, this was a species of reed (**Barsom** <note>A ritual bundle of twigs or reeds used in Zoroastrian ceremonies.</note>). Therefore, the Jews, when they returned <note>Likely referring to the return from the Babylonian Exile.</note>, actually used the word "hyssop" for a species of reed h). John, writing in the language of the Chaldean sanctuary, therefore intends nothing other than to ennoble the expression i).

### I. II. III.
## <term>Descent into Hell</term>, Resurrection, and Ascension of Jesus.
<note>The "Descent into Hell" (Höllenfahrt) refers to the ancient Christian belief that between his death and resurrection, Jesus descended into the realm of the dead to bring salvation to the souls held there.</note>

Just as everything in the history of Jesus’s suffering breathes simplicity and humility, from the moment of his death, everything floats as if in another world—in a realm of spirits from which only rays, voices, and manifestations reach us. So it is with the word of his message in the realm of the dead; so too with his appearances on earth, here and there, now and then. They were all certain and true. The one appearing never chose moments of horror or frozen astonishment; he showed himself gently and mildly, and ensured that their senses remained composed. But where was he when he did not appear? During those forty days before his ascension? He was not with them, he no longer taught them; he spoke to them only in hints and breaths from a higher world, promising them the Spirit in his stead. And yet, he was not yet exalted as he would be at the Ascension.

This state of the risen Christ made an eternal impression on the Apostles. He became for them a tangible model of the spiritual and heavenly state of their souls, of the resurrection of their bodies, and of the future feeling of bliss with Jesus, the Victor. They are always in heaven when they speak of Jesus. To him they sacrificed their lives: they had seen his higher life. — As if our dearest deceased friend appeared to us! — And how much more was Jesus than a friend or a mere apparition!

Finally, the elevation to heaven followed—and here everything becomes immeasurable! infinite! glorious!

<vocab>Descent into Hell, Resurrection, Ascension of Jesus, Apostles, Realm of Spirits</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the mystical period between Jesus's death and his Ascension, describing his post-Resurrection appearances as gentle encounters that provided the Apostles with a tangible model for the afterlife. The author emphasizes that these events transformed the Apostles' perspective, making them feel as though they were already living in a heavenly state.</summary>
<keywords>Resurrection, Ascension, Descent into Hell, Apostles, afterlife, spirituality, divinity of Jesus</keywords>

# Notes.

1. **On the Descent of Jesus into Hell.** — He fell asleep, and the earth shook, and the graves were opened, and many bodies of the saints who slept arose and went out of the graves after his resurrection. <note>original: "Er entschlief, und die Erde erbehte..." citing Matthew 27:52-53.</note> Behold, the first fully certain manner and consequence of his appearance in the realm of spirits. The soul of the deceased was in the assembly of his departed brothers (<term>Sheol</term><note>The Hebrew term for the dwelling place of the dead.</note>, <term>Hades</term><note>The Greek term for the underworld.</note>, Acts 2:24, 28; Matthew 12:40) a) and there too he immediately showed himself as victor, even before he was to show it on earth or in heaven. They were called to life, to resurrection with him—those saints who were worthy of resurrection; he unlocked the place of shadows, of twilight, and of waiting, and led them to the light. If one compares this news from that other world with the preparatory, sensory times of the Old Testament—times that saw only through veils: what progress! What development! In what state had these saints been for so long? Certainly not idle: for in the kingdom of God nothing is idle; and only through Jesus did they now become the first-fruits of his resurrection.

Before him, no one had risen directly to that life. Even these saints, whose graves opened at the very moment of his death as a sign that the conqueror of death had broken them, did not rise until he did. He was to be the forerunner to life. "Christ has taken the power from death and brought life to light!" <note>original: "Christus hat dem Tode die Macht genommen und das Leben ans Licht gebracht!"</note> truly means more here than simply demonstrating the immortality of the soul or preaching against the Jewish fear of death. He arose: he had earned resurrection for everything that lives: the noblest saints of the Old Testament became the first glorified crown of his ascension. (See Hebrews 11:39, 40.)

| a) Thus, his soul was simultaneously in Paradise, in the hands of the Father—that is, in the place of rest, etc. According to the concepts of the Orient, these ideas are not in opposition to one another. However, he entered into heaven at the right hand of God only with his resurrected, glorified humanity. | |

<summary>This page provides a theological commentary on the "Descent into Hell," explaining that Jesus visited the realm of the dead (Sheol/Hades) to lead the Old Testament saints toward resurrection. When Paul speaks of the highest height that Christ traversed, he also speaks of the deepest depth. When he brings together the furthest ends of creation over which Jesus became Lord, he speaks of an ascent into the heights and of a descent into the lowest places of the earth. <note>Refers to Ephesians 4:8–10 and Philippians 2:10–11.</note> Every knee shall bow to him and everyone shall confess that He is the Lord: in heaven, on earth, and under the earth—*Everything*.

Peter extends the message of his triumph even into the prison: he names the spirits who were regarded and called the evidence of the first godlessness b), and if we read him in context (1 Peter 3:17 to Chapter 4:6) and pay attention to his main point regardless of the parentheses, he leaves us in no doubt about what he says in the twilight light in which we can and should see it.

The Descent of Jesus into Hell <note>The "Harrowing of Hell," a traditional Christian doctrine stating that Jesus descended into the realm of the dead after his crucifixion.</note> is therefore truly an article worthy of being in our Creed. Once Jesus had finished his work, his triumph was not idle for a single moment. Before he could become known on earth, the regions of night and shadow felt him with both pain and joy, and thus the procession went upward through all the heavens to the throne of the Godhead.

## 2. Of the Resurrection and the Appearances of Jesus.

Here he was at the center of his kingdom, within his human race. He disdained to show himself to his enemies as a victor: he had already shown himself as a victor to fiercer, deeper enemies, whose blind tools they merely were. <note>The author suggests that Jesus’s true battle was against spiritual forces of darkness; having defeated them, he had no need to prove himself to mere mortal opponents like the Pharisees or Romans.</note> Here, his appearance was only what his arrival, his work, and his calling on earth had been: Love! Bliss! Hope! — How everything around the appearing Risen One breathes of heavenly air and a higher existence! He no longer lives the earthly life with them; he appears and vanishes; he speaks words full of life and their hearts glow; he speaks of himself, the Son of Man, as if in a past—

---

b) Throughout the *Zend-Avesta* <note>The primary collection of religious texts of Zoroastrianism.</note>, the first inhabitants of the world—whom God had to combat through the stars and angels with a Great Flood in order to wash the defiled elements clean again—are considered a brood of demons. The legends of the first ages, which deteriorated from gold to iron and were punished by the gods, are well known in all mythologies.

IV. V.
<meta>catchword: Notes-</meta>

### On Jesus Sitting at the Right Hand of God and the Sending of the Spirit.

Jesus sits at the right hand of God: for he has completed his work and rests. At the right hand of God he sits and reigns and represents our place as Priest-King in the vast kingdom of the Father. At the right hand of God he is, invisible, as if in apparent rest, and waits until everything has developed! Until everything lies at his feet, and his people gather to him.

In his place, he promised the Spirit, who was to represent him here below. He has fulfilled his word: the Spirit appeared in the symbol of flaming tongues—that is, individual flames, as it were a divided baptism of fire <note>original: "zertheilten Feuertaufe"; this refers to the biblical account of Pentecost in Acts 2, where the Holy Spirit descended as tongues of fire upon the disciples.</note> that once poured itself completely over him when he was a man on earth. Then there were great powers and gifts and the presence of Jesus in his members: the Spirit within them brought them to life for heaven, established, maintained, and continues to maintain the kingdom of Jesus. We still believe in an active Spirit of God who gathers us to Jesus.

The design of the Kingdom of God is beyond all human expression! Jesus performed his work in the quietest depths, in a majesty most misunderstood! He has completed it and is just as misunderstood and quiet in the highest heights, where he still works only invisibly and in concealment. But deep in the hearts of his own, the representative of his place—the Spirit—is powerful; it guides, cleanses, and impels them to draw near to him, through whom they, having overcome space and time, are already where their Head is.

---

**Vocabulary:** Jesus, Right Hand of God, Priest-King, Spirit, Baptism of Fire, Kingdom of God

<summary>This page describes the dual state of Christ’s current reign: his "invisible" rest at the right hand of God and his "active" presence on earth through the Holy Spirit. It emphasizes the Spirit as the force that sustains the Kingdom of God and connects believers to their heavenly "Head" across time and space.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Notes" section begun on the previous page, specifically expanding on the theological transition from the Old Testament priesthood to the spiritual "Priest-Kingdom" of Christ as interpreted by the Apostles.</meta>

# Notes.

1. **On Jesus Sitting at the Right Hand of God.** This expression comes from the 110th Psalm, where it is as clear as the sun: the image is not of the Levitical priests, nor of Aaron, enthroned beside the Ark of the Covenant; rather, it is the image of the priesthood of Melchizedek <note>Melchizedek is a mysterious figure from Genesis 14 who was both a king and a priest; in Christian theology, he is seen as a "type" or precursor to Jesus.</note>—that is, of the blessed times when Priest and King were still one, a gentle human majesty on the throne in God’s stead. He sat on the throne in God’s stead to bless, a priest of God, a shepherd of his own. The Word of God was his word; the obedience of his subjects was the duty of children, an offering in festive garments before a divine, fatherly majesty. Their path to victory was a divine service, entirely an offering in holy adornment under the blessing of the father. The victory gave birth to a new host of children, like dew from the dawn—that is the image of this beautiful, misunderstood patriarchal psalm in every stroke. King Melchizedek hovers within it, a priest of God, king of righteousness and peace. This image continues through the prophets. Zechariah is full of the blessed Priest-King, drawing from Chaldean <note>referring to ancient Babylonian/Persian influences during the Jewish exile</note> concepts where everything visible and invisible was viewed as such a kingdom. The old theocracy <note>a system of government where God is recognized as the supreme civil ruler</note>, the idea of their praised David, whose son and image he was to be, shaped the Jewish way of imagining things; and so the ideas were formed under which the angel announced Jesus—though in a sense entirely opposite to their earthly delusions. For a long time, the Apostles still clung to earthly concepts; but when the lowly, deceased, resurrected, and now even heaven-exalted Savior taught them something entirely different, the shell <note>original: "Scherbe", literally a shard or fragment; here it suggests the breaking of old, brittle perceptions</note> broke with force. They received and proclaimed concepts of a higher, spiritual, and blissful kingdom. When Paul arrived, he threw himself entirely into the depths and embraced the ideas of the heavenly kingdom of Jesus with a strength and universality that astounds. His letters to the Colossians, Ephesians, and especially the Hebrews, show Jesus as the spiritual head of the Fullness <note>original: "Fülle", likely a reference to the Greek theological term *Pleroma* used in Colossians to describe the totality of divine powers</note>, as the Priest-King who has passed through all the heavens and united all things to himself, etc. John draws this more quietly, more simply <note>The page ends on "einfältiger" (simpler/more modest), corresponding to the catchword "fältiger".</note>

<summary>This page provides a theological commentary on Psalm 110, interpreting the "Right Hand of God" not as a physical location but as the restoration of the Melchizedekian ideal where kingship and priesthood are united in Christ. It traces the evolution of this idea from Jewish earthly expectations to the spiritual, cosmic Christology found in the writings of Paul and John.</summary>
<keywords>Psalm 110, Melchizedek, Priesthood, Priesterkönig, Theocracy, David, Zechariah, Apostle Paul, Colossians, Ephesians, Hebrews, Pleroma, Christology</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological discussion of Christ’s role as the "King-Priest" according to the order of Melchizedek, contrasting this with the traditional Levitical priesthood and exploring the implications of his "sitting at the right hand of God."</meta>

->* ( ) *<-

...more complex, yet deep and heartfelt. However, all these sublime ways of imagining him are washed away if we separate the roles of Priest, King, and High Priest <margin>a) Against this separation, see Ernesti’s *Theological Works*, p. 411, on the triple office of Christ.</margin> and, contrary to the entire Epistle to the Hebrews, turn him into a Levite or an Aaron. For Paul specifically wants to show that he was never Aaron, but infinitely more than him—something entirely incomparable to him: namely, a Priest according to the order of Melchizedek <note>A biblical figure who was both a king and a priest, serving as a prototype for Jesus’s unique authority</note>, a King-Priest, which he remains even now at the right hand of God in his entire kingdom.

The aforementioned Psalm <note>Psalm 110</note> and its excellent interpretation, the Epistle to the Hebrews, point us toward the three concepts inherent in the often crudely understood phrase "sitting at the right hand of God." Namely: 1) Rest from a completed work; 2) Dominion with God (standing, as it were, next to God; see Sirach 12:12) or, as the Apostles paraphrase it, acting in God’s stead, with the arm, the right hand, and the majesty of the Father (Acts 2:33–35; 1 Cor. 15:24–28; Heb. 7:25; Eph. 1:20–23); and finally 3) a kind of rest, a waiting and an as-yet invisible dominion until his design has been completed <margin>b) See Ernesti’s *Theological Works*, p. 480, on Acts 3:21.</margin>. 

If we can grasp the development of the main parts of this book—from the Eternal Word, to God’s design for the human race through Jesus, and the linking of this race with its higher brothers, the angels, into a single thought: what could surpass the sublime, silent greatness of God's hushed thought? In Jesus, everything is unity and simplicity: creation, redemption, sanctification, and completion! The sphere rests upon its center and does not know what it rests upon. The center is indeed the deepest, most hidden, and most distant point from every gleaming surface. The misunderstood Christ, who created, sustained, and performed everything in the most silent and quiet manner, continues to do so invisibly and unrecognized until he completes his work.

Let us call upon a very sublime idea here: the so-called "intercession" of Jesus at the right hand of God, which has often been conceived of quite unworthily. Jesus is *for* us; he is at the right hand of God in our stead—this is what is meant by the word "to represent" or "to intercede" <note>original Greek: εντυγχανειν υπερ (*entynchanhein hyper*), meaning to intercede on behalf of or act for someone</note>...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Christ’s role at the right hand of God, transitioning from his status as a King-Priest to a deeper exploration of what it means for him to act "in our stead" or "on our behalf" in the heavenly realm.</meta>

*on behalf of someone* <note>original Greek: "υπερ τινος" (hyper tinos)</note>)—the actual meaning is *to be there in someone’s stead* c), in whatever capacity it may be: speaking, acting as guarantor, teaching, pleading, ruling—and what a comprehensive, powerful meaning the New Testament gives to this expression! "Jesus says: he goes there to take our place, i.e., for us, instead of *our* being there in the dwellings of light, and thus he need not first 'prepare places' d)—which he would gladly do if it were necessary, if they could find places there separated from him. But that is not the case; where He is, they also are: he is there in their stead." This is the triumph of the Apostles in all their letters: "Nothing can separate us from him! Not heights nor depths! Not the present nor the future! Nothing visible or invisible! No order of angels stands between Us. Space and time and intermediate forces have vanished. He is in our stead above: we are where He is." This is the confidence they have, for example, when praying in his name. "We pray in his stead, as if he were here below and we were above in the realm of power. There He prays in our stead; we must therefore be heard, for he himself can hear: he is King in the realm of blessing." This is their high, singular calling: "to purify themselves, to become light, as he who dwells in the light of God for them, in their stead; for otherwise they are not part of him: he is not in their stead there above. They shall be spirit, as he is spirit: to live in heaven because he lives in heaven, to suffer because he suffered while he was here; otherwise they cannot rule with him there." How intimate! How encouraging! How comforting! How difficult! Does being "in Christ, with Christ above" not involve more than what our makers of proselytes <note>likely referring to rationalist theologians who dismissed mystical concepts as mere "Jewish idioms"</note> explain away as a mere Jewish phrase? e)

<column-break/>

c) See Hesychius <note>An influential 5th-century Greek lexicographer</note>, who explains it through *to approach* <note>original Greek: "προσερχεσθαι" (proserchesthai)</note>. — *To intercede against someone on behalf of another* <note>original Greek: "εντυγχανειν κατα τινος υπερ ω. α." (entygchanein kata tinos hyper o. a.)</note> means to step forward against someone to plead for a third party. See Wisdom [of Solomon] 8:21, among others. *To approach* is the image of the priest.

d) Many have deliberately inserted a *not* <note>original Greek: "μη" (mē)</note> into John 14:2 because they could otherwise find no sense in it. From the 14th to the 17th chapters of John, Jesus considers himself the Head and the Apostles as members [of his body]. The former must therefore pull the latter along: they did not first need special places. — Throughout Zoroaster <note>Referring to the Avesta, the primary collection of religious texts of Zoroastrianism</note>, the dwellings of light are called *Behesht* <note>Persian: paradise</note>, the dwellings of the Saints, that part of Heaven where Ormuzd <note>Ahura Mazda, the supreme god of Zoroastrianism</note> and the celestial Spirits reside. III. p. 643.

e) I do not here contradict the otherwise common and worthy explanation of *to intercede for us* <note>original Greek: "εντυγχανειν υπερ ημων" (entygchanein hyper hēmōn)</note>: "he is above at the right hand of God, as King, to exercise all the consequences of the office of mediator for our benefit."
