--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how Christ acts "in our stead," specifically moving from the concept of his heavenly representation to how believers are called to mirror his role as "Priest-Kings" on earth. It concludes by introducing the Holy Spirit as the "Paraclete" or representative who continues Christ's work invisibly.</meta>

* ( ) *

Thus, the continuous main idea of the New Testament—"that we must be priest-kings here below, as he is above"—appears in its origin, light, and dignity. It was, especially in the writings of John, the common sacred expression of Chaldea <note>An ancient region in Mesopotamia often associated with celestial wisdom and hierarchy in early biblical interpretation</note> which, as has often been shown, took one image from God and the highest angels—viewed as satraps <note>original: "Satrapen"; refers to provincial governors in the Persian Empire, here used as a metaphor for high-ranking angels serving God</note>, helpers, and sub-kings of God—and merged it with the other image of divine service in Judea. If we divide these so-called offices, let us ensure that the concept does not entirely vanish; for both in Jesus at the right hand of God and in his followers here on earth, the two are one. He is in us, and in our stead upon the throne of God he is the eternal priest-king; we are to be his reflections here below: **Our life be hidden with Christ, etc.** <note>original: "u. f." (und folgende). This refers to Colossians 3:3: "For you died, and your life is now hidden with Christ in God."</note> work as tirelessly, powerfully, purely, swiftly, and blessedly as He does in the realm of light above: we are to be where He is!

Unspeakable dignity in this design of God for lowly humanity: in Jesus, it is elevated above the angels. They [the angels], who were subject to no temptation, become servants of a weak, fallible, sinful race, which is nonetheless meant to be the primary stage for virtue, faith, and moral testing. On higher levels of existence, this testing is not necessary and therefore cannot be rewarded; thus, Jesus did not appear for the sake of the angels. On the human stage—the lowest rung of virtue—he appeared, and he leads those who belong to him beyond angels and heaven. —

2. Concerning the representative of his position, whom Jesus promised. — This is how I translate the word <term>Paraclete</term> <note>original: "Parakletus"</note>, which means neither teacher, nor exhorter, nor advocate, nor comforter; though it can mean all of these if it occurs in another’s stead. Just as Jesus became our <term>Paraclete</term> <note>original: "Parakletus"</note> at the right hand of God, so he sent the Spirit to earth to represent his position and to invisibly develop what he had begun. This is the point of everything Jesus says regarding this Spirit (John 14–16), and this too was a quiet greatness in God’s design. Jesus was to plant his kingdom visibly only in the mustard seed; his invisible Spirit was to nurture the rest from it f).

<meta>catchword: We</meta>

---

<columns>2</columns>

...presented immediately before; only the idea of § 7 says, when we hear the explanations of the Apostles, that we should hear them quite intimately, intuitively, graspingly, and faithfully. Everything is to become one in Jesus; this is the basis of the representation here as well.

f) Ernesti, *Theological Works* <note>original: "opp. theol." (opera theologica). Refers to Johann August Ernesti (1707–1781), a leading German rationalist theologian and philologist.</note> on Acts 3:21, p. 479.

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of divine representation, transitioning from the "priest-king" role of believers to the specific role of the Holy Spirit as the successor and invisible representative of Christ on earth.</meta>

* ( 0 ) *

We have already seen how, from afar, in the images and personifications of the Prophets, this Spirit and its attributes were foretold: when one reads the Hellenistic writings, Sirach <note>Also known as Ecclesiasticus, a book of ethical teachings from the 2nd century BCE</note>, the Book of Wisdom, Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Jewish philosopher who blended Greek philosophy with Jewish scripture</note>, and the like, one always notices the ideas of the Holy Spirit of doctrine, instruction, and the correct sense of truth, and simultaneously the concept of the "All-fulfillment" g), of divine power, of inner reflection, and of the manifest status as children of God. Amidst all these concepts prepared for him from afar, Jesus finally speaks, characterizing this Spirit of God—the Spirit of wisdom, power, humility, the calm soul of truth, and the All-fulfillment—as his Spirit, whom he would send from the Father, in his name, in his stead, to fulfill everything that he would leave vacant. And as surely as Christ speaks here as a person, distinct from the Father and yet one with him—his image, his eternal Word—so surely does he speak of the Spirit as distinct from himself and the Father and yet one with them: their Paraclete <note>original: "Parakletus"; from the Greek *parakletos*, meaning an advocate, helper, or comforter called to one's side</note>, their representative, with whom the Father had endowed Jesus during his life among earthly humanity, and with whom Jesus, once he became heavenly, wished to endow his earthly members. The formula of our baptism is therefore no nonsense, but the very essence of Christianity. Conversely, if Christ had spoken of a mere word or an attribute as if it were a participating being—like himself and the Father—and had even commanded faith and devotion to this word as to himself and the Father, yes, even giving it a name (which, in the language of the Orient, means an office, an activity, a destiny), indeed, a fundamental destiny distinct from himself and the Father: what nonsense and superstition that would be!

It is therefore wrong to divide the offices and actions of the Holy Spirit as one divides the offices of Christ and of Christians: he has only one office, to represent the place of Christ invisibly, to become within the souls of his followers the Spirit of doctrine, of love, of power, of humility, of the calm sense of truth—in short, to become everything that Jesus was here and for which he initially formed his followers. — And if offices, actions, and modes of operation are even ascribed to the Spirit which Jesus did not have here on earth,

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

---

<columns>2</columns>
g) <note>original Greek: νυς, πνευμα ελεγχομενον, ηγεμονικον, παντα δεχομενον</note> Mind, a spirit that convicts, a guiding principle, all-receiving — as Hesychius <note>A 5th-century Greek grammarian and lexicographer</note> for example explains "Paraclete." See Biel, *special thesaurus of philology*, page 71.

<meta>This page continues the argument that the Holy Spirit is not a source of magical, unearned knowledge, but rather a guide that works through human instruction and maturity, mirroring the stages of life and the structures of historical religious initiations.</meta>

...which he neither formed in his apostles nor promised them: then it is a deception and not the Spirit of Christ. He was only the Paraclete <note>original: "Parakletus"; a Greek term for an advocate, helper, or comforter called to one's side</note>, the invisible representative in his place, and here too, in the life of Jesus, we have the firmest, ongoing, visible proof.

I take, for example, "the anointing of John, which remains in us and which teaches us all things, and as it has taught us, so shall we remain with it" <note>A reference to the First Epistle of John 2:27</note>: if we understand the language of the apostle even in the slightest, it is nothing less than an earthly omniscience, a consulting of the spirit in mystical cases, or a wisdom dripping from heaven independent of all human instruction. John distinguishes between children, youths, and fathers, and speaks here also in the several-times noted sacred language, where exactly these three classes existed with these very characteristics h). "Children were those who were still under the responsibility and religious care of their parents:" of them John says they still enjoyed milk and were learning to know the Father. "In the seventh year the child became a youth, received with many customs the girdle of struggle" (Kosti) <term>Kosti: the sacred girdle worn by Zoroastrians as a symbol of their faith and commitment to battle evil</term>, to overcome the Evil One, because all their prayers and religious duties were namely a struggle of good against evil. John says the youths should be strong and have the word of God with them as a weapon and overcome the Evil One. "Fathers finally (Herbed)" <term>Herbed: originally a title for a Zoroastrian priest or religious teacher</term> "they became when they learned the secrets of religion (the original principles <term>αρχην: Greek for "beginnings" or "first principles"</term>) and, with the highest purification and initiation i) <term>Initiation: a formal entry into a higher level of spiritual or social standing</term>, which was a type of anointing, became themselves images of God, heads of households, and kings, who could also always be priests. Then they were instructed in everything; they must not lose the purification, anointing, and dignity, and so forth." Behold there the language of John: the fathers knew him who is from the beginning and had the anointing that taught them everything: namely, not without the instruction of childhood and the practice of the youth. The Spirit had dictated nothing to them, just as little as he changed their natural humanity. He was a gift of higher instruction, of more mature experience, of greater firmness and strength, just as we have the progression in the natural ages of human life.

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that the Holy Spirit is not a source of mystical, supernatural knowledge, but rather the "Mind of Christ" working through the believer's own disposition and prayer. It transitions from discussing the stages of spiritual maturity to the internal experience of prayer and the historical events of Pentecost.</meta>

->* ( ) *<-

The Spirit of God, who tells us in our prayers what we should pray—is otherwise called the *Mind of Christ* <note>original: "Sinn Christi"</note>, which allows us to pray for nothing foolish, and which intimately assures us of what we pray for in the name of Jesus; that is, what he himself would ask with his heavenly sense of truth. The Spirit, who bears witness to our soul (not "witnesses with" it) that we are children of God—what is he, if not the mind and power of Jesus, which intimately assures us of this? The Spirit, who, when we cannot pray, intercedes for us with unspeakable groanings, with a prayer without words; what is he, if not the godly disposition <note>original: "Gottgesinnung"</note> remaining within us, which, even when we cannot pray, keeps us united with God as if we were eternally saying: Abba! <note>An Aramaic word for "Father," used by Jesus to express intimacy with God</note>. In short, let us see how Jesus was, how he wanted the apostles to be, and how they actually were: thus we cannot go wrong. And you, O level, open, simple life of Jesus! Active life of the apostles! How far removed you are from fanaticism <note>original: "Schwärmerei"; often used in this period to describe religious "enthusiasts" who claimed direct divine inspiration</note> and monastic delusion! —

God forbid that I should speak a single word in favor of the self-made, self-righteous, cooled-down, Christ-empty, inertly-quiet, or misanthropically-respectable Pharisaism of the latest modern morality! If Christ appeared now, by whom would he be first hated or mocked, since we no longer crucify? And yet they are Christians! Images of Christ! Apostles of self-denial, sacrifice, Christ-like gentleness, and humility! —

->* * *<-

Let us look at the first appearance of the Spirit of God; how it confirms what I am showing! No thunderstorm struck the house, according to the latest favorite hypothesis, nor will anyone ever bring forth evidence that the sound as of a mighty wind signifies a thunderclap, or that a vision of hovering flames of fire, as if they were tongues, signifies lightning k). The Evangelist defines both clearly enough: the first as an unusual sound gathering the crowd, which filled the house—(but the Lord was not in the storm!) and this: there appeared to them <note>Greek: "ôphthēsan autois"</note>

<columns>2</columns>

k) Nothing is proven with phrases about "licking fires" <note>Latin: "lambentibus ignibus"</note>, for every flame "licks" in this way, that is, it has the shape of a tongue—but specifically lightning does not. "A sound like a breeze" <note>Greek: "Ēchos hōs auras"</note> never means thunder, and considered as symbols of a Theophany <note>a visible manifestation of a deity to humankind</note>, every pagan has let his god appear more worthily. See Spanheim's Callimachus <note>Ezechiel Spanheim (1629–1710), a Swiss diplomat and scholar who provided extensive commentary on the works of the Greek poet Callimachus</note> Vol. II, here and there.

Explanations of the New Testament

He also begins a rationalist critique of the Pentecost narrative, arguing the signs were symbolic rather than a literal thunderstorm.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the Holy Spirit, moving from the internal disposition of prayer to the historical events of Pentecost. The author is contrasting the "undivided" light that descended upon Jesus with the "divided" flames given to the apostles, arguing that these spiritual gifts were not mere magic tricks but outgrowths of a specific moral character.</meta>

->* ( 0 ) *<-

...the sacred expression of the appearances!) cloven tongues as of fire, and the Spirit settled itself, and so forth. Who has not seen there that every flame, when it blazes up, is *tongue-shaped* <note>original: "γλωσσοειδες"</note>, that is, it flames like a tongue? Just as water gathers itself into drops. Now, this was exactly a common expression among the Greeks <margin>l) They called a promontory, or an ornament on clothing in the shape of a tongue, and so forth, *tongue-shaped* <note>original: "γλωσσοειδες"</note>.</margin>, which naturally occurred to the historian—if one reads with spiritual insight—since the tongue was immediately to become the instrument of the Spirit. In short, it is the so-often promised, now fulfilled, divided baptism of fire of Jesus. Upon Jesus, the Son of God, the light of heaven came undivided, without any noise or measure: the heavens broke open, the dove flowed down; it was omnipresent light and no earthly flame. Here, from his Spirit, there appeared *like the wings of a dove* <note>original: "ως πλερυγες ομματος πελειας"; likely a poetic or slightly variant reference to the Spirit's descent</note> only divided flames, various gifts and powers according to the receptivity of each person, from him in whose hand was the fullness of the Spirit for his own people. — Why can I not continue the story?

We are accustomed to marvelling at these gifts, as well as the miracles of Jesus, only as <term>prodigies</term> <note>miracles or supernatural signs viewed as external proofs of power</note>, as such-and-such external aids and proofs of religion, as if they were not truly inward. They welled up from the Spirit of Christ—that is, from love, self-sacrifice, purity, and heavenly humility—without which they could not exist, just as a flame cannot exist without the ether. As soon as that Spirit was gone, the stirring up and forcing of these things was in vain. The Apostles all foretell the falling away, the <term>Man of Sin</term> <note>a biblical figure from 2 Thessalonians, often interpreted as a personification of pride or an anti-Christian power</note> (a personified name which they contrasted with the Spirit, the "pure new man") <margin>m) See Schöttgen, *On the Struggle of Flesh and Spirit* <note>original: "de lucta carnis & spiritus." Christian Schöttgen was an 18th-century scholar known for explaining the New Testament through Jewish tradition.</note>.</margin> who, with many adored talents, brilliant facets, and even miracles and signs, would rise up against God; he would become the idol of the centuries, a tyrant under whom, if it were possible, even the elect would succumb — That is the night when no one can work! The spark of the Spirit glows, wherever it is, under a mountain of rubble and ash, or shines in secret and is despised.

<summary>The author explains the imagery of Pentecost (tongues of fire) as a natural metaphor for the Spirit's distribution among the apostles, contrasting this with the pure light of Christ's baptism. He warns that spiritual gifts are worthless without the "Spirit of Christ" (love and humility) and laments a coming age of spiritual darkness.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the Holy Spirit's historical arrival at Pentecost to the ultimate theological conclusion of the Christian narrative: the resurrection of the dead and the final judgment. The author argues that human life is not a series of random events but a purposeful "moral economy" directed by God toward a spiritual harvest.</meta>

->* ( ) *<-

### VI. VII.
### Resurrection of the Dead, Judgment, and the End of the World.

The Creator, Redeemer, and invisible Ruler of creation shall one day also become its visible Arbiter and Completer. Earthly life was merely the seed for a higher life, for God’s <term>Moral Economy</term> <note>original: "Moralischen Haushaltung"; a term referring to God's purposeful management of the world toward a moral and spiritual end</note>; just as this stage once began wonderfully through Him, it cannot end without Him and without the clearest development of His purposes.

What would the human race be if it fell into the dust in isolation, without a destiny for the whole, without a final purpose for a higher life? Or what more would it be if the soul—as a <term>monad</term> <note>original: "Monade"; in philosophy, a simple, indivisible unit of being or consciousness</note>, as a breath, as an atom—were to force its way through a hundred chasms and forms without consciousness of the progress of its inner powers? You would be a game of fools, O Human Race! — But as the image of God and Jesus, you are ennobled for eternity. We sow a seed into the earth for a harvest of spiritual humanity: as surely as Jesus, the Word of God, created us, and as our Savior and Brother sits at the right hand of God: so surely will he visibly come again to judge the living and the dead!

Great time of decision! That which is done in Jesus—the members of his light and life—rise up to him, spiritual parts of his blessedness and fellowship; earth and darkness and filth sink, like dross, into the fire. The human race here is also like the individual human being. The heavy corpse sinks to decay: only the invisible soul is life.

<vocab>Resurrection of the dead, Judgment, End of the world, Creator, Redeemer, Human race, Eternity, Jesus, Word of God, Savior</vocab>

<summary>The author describes earthly life as a "seed" for an eternal, spiritual existence, concluding that human history must end with a visible judgment by Jesus. He rejects the idea of the soul as a mindless atom, arguing instead for a conscious, ennobled destiny where the spiritual is separated from the earthly "dross."</summary>

### Notes.

-><image-desc>A decorative typographic ornament consisting of a central floral or star-like element flanked by two smaller leaf-like flourishes.</image-desc><-

"Zoroaster <note>The prophet Zarathustra, founder of Zoroastrianism.</note> asked Ormuzd <note>Ahura Mazda, the supreme creator deity in Zoroastrianism.</note>: a) how shall the bodies rise? Wind and earth carry bodies away: how shall they be restored? —

"Through me, answers Ormuzd, the starry heavens were made, which show their face above and shine in the night. Through me is the earth drawn out of nothing, upon which the Lord of the earth walks. I have created seed, that it may renew and multiply itself innumerably in the earth. In trees, roots, and in all the veins of things, I have placed an unburning fire by which they live. It is I who forms each after its kind in the mother's womb. Skin and nails, blood and ear and eye I have given them. I created man, whose eye sees and whose life is breath. Should the <term>Evil One</term> <note>original: "Urge"; likely referring to Ahriman (Angra Mainyu), the destructive spirit</note> come to awaken the dead: he cannot. But I can. Etc.

"Through the Word (that is the will of Ormuzd!) the dead come to life: first their souls, then their bodies. Heavenly earth shall the bones become, the water blood, the trees hair, and fire shall give them life, as in the first creation. In 57 years the resurrection and judgment shall be completed.

"<term>Sosiosch</term> <note>original: "Sosiosch"; referring to the Saoshyant, a savior figure in Zoroastrianism who brings about the final renovation of the world</note> will come and awaken them, so that resurrection need never happen again. Sosiosch judges upon a throne: all the <term>Izeds</term> <note>original: "Izeds"; the Yazatas, or benevolent divine beings</note> of heaven are with him, and around him all the assemblies of the earth. Light will shine around them, so that every soul recognizes its own body. 'That is my father! Mother! Sister! Brother! Wife! All who are mine!' — Everyone in the assembly will see what they have done, both good and evil. The unjust shall appear as a black animal in a white flock. To the righteous man who was his friend, he will say: 'Why did you not teach me to act purely while I was in the world? Now I must depart from Paradise.' — And they shall be separated, the godless and the righteous, father and mother, sister and brother, friend and friend. Each shall be judged according to their works.

"Righteous and sinners must cross the <term>Chinvat Bridge</term> <note>original: "Brücke Tschinevad"; the sifting bridge that separates the world of the living from the world of the dead</note>, the latter with fear, the former with great joy.

"He who

"Whoever has not prayed, has not fulfilled the law, and whoever has given no garment to the righteous poor, shall then be naked. But for those who have done these things, the heavenly gifts shall clothe them.

"The heaven beneath the moon falls to the earth. The earth trembles like a sick woman, just as a sheep quivers before a wolf. The mountains melt with their treasures: the burning stream of molten ore <note>The Zoroastrian concept of *Ayohshust*, a river of molten metal that purifies the world.</note> flows, and all people must pass through it. To the pure, it feels like a mild, warming stream of milk; but the wicked must remain within it until they are purified.

"Zoroaster asked Ormuzd <note>Ahura Mazda</note>: what will then happen with their soul and their body? They will all, speaks Ormuzd, unite in one work, in one fulfillment of the law with Sosiosch <note>The Saoshyant, a messianic savior figure.</note>. They will perform it with zeal for Ormuzd. Ormuzd has finished his works and will create nothing more; the awakened will no longer have need for labor. Sosiosch and all the awakened will offer songs of praise.

"Sosiosch gives them of the sap of life <note>original: "Saft des Lebens"; likely referring to the white Haoma, a sacred drink that grants immortality.</note> and they can die no more. Sosiosch lifts them up to himself: they walk in Paradise under his protection, for as long as beings shall endure.

"Serosch (that is the will of Ormuzd!) <note>Sraosha, the divinity of "Obedience" or "Listening."</note> and Eschem (the poisonous devil of envy) <note>Aeshma, the demon of wrath and fury.</note> will offer praise together.

"Yet Daroudj-Ahrimann (the supreme Satan) <note>Ahriman, the spirit of darkness and destruction.</note> remains and wishes to return to the world of Ormuzd: the wicked one! But he is defeated, his power taken from him: he plunges into the darkness, and the dragon is purified in the burning streams of molten ore. All rot and impurity is purified in the burning streams of ore. It appears again: hell is a paradise: the world, through the Word of Ormuzd, is immortal, pure and great, for as long as beings shall endure." — — —

These are the ideas gathered from the end of the latest book, the *Bundahishn* <note>A 9th-century collection of Zoroastrian cosmogony and mythology.</note>, in which I certainly suspect—especially regarding Sosiosch (perhaps a reference to Jesus)—additions from the Disciples of John <note>The Mandaeans, a Gnostic sect who revere John the Baptist and lived in proximity to Persian communities.</note> who have entwined themselves with this sect since ancient times. In the actual *Zend-Avesta* and in the liturgies, all these ideas do not occur. "The dead become alive through Hom <note>Haoma</note>, the sap of life: it is a mighty resurrection: one prays to be able to dwell in the dwellings of the blessed"—that is all I have noted therein. Also, Sosiosch is mentioned only as—

<meta>This page transitions from the specific Zoroastrian descriptions of the end times to a broader historical and philosophical discussion of how these ideas influenced Jewish thought (Pharisaism) and the teachings of Jesus. It addresses common rationalist critiques of resurrection imagery, arguing that such language was a necessary cultural tool for spiritual teaching.</meta>

* ( ) *

...thought of the third son of Zoroaster and nothing more. Therefore, do not confuse this piece, taken from a late philosophical book, with that which has been drawn from the law books themselves <note>The author distinguishes between the later Zoroastrian commentaries and the original Avestan scriptures.</note>, and think of it what you will.

One thing is certain: by the time of Jesus, the doctrine of the resurrection was generally accepted and formed the cornerstone of Pharisaism, as we see from the Evangelists and the Acts of the Apostles. It also becomes probable from Ezekiel and Daniel <margin>b)</margin> that since the time of Chaldea <note>Referring to the Babylonian Exile, where Jewish thought was heavily influenced by Persian/Chaldean ideas.</note> this doctrine had been illuminated, whereas in the times prior, people seemed content with the realm of the dead <note>Referring to *Sheol*, a shadowy underworld where there was little distinction between the righteous and the wicked.</note>. Jesus can therefore always speak to the people in parables, using images of this kind, as anyone must have already noticed who has read the collected passages of Pharisaism regarding the resurrection of the dead.

Most objections against the resurrection and the final judgment—which focus on external, petty modes of representation that they find ridiculous—do not really amount to much. These were the given words and modes of representation that Jesus found; without them, he could not have made himself understood, and he always employed them very worthily for his purpose. People take offense at the "Day" <margin>c)</margin>: but who does not know what a "Day of Judgment" or a "Day of Appearance" signifies in the language of the Orient? It often refers to a vast period of development! — "Resurrection of the flesh": yet how worthily does Paul explain the future resurrection of bodies! Does he not remove it from every reasonable doubt? — "Assembly before the judgment seat," seen perspectively in the clouds—but the images of the Oriental judgment seat <margin>d)</margin> were already established.

To a rational person, such objections must be "dust beneath the feet" when one considers resurrection and moral development according to the pure concepts of Jesus. Reason itself asks: should the labyrinth of earthly scenes not one day have a visible exit, just as it had a beginning? Is our personality—the first moral stage we feel above the animal—in vain, and does everything swallow itself up again in night and horror and dark ideas? Or must our moral "I," that deepest and most profound feeling of good and evil existence, continue, and—

<meta>catchword: nach (toward/after)</meta>

<columns>2</columns>

b) Ezekiel 37. Daniel 12:2. On Pharisaism, see Schöttgen, Lightfoot, and especially Pocock Chapter VI. *Miscellaneous Notes* <note>original: "Not. miscell."</note>. When Peter speaks of the end of the world, it is in these same images.

c) See Biel’s *Specimen Lexicon* <note>original: "Specim. lexic."</note> on the word *day* <note>original Greek: "ημερα"</note>.
d) Trumpet: Archangels as court officials and so forth.

<summary>This page argues that the concept of resurrection entered Jewish thought during the Babylonian exile and was the standard framework used by Jesus and Paul to explain moral continuity and the afterlife. The author defends the use of "Oriental" imagery—like trumpets and judgment seats—as necessary cultural metaphors for a deeper moral reality.</summary>

<keywords>Resurrection, Pharisaism, Jesus, Paul, Ezekiel, Daniel, Chaldea, Day of Judgment, Moral Development, Eschatology</keywords>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the specific historical origins of the doctrine of resurrection (Zoroastrian and Pharisaic influences) to a more philosophical and spiritual interpretation. The author argues that Christianity’s value lies not in abstract philosophical proofs of the soul’s immortality, but in the moral transformation and the "natural law" of returning to the divine.</meta>

->* ( ) *<-

...continue to develop according to the laws of spiritual nature, just as we see in every physical state? Here reason stands still; it suspects, it hopes, but it sees no further, because it only perceives this stage of existence. It is here that Jesus suddenly provides the most glorious light. It is not at all true that he demonstrated the **immortality of the soul** <margin>c)</margin> or brought its doctrine to light; Christianity knows nothing of the "immortality of the soul" <note>The author distinguishes between the Greek philosophical concept of an inherently immortal soul and the Christian promise of a divinely granted resurrection.</note>, and I do not know if anyone truly thinks much of the term? It teaches the **Resurrection of the Dead**, a **moral transition**, and a clear decision following this transition. One cannot imagine more spiritual laws of continuity, transition, and decision than those developed by this simplest of religions. Spinoza <note>Baruch Spinoza (1632–1677), a philosopher who emphasized a rational, ethical life lived in accordance with the "laws of nature," which he identified with God.</note> was undoubtedly neither a Christian nor a fanatic <note>original: *Schwärmer*—a term often used for religious enthusiasts or visionaries who claimed direct inspiration.</note>. Yet if one sets aside his metaphysics and looks at the purely moral part of his *Ethics* <note>original: *Sittenlehre*</note>, in which religion does one find this doctrine and outlook confirmed by facts, grounded in its entire design, in the simplest and strongest way?

Nothing shall pass over into that world except what is done in Christ, who became the model of quiet, pure, universal love of God in human form. His religion seeks to free us from everything that is "Self"—in our concepts, inclinations, and actions—to seek not just truths, but the Truth; to love not just good things, but the Good; to teach us to forget even what is a mere image, and to become one with him, one with all that is good, just as He is with the Father and with us. This religion overcomes space and time; it casts away imagination, senses, and passions like mist; even in the midst of the earth, it walks in heaven—that is, among eternal things, or rather, in only one eternal thing: God! It recognizes God in Jesus and partakes in Him through the Spirit. The more we are transformed into this heavenly nature, the more our soul drinks the sap of life; it is light, just as He is light. And there, says the Author of our salvation, the soul already has eternal life within itself: **it does not come into judgment, but has passed from death into life.** <note>A paraphrase of John 5:24.</note> We do not wonder when a stone falls or a flame rises: in the same way, we shall feel the law of nature when the eternal goes to the eternal, and darkness and earth sink into the great decay of the world. Therefore, whoever has such hope in Him purifies himself, just as He is pure.

---

<margin>c) As Mosheim <note>Johann Lorenz von Mosheim (1693–1755), a pioneering German Lutheran church historian.</note> intended to show in a treatise, though he himself did not know or use Pococke’s <note>Edward Pococke (1604–1691), an English scholar of Oriental languages whose work on Jewish traditions (Pharisaism) was highly influential.</note> collected passages on Pharisaism regarding this matter.</margin>

<meta>catchword: Content,</meta>

<summary>This page argues that Christianity prioritizes the moral "Resurrection of the Dead" and the pursuit of universal love over abstract philosophical theories about the soul's immortality. It suggests that spiritual continuity is a natural law where those who live in the "Truth" naturally transition toward the eternal divine.</summary>
<keywords>immortality of the soul, resurrection of the dead, moral transition, Spinoza, love of God, nature law, Mosheim, Pococke, Pharisaism</keywords>
